35 Karma and Punarjanma

[[P1530]]

CHAPTER XXXV

The doctrine of Karma and Punarjanma

(transmigration or metempsychosis )

This is one of the most fundamental doctrines of the Indian system of religion and philosophy, is an endeavour to answer the question that occurs to all thoughtful [[persona,|persons,]] viz. what happens to man after the death of the body and has influenced for thousands of years or at least from the times of the Upaniṣads all Indian thought and all Hindus, Jains and Buddhists. This is a vast subject [[and bas|and has]] during the last few decades attracted the minds of [[numerours|numerous]] writers in the West. There is now a voluminous literature in the West on [[reincarnation,|reincarnation.]]1 Several countries in historical times believed in transmigration. Herodotus2 states that some of the Greeks (whose names [[he know|he knew]] but did not disclose ) had used that doctrine as their own, but that the Egyptians were the first to teach that the human soul was immortal and that at the time of the death of the body it entered into some other living thing then coming to birth. Pythagoras appears to have believed in it and there are controversies whether he derived it from India. Prof. A. B. Keith (in J. R. A. S. for 1909 pp. [[569 606)|569–606)]] after a lengthy discussion holds that Pythagoras did not borrow it from India. The present writer does not express any opinion on that subject as it is irrelevant for the purpose of this volume. Hopkins and Macdonnell accept the theory of Indian Influence on Pythagoras, but Oldenberg and Keith do not.

Not only Pythagoras, but Empedocles (who is reported to have said that he was a boy, a girl, a bush, a bird and a fish) and Plato believed in the pre-existence and post-existence of the soul, in the idea that the bodies which will accompany the soul in several births would bear similitude to the lives that [[P1531]] they had led, that the happiest would be those who had practised the social and civil virtues called temperance and justice and that supersensible thinking would be the only means of [[dis engaging|disengaging]] the soul from successive lives of sense. Vide Kenneth Walker’s [[“The circle|‘The circle]] of Life’ p. 93 (where he says that the [[do ctrine|doctrine]] of metempsychosis was probably well-known in India at the time of Christ) and Gough’s ‘Philosophy of the Upaniṣads’ (London 1882), pp. 25-28, where he cites a long passage from Plato’s Phaedon on this subject and pp. 29-31 for a long quotation from [[Humo’s|Hume’s]] Dialogues [[concernig|concerning]] Natural Religion on the miseries and migration of the soul from body to body. Gough on p. 25 of the above work opines that since in the Vedic literature prior to [[the Upanisads there|the Upaniṣads there]] is no reference to the belief in the passage of the soul into plants and human bodies, it is reasonable to suppose that the Hindus borrowed this doctrine from the indigenes in the course of their absorption of indigenous blood. Vide also ‘Studies in honour of [[Bloom field’|Bloomfield’]] pp. 76-88 for G. W. Brown’s views to the same [[effeot.|effect.]] This is a most gratuitous assumption, it is a pure [[con jecture|conjecture]] and without any evidence whatever. If belief in [[trans migration|transmigration]] could exist among Egyptians and many primitive tribes, there is no reason to assume that Indians did not [[them selves|themselves]] arrive at the doctrine, particularly when there exists [[Dowhere|nowhere]] else in the whole world any theory of Karma and transmigration [[80 detailed,|so detailed,]] so influential and so thorough as in Sanskrit Literature. One may dismiss the conjectures of Gough and G. W. Brown as verbiage (the latter conjectures on pp. 87-88 that even the words Yoga, Sāṅkhya [[and Upanisad are|and Upaniṣad are]] coined from similar words in some Dravidian tongue now lost). All Scholars ( particularly Western ones when writing about the East) should lay to heart the words of Mallinātha [[’nāmālam likhyate kingit’.|‘nāmūlaṁ likhyate kiñcit’.]] The present writer is not against conjectures at all but they must not be bold and must always be treated as such. But the danger is that the [[conjecturos|conjectures]] of former scholars or men are often treated as valid conclusions by [[later writers,|later writers.]] All scholars must bear in mind Acton’s warning ‘Guard against the prestige of great names; no trusting without testing’. In the H. of Dh. Vol. IV. pp. 38-40 the doctrine of Karma and Punarjanma was briefly discussed in relation to the [[con sequence|consequence]] of sins and their removal and details were reserved for treatment later. In the present chapter the author proposes to examine the Vedic literature for tracing the origin and the growth of [[P1532]] this doctrine and the vicissitudes and modifications of it and the objections raised against it in modern times. It is a remarkable circumstance that, though the several darśanas (such as Sāṅkhya, Yoga, Nyāya, [[Vaiśesika,|Vaiśeṣika,]] [[Pūrvamlmāmgā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] and Vedānta ) severely criticize some doctrines of each other, the doctrine of Karma and transmigration received [[alınost un animous|almost unanimous]] support except among materialists (like [[Cārvāka),|Cārvāka).]] Prof. Wilson in ‘Religion of the Hindus’ (London, ed. of 1862) vol. II, p. 112 remarks [[‘metem psychosis|‘metempsychosis]] is not only the one point on which all are agreed, it is the one point which none have [[over disputed’.|ever disputed’.]] The [[Buddhista|Buddhists]] and Jains, though they differed on many matters from Vedic and [[Smrti|Smṛti]] literature, adopted this doctrine in their own way. There are some suppositions antecedent to all beliefs in Karma and [[trans migration|transmigration]] viz. (1) man has a soul, eternal and separate from the physical body, (2) that other organisms, animals, plants and probably inanimate objects possess souls, (3) that the souls of men and lower animals can pass from one kind of physical organism to another, (4) that the soul is both doer and [[sufferer,|sufferer.]]

It has already been shown at some length (in Vol. IV. pp. 154-171) how ideas of Heaven and Hell were developed from ancient Vedic times and it has been stated [[(on y. 158|(on p. 158]] of that volume) that the doctrine of Karma and transmigration modified the doctrine of Heaven [[and Hell,|and Hell.]]

The word karma occurs in [[the Rgveda over|the Ṛgveda over]] [[forty times,|forty times.]] In some passages it appears to mean exploits’ or ‘valiant deeds’ as in the following: ‘Observe the exploits of Viṣṇu’ [[(Rg. 1.|(Ṛg. I.]] 22. 19), [[proclain|proclaim]] by your word [[(or Verses)|(or verses)]] the3 ancient deeds of Him (Indra) who is worthy of praise’ [[(Rg. I. 61.|(Ṛg. I. 61.]] 13); “that deed of his (of Indra), who is wonderful, is most worthy of worship, that deed is most beautiful, that he filled with sweet waters of four rivers’ [[(Rg. I. 62.|(Ṛg. I. 62.]] 6); ‘who is firm in each exploit’ [[(Rg. I. 101, 4);|(Ṛg. I. 101. 4);]] [[‘O Indral|‘O Indra!]] four are your divine and [[unconquer able|unconquerable]] names,… under which you performed [[exploita’(Rg.|exploits’ (Ṛg.]] X. 54. 4);[[‘O Aspins!|‘O Aśvins!]] frequent drinkers of delicious Soma and Lords of splendour, you helped (or protected) Indra in his exploits [[P1533]] against [[Namuoi,|Namuci,]] son of Asura (or of the Asura breed)’ [[in Rg,|in Ṛg.]] X. 131. 4. In several other passages [[of the Rgveda “karma|of the Ṛgveda ‘karma]] means ‘religious works’ (such as sacrifices or gifts); (the gods ) accept (or like ) all works of this poet who brings to you a hymn of [[praise ‘|praise’]]4 [[(Rg. I 148.|(Ṛg. I. 148.]] 2); (O Indra), give your ear to (the prayer) of [[Syāvāśva|Śyāvāśva]] who offers Soma to you, as you listened to (the prayers of) Atri who performed religious works’ [[(Rg. VIII.|(Ṛg. VIII.]] 36. 7); [[O pure Soma!|‘O pure Soma!]] with thy help our ancient and wise forefathers performed works’ [[(Rg. IX.|(Ṛg. IX.]] 96. 11). In very ancient times Heaven was believed to be the place for enjoying the rewards of most works. Rewards of this world such as wealth, heroic sons were no doubt prayed for, but it was immortality [[(amptatva)|(amṛtatva)]] and the joys of heaven, that were highly valued. [[In Rg. X.|In Ṛg. X.]] 16. 4 Agni is prayed to take the deceased to the world of those who performed good works (tābhir-vahainam [[sukstām u|sukṛtām u]] lokam). The [[words sukstām lokam’|words ‘sukṛtāṁ lokam’]] occur in the [[Atharva veda|Atharvaveda]]5 [[(III, 28.|(III. 28.]] 6, XVIII. 3.71) and in the Vāj. S. 18. 52. [[In Rg. IX,|In Ṛg. IX.]] 113. 7–10 the sacrificer who offers Soma to Indra prays that he may be placed in that world (heaven) as an immortal, where there is never-ending light, where Yama, the son of Vivasvat, is the King, where there are joys and delights and where there are desires and their fulfilment. The prayers for immortality are made [[in the Rgveda to|in the Ṛgveda to]] all gods, e. g. to Agni [[in I 31.7, IV 58.|in I. 31. 7, IV. 58.]] 1, V. 4. 10., [[Vi. 7.|VI. 7.]] 4, to the Maruts in V. 55. 4, to Mitra and Varuṇa [[in V, 63.|in V. 63.]] 2, to [[Visve-Devas in X 52.5|Viśve-Devas in X. 52. 5]] and X. 62.1, to Soma in I. 91.1, IX. 94. 4, [[IX. 108, 3.|IX. 108. 3.]] But about the fate of evil-doers not much is said [[in the Rgveda. In|in the Ṛgveda. In]] the [[Brāhmana|Brāhmaṇa]] works greater details are offered as to rewards of good works and the retribution for evil deeds. Śat. Br. (XII. 9. 1. 1) puts forth the idea of retribution [[(oited above).|(cited above).]]6 The same idea is set forth as to flesh-eating in Manu and Viṣṇu-dharmasūtra, which state [[P1534]] [[Mthat|“that]] being [[wilde flere|whose flesh]] I eat here would eat me in the next world, [[tbe wise|the wise]] [[declare tils|declare this]] to be the origin of the word [[’nāṁra’.*|‘māṁsa’.”]] [[The Smt. Br.|The Śat. Br.]] in another [[pandaye|passage]] refers to a [[strange legend|strange legend.]]7 [[Burgu,|Bhṛgu,]] who had [[boorne|become]] vain on account of his learning and thought [[hitarelf sagre|himself more]] learned [[than big father|than his father]] [[Varuna,|Varuṇa,]] was asked by his father to go to the four quarters from east to north and report what he would see there. Horrible sights met him in all directions e. g. in the east he [[say non|saw men]] dismembering men, [[huwing|hewing]] off their limbs one by one and saying ’this to you, this [[to mo’.|to me’.]] [[11s said this|He said ’this]] is horrible’. They replied ’these indeed dealt with us in yonder world and so we now deal with them [[in return. Then|in return’. Then]] in the north [[be saw|he saw]] that men crying aloud were [[hoing outon lig|being eaten by]] men crying aloud. When he said ‘horrible &c’; they [[replied those indeed|replied ’these indeed]] dealt with us… in return’. This is a long story and it is not necessary to set out the whole. This story probably gives expression to the popular notion of ’tit for tat’. But one thing is clear that [[the sat. Br.|the Śat. Br.]] indicates by this story that a belief had then arisen that one who does evil [[in ono life|in one life]] has to suffer for it in a later life from that being whom he treated badly. [[The Śat. Br and|The Śat. Br. and]] the Tai. Br. both several times speak of conquering or [[casting off’ punarmītyu’ (ronowed|casting off ‘punarmṛtyu’ (renewed]] death, liability to be born and die again and again). Two passages [[from the Sat, Bṭ. and|from the Śat. Br. and]] Tai. Br. may be quoted. [[In X. 4, 4|In X. 4. 4]] [[the Sat. Br. tells|the Śat. Br. tells]] the story that gods became immortal by means of the proper performance of Agnicayana as [[Prajapati|Prajāpati]] advised them [[viz. hy|viz. by]] laying down 360 enclosing stones, 360 [[Yajusmati|Yajuṣmatī]] bricks and 36 more [[thoroon,|thereon,]] 10800 [[Lokam prpā brioke.|Lokampṛṇā bricks.]] In X. 4. 4. 9 it is said ‘he who is to become immortal through knowledge (vidyā)8 and through sacred works (karman) [[mball become|shall become]] immortal after separating [[from the body,|from the body’,]] and then X. 4. 4. 10 [[assorts that those|asserts that ’those]] who know this or those who do this holy work come to life again after dying and [[coming to life thoy|coming to life they]] secure immortal life; but those who do not know [[P1535]] this or do not perform this sacred work [[oome|come]] to life again when they die and they become the food of him (Death) time after time.’ In the Tai. Br. [[III, 11, 8|III. 11. 8]] the story of Naciketas is narrated just as in the [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] (some of the verses are the same in both). Death grants [[Naoiketas three|Naciketas three]] boons in the [[Tai. Bṛ.,|Tai. Br.,]] the third being different in the [[Kathopan iṣad.|Kaṭhopaniṣad.]] The third boon asked by [[Naoiketas in|Naciketas in]] Tai. Br. is ‘Declare to me how to cast off [[punarmstyu.’|punarmṛtyu.’]] Death declared to him the [[Nāoiketa|Nāciketa]] fire, thereby he [[(Nacikotas)|(Naciketas)]] cast off or kept off [[punarmṛtyu,|punarmṛtyu.]]9 The words ‘apa [[punasmṛtyum|punarmṛtyum]] jayati’ occur in [[Kauṣitaki|Kauṣītaki]] Br. 25. 1 and several times in the Bṛ. Up. I. 2. 7., I. 5. 2, [[III. 2., 10,|III. 2. 10,]] III. 3.2.

From the ancient popular idea of retribution for evil deeds probably arose the idea of the setting of good deeds against the [[evil deads|evil deeds]] of a person and weighing them as if in a balance. [[The Sat. Br.|The Śat. Br.]] states10 [[“Now this|‘Now this]] one is the balance, viz. the right side of the vedi.[[ 24%|]] Let him sit down touching the right edge of the vedi, for indeed they place him on the balance in yonder world and whichever of two will rise he will follow, whether it be good or evil. And whosoever knows this mounts the balance even in this world and escapes being placed on the balance in yonder world, for it is good deed that rises and not his evil [[deod|deed]]’,

The [[Satapatba|Śatapatha]] had arrived at the idea that man’s will (and corresponding deed) governs what world he will reach after death. It says ‘Let him meditate on truth as brahma. Now man here is mostly will and, [[according 8.8 his|according as his]] will is when he departs from this world, does he on departing become of similar will in the yonder world’.11

There is a curious passage in the [[Satapaths|Śatapatha]] (X. 1.5. 4) about the powers conferred by sacrifices in the yonder world. It states that he who had regularly performed Agnihotra eats food [[P1536]] in the [[eveniog|evening]] and morning in the [[yondar|yonder]] world, the performer of [[Darsa|Darśa]] and [[Purnamāsa|Pūrṇamāsa]] [[sacrifica|sacrifice]] eats food every [[balf|half]] month; the performer of [[Caturmabyas (Abasobal sacrificos) eate|Cāturmāsyas (seasonal sacrifices) eats]] food in the yonder worlds every four months. the performer of animal sacrifice every six months; the Soma sacrificer once a year; the builder of [[tbe|the]] [[fire after|fire altar]] [[(agoicit)|(agnicit)]] eats food every hundred years at his will or may not require it at all after once taking it, for a hundred years are as much as immortality, unending and everlasting.

The [[Sat. Br. bad|Śat. Br. had]] arrived at the conclusion that every man is born in a world fashioned by himself. It also asserted that he who sacrifices to the gods does not win such a place as the one who sacrifices [[to the Atman|to the Ātman]] and the latter [[froeg|frees]] himself from the mortal body, from sin, as a [[anako|snake]] frees itself from [[its slough|its slough.]]12

It must be conceded that a clear statement about the doctrine of karma and punarjanma is absent from the [[whole of the Rgveda.|whole of the Ṛgveda.]] [[Rg. VII.|Ṛg. VII.]] 33 is an important hymn. The first nine verses are spoken by [[Vasiṣtha about|Vasiṣṭha about]] [[his song.|his sons.]] Verses 10-14 [[refer to Vasiṣtha|refer to Vasiṣṭha]]13 himself and are either attributed to his sons or according to another view are part of a dialogue with Indra. The verses are [[inythological,|mythological,]] mystical and rather difficult to explain. Verse 10 refers to one birth [[of Vasiṣtha when|of Vasiṣṭha when]] the gods Mitra [[and Varuna|and Varuṇa]] saw him reaching the refulgence of lightning and it is said that Agastya brought [[him (Vasiṣtha)|him (Vasiṣṭha)]] to the people. Here the [[words skam janma’|words ’ekam janma’]] indicate that another janma [[of Vasistha is|of Vasiṣṭha is]] intended in this hymn. [[Verge 11|Verse 11]] refers [[to Vasiṣtha as|to Vasiṣṭha as]] born of Mitra [[and Varupa|and Varuṇa]] [[from Urvasi|from Urvaśī]] and states that all the gods placed him [[in puskara|in puṣkara]] (either antarikṣa or lotus); verse 12 is [[iraportant|important]] for the metaphorical and mystical statement that [[P1537]] wishing to weave the cloth stretched [[by. Yama,|by Yama,]] [[Vasistha was|Vasiṣṭha was]] born from [[Urvasi.|Urvaśī.]] Verse 13 refers to the seed cast [[in. pitohor|in a pitcher]] by the two (Mitra and Varuṇa) from the midst of which arose ( Agastya) and from which [[also Vasiṣtha|also Vasiṣṭha]] was born. Verse 14 is addressed to [[Pratxds|Pratṛds]] (the [[Tritsus|Tṛtsus]] according to [[Sayana)|Sāyaṇa)]] and calls upon them to wait upon (or [[honour) Vasiṣtha|honour) Vasiṣṭha]] who would be coming to them (for [[performing sacrifice.|performing sacrifice).]] This, it seems, is the second [[birth of Vasistha,|birth of Vasiṣṭha.]]

Prof. R. D. Ranade14 in his ‘Constructive survey of the Upanishadic philosophy’ (pp. 145-166) relies upon [[oertain|certain]] [[mantras of the Rgveda|mantras of the Ṛgveda]] for stating that there is evidence for holding that an approach to the idea of Transmigration was being made by the [[vedio|vedic]] sages (p. 147), though he concedes (on the same page ) that in the major [[part of the Rgveda|part of the Ṛgveda]] the idea of transmigration is conspicuous by its absence.

Among the texts on which he relies are [[Rs.|Ṛg.]] I. [[164.3,4,16, 17,20,30,31,38.|164. 3, 4, 16, 17, 20, 30, 31, 38.]] The whole hymn has several riddles, has highly imaginative descriptions of the year, the path of the Sun, questions and answers and is a late hymn, as he [[him self|himself]] admits (p. 150). Verses 3 and 17 contain nothing suggestive of punarjanma. [[Verge 4|Verse 4]] is really concerned with the first creation, as it literally means ‘who saw that one that was first being born, when the boneless [[(prakrti)|(prakṛti)]] bears a body possessing [[bones3B|bones;]] where was the life [[(prāna)|(prāṇa)]] of the earth, or its blood or its [[P1538]] self? Who ( a pupil) approached a wise one (a guru) to ask this ?’. Verse 16 means [[“they declare|‘they declare]] to me the women that exist and [[the mon;|the men;]] one possessing eyes (the light of true knowledge ) may see (the Reality); the blind (the ignorant) will not know; the wise son may know this; he who knows these would be the father’s father’. Sāyaṇa gives [[goveral mea. nings|several meanings]] of this verse. The present author fails to understand how the words ‘father’s father’ indicate any reference to [[transmig. ration|transmigration]] in this verse, as Prof. Ranade asserts. Why he presses this verse of I. 164 into service the present author cannot understand. After referring to I. 164. 32 ( which doubts [[whether be who|whether he who]] created all this knows its real nature ) he states [[it gets such|‘it sets such]] a high price on the mystical knowledge which it [[glori. fies|glorifies]] that any one who comes in possession of this knowledge may be said to be his father’s father.’ To whom does [[‘his refor?|‘his’ refer?]]

The verse [[20’dvā suparnā’|20 ‘dvā suparṇā’]] also occurs in the [[Mundako paniṣad|Muṇḍakopaniṣad]] III. 1. and [[SV.|ŚV.]] Up. [[IV, 6|IV. 6]] and refers to the individual soul and the Supreme Self and tells us that the former is [[bhoktr|bhoktṛ]] (taster of worldly pleasures), while the latter simply looks on. But so far as the present writer can see there is absolutely nothing about a former or future life in the verse. Verse 30 [[draws & sharp|draws a sharp]] distinction between the self which is immortal and the body which is mortal and states that the two are bound together to the same place. Verse 31 is a description of the movements of the Sun who is called ‘gopā’ (protector, guardian) and speaks of his coming to the worlds again and again. Prof. Ranade sees too much in the words ‘gopām’, which is applied to [[Agni (Rg.|Agni (Ṛg.]] I. [[1. 8, 1. 96.|1. 8, I. 96.]] 7), to [[Soma (in Rg.|Soma (in Ṛg.]] VI. 52.3), [[to Varuna|to Varuṇa]] [[(in Rg. VIII, 42.2),|(in Ṛg. VIII. 42. 2),]] to [[Viṣṇu in Rg.|Viṣṇu in Ṛg.]] I. 22. 18. [[In Rg. III.|In Ṛg. III.]] 43.5 the words ‘gopām janasya’ (protector of the people ) [[ocour,|occur.]] Prof. Ranade translates (p. [[151 ) he saw|151) ‘he saw]] the guardian returning frequently to mundane regions’. Why should we not translate (particularly when we have the words [[‘bhuvanesvantaḥ ‘)|‘bhuvaneṣvantaḥ’)]] ‘I saw the guardian Sun again and again returning to the worlds and moving towards me and away from me by different [[paths’’.|paths’?.]] Prof. Ranade also15 relies [[on Rg. X.|on Ṛg. X.]] 16. 3 and [[partioularly|particularly]] on the [[P1539]] [[word’ dharmaṇā’|word ‘dharmaṇā’]] in it which he renders [[as his qualities’. Rg.|as ‘his qualities’. Ṛg.]] [[X, 16|X. 16]] has 14 verses, is one of the funeral hymns and the [[Gre mation|cremation]] fire ( as Agni or Jātavedas) is expressly mentioned in eleven verses. Verse [[3(|3 (]]addressed to the deceased) may literally be translated as ‘may thy eye go to the Sun, ātman (prāṇa) to wind, go to heaven or the earth by dharman or go to the waters if you find your benefit in them; be firmly established in the plants with the limbs of the body.’ The express mention of ‘heaven’ shows that this is only one of the numerous verses [[of the Rgveda|of the Ṛgveda]] in which heaven is referred to as the abode of those who perform good works and that there is nothing more in it than that. Vide above pp. 20–21 for the meaning of dharman (which is the only form of the word [[employed in the Rgveda)|employed in the Ṛgveda)]] viz. ‘religious rites or sacrifices’ (in [[most Rgveda|most Ṛgveda]] passages) or rarely ‘fixed principles of conduct’. The very next [[yerse (X16.|verse (X. 16.]] 4) contains a prayer to Agni to carry the deceased to the world of people of good deeds [[(tabhirvahainam|(tābhirvahainam]] sukṛtām-u lokam). There is no reason to suppose that [[in Rg. X. 16. 3|in Ṛg. X. 16. 3]] anything more is meant than what is prayed for [[in verge 4|in verse 4]]. Besides, the basic idea of punarjanma is that future existence is regulated by the good or evil deeds of a person and he has no option whatever. [[In Rg. X. 16. 3|In Ṛg. X. 16. 3]] the departed spirit is told that he may go to heaven or [[earthi|earth]] or waters or plants. This is not the [[doct rine|doctrine]] of punarjanma at all. In X. 16. 5 the cremation fire is addressed ‘O [[Agnil|Agni!]] send forth the departing man who is [[offered 88 an|offered as an]] oblation into thee and who will move on with the food offered, again to (the [[world of) pitrs;|world of) pitṛs;]] the remainder (left after the body is burnt) putting on a new life may go near [[(pit?s)|(pitṛs)]] and be connected with [[(a now)|(a new)]] body in [[that world. This|that world.’ This]] verse [[con tains|contains]] the same idea as [[in X 16.|in X. 16.]] 4 in different words and refers [[P1540]] only to the other world [[(pitȚs)|(pitṛs)]] and not to [[transmigration,|transmigration.]] Prof. Ranade refers (p. 148) [[to Rg. X.|to Ṛg. X.]] 58 and translates [[one of the perses|one of the verses]] as meaning that the sage will recall the soul and make it live again by his song. It is not clear what verse [[or Verses he|or verses he]] has in view. He has probably only the first verse in view. That hymn has [[twelve yerses|twelve verses]] in each of which a quarter is different but three quarters are the same. None of the verses contains any express word for song. Besides, mind is different from the soul [[even in the Rgveda|even in the Ṛgveda]] [[(vide Rg. X.|(vide Ṛg. X.]] 57. 5 where both words ‘manaḥ’ and [[‘jivam’|‘jīvam’]] occur). Moreover, the man is not yet dead. The composer uses the present tense (ā vartayāmasi) and means to say that he will try to prevent his dying. In the present author’s opinion there is not even a distant reference to [[trans migration|transmigration]] in [[this hynn|this hymn]] or in any of the four hymns [[(Rg. X.|(Ṛg. X.]] 57-60) which are assigned to brothers Bandhu, [[Srutabandhu|Śrutabandhu]] and Vipra-bandhu, who are called Gaupāyanas in the [[Anukra mani.|Anukramaṇī.]] [[In Rg. X.|In Ṛg. X.]] 60. 7 Subandhu who appears to have [[been & brother|been a brother]] of the three is addressed ‘O Subandhu ! here comes your mother, your father, your very life’ etc., and in [[X.60.10|X. 60. 10]] it is [[said * I have|said ‘I have]] brought the mind of Subandhu from Yama Vaivasvata in order that he may live and not die.’

[[In Rg.|In Ṛg.]] X. 14. 8 there is no reference (as some suppose) to return to this world after enjoying the fruits of good deeds in the company [[of pitȚg.|of pitṛs.]] The first half of the [[verge|verse]] expresses that the newly departed spirit will be united [[with pitrs,|with pitṛs,]] with Yama and with his good deeds. The 2nd half asks the departed to leave behind his sons and be united in heaven with a new and brilliant body.

[[Rg. IV.|Ṛg. IV.]] 42 (in ten verses) is a hymn of a sage-like king [[Trasadaggu,|Trasadasyu,]] son of Purukutsa, who identifies himself with Indra and Varuṇa in verse 316 ‘I [[who bave|who have]] become enlightened about the worlds [[am Indra and Varupa and|am Indra and Varuṇa and]] by my greatness am the two worlds (heaven and earth) that are wide, deep (spacious) and well-fixed. I sent forth heaven and earth like [[Tvaṣt?|Tvaṣṭṛ]] (Prajāpati) and support them.’ Here by virtue of true knowledge he identifies himself [[with Indra and Varuna but|with Indra and Varuṇa but]] there is no conception of transmigration. Similarly, [[Vāmadova|Vāmadeva]] [[P1541]] having attained knowledge of Reality asserts17 ‘I have become Manu and the Sun, [[I. am|I am]] the wise sage [[Kaksivat;|Kakṣīvat;]] I reached Kutsa, the son of [[Arjuni,|Arjunī,]] I am the seer Uśanas; look upon me (as identifiable with all)’. This verse is quoted in Bṛ. Up. I. 4. 10 (which would be set out later on and [[explai ned).|explained).]] In this also there is no [[expregs|express]] reference to a past birth as Manu or as the Sun. [[In Rg.|In Ṛg.]] IV. 27. 1 Vāmadeva declares [[while still staying|‘while still staying]] in the womb (of my mother) I learnt all the births of these [[gode;|gods;]] if a hundred fortresses of ayas (copper or iron) had guarded me, I would have still escaped like a hawk with swiftness’ (another meaning is also possible which may be set out later). This is quoted in the Ait. Up. II. 5 (to be quoted and explained later). [[Prof. R. D. Ranade understands (pp. 49, 153) Rg.|Prof. R. D. Ranade understands (pp. 49, 153) Ṛg.]] IV. 26. 1 as saying that Vāmadeva makes an [[uncon scious|unconscious]] utterance that he was in a former life Manu or the Sun. In that verse Vāmadeva [[indentifies|identifies]] himself with five persons, Manu, Sūrya, [[sage Kaksivat,|sage Kakṣīvat,]] Kutsa and Uśanas. There is no word [[like ‘janma’ On|like ‘janma’. On]] Prof. Ranade’s view Vāmadeva would have to be [[beld|held]] to state five previous births at least (and if so in a confused order). Besides, the following verses clearly show that Vāmadeva makes a claim to be identical with the Supreme Principle by giving the earth to the Āryas, sending rain to those who give offerings, making the gods follow his desire or will. The utmost that can be said is that he claims identity with the Godhead. Deussen in ‘Philosophy of the Upanishads’ (p. 318) holds that both the [[verses (Rg.|verses (Ṛg.]] IV. 26. 1 and IV. 27. 1.) have nothing to do with the doctrine of the [[soul’s transmigration,|soul’s transmigration.]] The present author respectfully agrees with Deussen and thinks that [[Prof, Ranade|Prof. Ranade]] is wrong. [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]]18 [[on V.S. I. 1.30|on V. S. I. 1. 30]] explains that Indra looking upon himself as brahma according to the Vedāntaśāstra gives the instruction ‘know me alone’, [[quotes’ Br.|quotes Bṛ.]] Up. I. 4. 10, and on V. S. III. 4. 51 reiterates [[that in Rg.|that in Ṛg.]] IV. 27. 1 the Veda expressly asserts that [[Vamadova,|Vāmadeva,]] while still in the mother’s womb, realized his identity with brahma and then remarks that [[this vedio|this vedic]] [[P1542]] assertion indicates that correct knowledge of Reality may arise in a succeeding life owing to the means (or efforts ) made [[in & previous|in a previous]] life or lives. It may be noted that [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] draws a distinction between what [[Sruti|Śruti]] (Veda) expressly declares [[(vadanti)|(vadantī)]] and what it indicates or what is to be inferred from it ( darśayati).19

Mr. J. S. Karandikar of [[Poona (&|Poona (a]] staunch disciple and follower of Lokamānya Tilak) in his learned work [[‘Gitatattva māñjarl’|‘Gītātattva-mañjarī’]] (in Marathi, Poona 1947) asserts (on pp. 775-776 ) that the doctrine of transmigration is as old as the Vedic age (i.e. the [[Samhitās)|Saṁhitās)]] and relies for this proposition on only four verses [[of the Rgveda viz.|of the Ṛgveda viz.]] X. 14. 8, X. 16. 3 and 5 and X. 135. 6. The first three of these four [[hare been|have been]] explained above in dealing with Prof. R. D. Ranade’s views. Mr. Karandikar [[summarises Rg.|summarises Ṛg.]] X. 16.5 as a prayer to Agni that the latter should take the departed to the [[world of pitss|world of pitṛs]] and should send him [[baok|back]] to his relatives after endowing him with a new brilliant body. There are no words for ‘back to his relatives ’ nor [[for ‘brilliant in|for ‘brilliant’ in]] the verse at all. The word ‘punaḥ’ occurs in the first half and refers to the deceased’s coming to [[pitflola|pitṛloka]] after the body is burnt on the earth and the second half refers to the new body to be had in the [[world of pitra|world of pitṛs]] (and not [[to relatives|to relatives).]] [[Rg. X. 14.|Ṛg. X. 14.]] 8. on which Mr. Karandikar relies has nothing to do with returning to the earth. After leaving his bad deeds (avadya) behind the deceased is asked to go to the [[home of the pitra|home of the pitṛs]] with a brilliant body. The brilliant body is not earthly but of [[the pitr world.|the pitṛ world.]] [[Rg. X. 135.|Ṛg. X. 135.]] 6. is quoted in the note below.20 That verse is an extremely vague one and is a riddle or is [[metapho rical.|metaphorical.]] That hymn of seven verses is addressed to Yama and the [[Anukramaṇi|Anukramaṇī]] says that [[the rși|the ṛṣi]] of it is Kumāra of [[Yama’s family,|Yama’s family.]]

[[P1543]] The first [[verse of Rg.|verse of Ṛg.]] X. 135 refers to Yama who is said to drink (Soma) along with the gods under a tree (i. e. in a garden) with fine foliage, where our (my) father, lord of people, desires to send (me) to the ancient fathers. It is possible that this refers to Naciketas as Sāyaṇa, following the [[story of Naoiketas|story of Naciketas]] in the Tai. Br. [[IIL 11.|III. 11.]] 8 and in the [[Kathopaniṣad,|Kaṭhopaniṣad,]] holds. This verse may be taken as containing the words of Naciketas, sent by his angry father [[Vajaśrayaga|Vājaśravasa]] to the world of Yama [[28 donation|as donation]] in the Viśvajit sacrifice. The 2nd verse also contains what Naciketas said viz. that he (at first) looked upon his father in anger, since he (the father) desired to send him (the son) to the [[ancient pitss|ancient pitṛs]] in that evil way, but that he (on further consideration) liked ( what the father did ). He liked the idea of facing Yama himself. Verses 3 and 4 are addressed by Yama to the boy Naciketas who approached Yama with prayer (or metaphorically in a chariot). [[Verae 3|Verse 3]] says ‘O boy! the new wheel-less chariot [[(viz. Țk|(viz. ṛk]] prayer) with only one beam that you created by your intellect soars up in all directions and that you mount it without seeing (without considering the result of your action).’ [[In Rg. a|In Ṛg. a]] prayer to (and a laud of) gods is often compared to a chariot (as in V. 73. 10, VIII. 3, 15). Therefore, verse 3 suggests two meanings viz. [[Naoiketas went|Naciketas went]] to Yama in a chariot or that he sent up a prayer to Yama. Verse 4 is spoken by Yama [[‘O boył|‘O boy!]] that chariot [[(i. o.|(i. e.]] prayer which you sent up from the learned priests on the earth) was followed by Sāman (a melody) from this place (the earth) as if it were placed in a boat. Almost all the verses of the Sāmaveda except about 75 are taken [[from the Rgveda|from the Ṛgveda]] and therefore the [[Obān, Up. sayg|Chān. Up. says]]21 therefore Sāman is sung as resting [[on the śk’.|on the ṛk’.]] As the melody [[(Pāman)|(Sāman)]] is sung [[on a sk perse,|on a ṛk verse,]] the [[Saman is said|Sāman is said]] in this verse (4) as placed in a boat. Verse 4 may also convey another meaning. Sāman also means ‘reconciliation’ and the verse may be taken as referring to the legend that Yama gave the boy a boon that the boy’s father would become well-disposed to him. Verse 5 contains a question (supposed to be asked by Yama). It means ‘who is the father of this boy (meaning probably that the father must be a bad man as he sent his own son to Yama)? Who sent up the chariot? Who would tell me to-day how (this boy) [[became anudeyi|became anudeyī]] (a small gift [[accom panying|accompanying]] [[P1544]] the large principal gift) ?’ Then follows the verse on which Mr. Karandikar relies as expressing [[Karms|Karma]] and rebirth. [[That verso|That verse]] literally means ‘as it or he became [[an anudeyi,|an anudeyī,]] it stood in front ( or on top); the bottom (or base) was [[apread|spread]] in front (or first or to the east), the coming out (or end) was behind (or afterwards or to the west).’ This conveys no clear sense. But it is possible to explain it in this way, viz, though the boy was sent by his father as [[an anudeyi (as & subsidiary|an anudeyī (as a subsidiary]] gift) still he came on top of all (in that Yama was pleased with him and bestowed on him [[three boobs);|three boons);]] he was in front of all though he (boy) was at the bottom of all gifts (i. e. though he came last of all gifts)’. There is another possible way of [[explai ning|explaining]] this verse. Each Sāman in a stotra has five parts called [[Prastāya,|Prastāva,]] [[Udgitha,|Udgītha,]] [[Pratibāra,|Pratihāra,]] Upadrava and Nidhana (finale); vide H. of Dh. Vol. II p. 1169 note 2589. Naciketas (who was a subsidiary donation following the donation of [[all hia|all his]] father’s property and designated [[‘anudeyi’)|‘anudeyī’)]] is deemed here to have chanted a Sāman; when he began that, [[the ik verse|the ṛk verse]] on which [[the Saman rests|the Sāman rests]] went forth. The bottom of a Sāman is [[a fk verse.|a ṛk verse.]] Therefore it is again said that the base or bottom went forward and the [[‘Nirayapa’|‘Nirayaṇa’]] (i. e, the [[nidbana|nidhana]] [[of the Saman)|of the Sāman)]] came last. Mr. Karandikar explains verse 6 as ’the cremation rite of the body of a deceased person shows the two-fold path; by the front path he should go to the highest world or by the hind path he should return to this world’ (p. 776). It passes the [[presont|present]] author’s understanding how this translation follows from the words of verse 6. [[Verge 7|Verse 7]] of the hymn [[presenta|presents]] no difficulty; it means ’this is the house of Yama that is [[called the the palace|called the palace]] of gods; here is played (or blown) the flute (or tube or trumpet) for him (Yama); he (Yama) is embellished with songs (of praise ).’

An interesting passage of the [[Taittiriya-samhita|Taittirīya-saṁhitā]]22 may be cited here. “Whoever threatens a brāhmaṇa, he shall atone for [[P1545]] it with a hundred years; he who strikes him (shall atone) with a thousand years; he who sheds the blood of a brāhmaṇa would not know the world of the Fathers for as many years as the number of the particles of dust that would be moistened into a ball by the stream of blood. Therefore a person should not threaten [[a brāhmapa or|a brāhmaṇa or]] strike him or draw blood from his body, since ( in doing so ) so much (sin) is involved.” This does not mean that only the world of Fathers had been conceived at the time of this passage (as [[Deugsen|Deussen]] supposes on p. 325 of his Ph. Up.). It will be shown below that [[even the Rgveda|even the Ṛgveda]] was well aware of both Devayāna [[and Pitryāṇa.|and Pitṛyāṇa.]] Most men would [[go to pitsloka|go to pitṛloka]] presided over by Yama according [[to the Rg.|to the Ṛg.]] and only the blessed would go by the Devayāna to the world of gods. This passage is important as conveying that for a very mortal sin the offender would have to suffer for a thousand years or even several thousand years and, therefore, would have to be born for several lives, since one human life was held to extend to one hundred years only [[(Rg. X, 161,|(Ṛg. X. 161.]] 4 = Atharva III. 11. 4, [[Rg. 1. 89.9=Vāj.|Ṛg. I. 89. 9 = Vāj.]] S. 25, 22). Following the above Tai. S. passage, the Gautama Dh. S. provided that threatening a brāhmaṇa in anger would bar going to heaven for a hundred years (or lead to [[hell for &o),|hell for &c.),]] striking him would bar for a thousand years and on drawing blood there would be bar for as many years as the number of the particles of dust that would be made into a ball by the stream of blood. Manu (XI. 206-7) understands that [[the goveral|the several]] [[acts against a brāhmana mentioned|acts against a brāhmaṇa mentioned]] therein would lead to the offender being consigned to hell for 100, for 1000 or for thousands of years. Jaimini [[(III, 4,|(III. 4.]] 17) discusses this passage and holds that the prohibition is not restricted only to [[Darśapū. raamāsa,|Darśapūrṇamāsa,]] but it applies to all brāhmaṇas in all conditions. It is possible to understand by the words [[• satena’|‘śatena’]] and ‘sahasrena’ as a hundred or a thousand cows (or money). The [[Tantravar tika|Tantravārtika]] refers to this meaning but seems to prefer the idea of connecting the two words with years. Vide H. of Dh. Vol. II. pp. 151-152 where the first meaning was put forward and also [[A, B, Keith’s|A. B. Keith’s]] tr. of the [[Tai. S,|Tai. S.]] in the H.O.S. vol. I pp. 216-217 [[him who|‘him who]] reviles him (a brāhmaṇa) he shall fine with a hundred’ [[&o.|&c.]]

The doctrine of how a man’s own works and conduct [[fashion bis|fashion his]] future life is taught as follows in the23 Bṛ. Up. [[IV.4.5-7,|IV. 4. 5–7:]] [[P1546]] “so truly according as he works and according as he behaves so will he be, a man of good deeds will become (be born) good, [[a a man|a man]] of evil deeds will become (be born) evil; he becomes holy by holy deeds, evil by evil deeds. Here they say ‘a person only consists of desires (or is fashioned out of) desires and as his desire is so will be his ( determination or ) will and as is his will, so is his deed; and whatever deeds he does that he will become (reap).”24 On this there is [[av erse To whatever|a verse ‘To whatever]] a man’s mind and subtle body are attached to that he goes together with (the fruits of) his deeds and after having obtained the end [[P1547]] (the last [[rosults)|results)]] of whatever deeds he does in this world, he [[re turns|returns]] again from that world ( where he temporarily went by way of [[roward)|reward)]] to this world of action; so much with regard to him who is consumed by desires; now concerning the man who has no desires (akāmayamāna); he who is without desire, free from desire, in whose case desires are laid to rest, who is himself his own desire, his vital spirits do not withdraw elsewhere but he, being brahma itself (here), is absorbed [[into brahina|into brahma]] (or attains to brahma). On this point there is a verse: ‘When all [[hanker ings|hankerings]] that found an abode in a man’s heart vanish, then he who was liable to death becomes immortal, here (in this body itself) he attains to brahma.” In the above passage the sequence is : desire, will and act.

About the grandeur of this [[paggage|passage]] it is enough to quote what Deussen (in ‘Philosophy of the Upanishads’ p. 348) says there follow words than which deeper, truer, more noble were never uttered by human lips. Vide also remarks of Gerald Heard in ‘Is god evident’ (Faber and [[Gaber,|Faber,]] London) p. 34 ‘Here (in Vedānta and Mahāyāna) is spirituality showing its full fruits; freedom from physical craving, from economic possessiveness, from social desire for recognition and that triple freedom removing all [[fear!|fear’]] and he hopes that the Vedānta picture, far from being [[contra dioted,|contradicted,]] is being increasingly confirmed by modern science.

This sublime passage is [[proceded|preceded]] and followed by [[illus trations|illustrations]] two of which may be cited for explaining the [[ocnception|conception]] of the passage of the soul from one body to another: ‘Just as the caterpillar, having reached the tip of one blade of grass, makes an approach to another blade, draws itself towards it and establishes itself thereon, so this (individual) self, casting down this body in death, dispelling avidyā (ignorance) and making approach to another body draws itself to another body and [[esta blishes|establishes]] (or identifies itself) therein.’ [[This is Br. Up.|This is Bṛ. Up.]] IV. 4. 3. Another illustration [[(in Br. Up.|(in Bṛ. Up.]] IV. [[4. 7) is just as|4. 7) is ‘just as]] the skin [[(alough)|(slough)]] of a snake lies dead and cast-off in an ant-hill, in the same way the body lies (dead and cast-off) and then [[the self in bodiless,|the self is bodiless,]] immortal spirit, is brahma only and is light only.’

This whole passage [[(Bp.|(Bṛ.]] Up. IV. 4. 5-7) is the leading, the oldest and the clearest passage on the doctrine of [[trans migration|transmigration]] in the Upaniṣads. There are several others of [[nimilar|similar]] import. At the end of the story about Yājñavalkya [[and Artabhāga|and Ārtabhāga]] referred to above (where Yājñavalkya discussed [[vith Aratbhāga|with Ārtabhāga]] alone in private the question as to what happens [[P1548]] to a man when the body dies) the Upaniṣad states ‘what they said was Karma alone, what they praised was Karma alone viz.25 that a man [[becomos|becomes]] good by good works and evil by [[evil works.|evil works.’]] These two are the fundamental passages which express the reason and motive that lie at the basis of the [[doutrine|doctrine]] of transmigration,

The gist of these two passages is that works and conduct done in this life fashion a man’s future life and that the present birth of a man depends on his actions and conduct in a past life or lives. But works and conduct are the result of volition or will and this last is due to desires. A man may [[baye|have]] several desires, he may curb some of them, but may make a resolve to consummate some of his desires. Therefore, desires [[(kūma)|(kāma)]] are the root of volition, of works and conduct and ultimately of the cycle of birth and deaths (that is called [[‘samsāra’).|‘saṁsāra’).]] Hence [[Saṅkarācārya,|Śaṅkarācārya,]] following the idea of the verse [[‘gadā|‘yadā]] sarve [[pramucyanto|pramucyante]] kāmā’(in [[Bļ.|Bṛ.]] Up. IV. 5. 7) [[gays|says]] ‘Kāmo [[mālam|mūlaṁ]] [[samsārasya’|saṁsārasya’]] (Kāma is the [[root of samsāra).|root of saṁsāra).]]

Then there is another important passage in the [[Bf,|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. There the story is told [[of Svetaketu,|of Śvetaketu,]] son of [[Arupi,|Āruṇi,]] who being proud of his learning, came to the assembly hall of the [[Pancālas|Pañcālas]] and saw there [[Pravābana|Pravāhaṇa]] Jaivali( a kṣatriya or prince) being waited upon by servants. When the prince saw him he [[asked Svetaketu|asked Śvetaketu]] ‘have you been taught by your father’? [[When Svetaketu|When Śvetaketu]] replied ‘yes’, the prince put to him five questions [[ViZ.|viz.]] (1) do you know how men when they depart from this world go in separate ( different) directions; (2) Do you know how they come back to this world; (3) Do you know how the yonder world does not become full with many men going there again and again; (4) Do you know at the offering of which [[obla tion|oblation]] waters become endowed with human voice and rise and speak; (5) Do you know the access to the path called [[‘Devayāna “|‘Devayāna’]]26 [[P1549]] and to the path [[oalled|called]] [[‘Pitryāṇa’|‘Pitṛyāṇa’]] [[(1.e.|(i. e.]] the deeds by which men gain access to the paths called [[Devayana|Devayāna]] [[ans Pitryāṇa),|and Pitṛyāṇa),]] for we have heard the saying of a sage ‘I heard two paths for men, one leading to the Fathers and the other leading to the Devas; on those two paths all the world that is active moves on, whatever exists between father (sky) and mother (earth).’ To all these five [[questions Svetaketu|questions Śvetaketu]] replied that he did not know any one of them. The prince offered [[hospitality but Svetaketu|hospitality but Śvetaketu]] ran to his father and demanded how the latter could say that he had been thoroughly-taught by him and that he could not answer even one of the five questions that the fellow of [[a Rajanya|a Rājanya]] asked. Then the father replied that he had taught all that he knew but he himself did not know the answers to those questions. He went to the prince [[(ksatriya)|(kṣatriya)]] who honoured him with offerings. [[Ārupi|Āruṇi]] did not want wealth but the replies to those questions. The prince [[said como|said ‘come]] [[as pupil’|as pupil.’]] [[Aruṇi (Gautama)|Āruṇi (Gautama)]] stated he came [[as & pupil.|as a pupil.]] The [[pridice|prince]] stated that the vidyā27 that he would teach was never before with any brāhmaṇa. Then he [[propounds to Svetaketu|propounds to Śvetaketu]] the [[anwers|answers]] to the five questions (to be brief) viz. that the five fires are (figuratively) heaven, god of rain, the earth, man and woman, and the five [[āhutts|āhutis]] (oblations) are [[sraddhā|śraddhā]] (faith), Soma (Moon), rain, food and seed. This answers the 4th question. The first and fifth questions are [[angwered|answered]] by the [[statement Some go|statement ‘Some go]] by the path of Devas, others by that [[of pitts|of pitṛs]] and others (like flies and worms) know no path (they merely live and die )’; vide [[Bļ.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 15–16. The 2nd and 3rd questions are answered by the same viz. those that go by the path of pitṛs return to the earth and others that go to brahman do not return and therefore the world does not become full.

In the Ch. Up. [[V. 3. %|V. 3. 2]] the questions are put in a slightly [[‘different|different]] form :(1) do you know to what [[place mon go|place men go]] from here, (2) how they return, (3) do you know where the path of devas and the path [[of tbe|of the]] fathers diverge, (4) why the world never becomes full, (5) why in the 5th oblation water is called ‘man.’ The answers to these [[in Bś. Up.|in Bṛ. Up.]] [[and Chāu.|and Chān.]] Up. are not identical, though very similar. A fire has five constituents, fuel, smoke, flame, live coals, sparks. In both Chān. Up. V.10,4-9 [[and Bļ. Up.|and Bṛ. Up.]] VI. 2. 9-13 the fires are the same, but the constituents of [[P1550]] [[oach|each]] of these five differ slightly; [[oompare,|compare,]] for example, [[Br. Up. VI.|Bṛ. Up. VI.]] 2. 11 with Chān. Up. V. 3.6. The first question in the Ch. Up. is answered by the mention of the two paths. The 2nd is answered in Chān. Up. V. 10. 3-5.28 The paths diverge (3rd question ) after reaching the moon (Chān. Up. V. 10. 2 and 4-5), the 4th is answered in Chān. V. 10. 8. The [[fifth in answered|fifth is answered]] by means of the statement on [[pancāgaividyā.|pañcāgnividyā.]]

Before proceeding further some remarks are called for about what might possibly happen when a man’s body dies. There are mainly three possibilities, viz. (1) annihilation, (2) endless retribution in heaven or hell and (3) punarjanma [[(transmigra tion.|(transmigration).]] Those who do not believe in an individual immortal self, including some most eminent29 men such as G. B. Shaw, hold the first view and even in ancient India (as the [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] [[I 20 testifies)|I. 20 testifies)]] there were people who had doubts about survival after death. Those who hold that there is no survival after death are not troubled by other questions. Therefore, the most vital question is the one about survival after death. The very first verse [[of Sv.|of Śv.]] Up. puts forward four problems; is brahma the cause, whence do we come, what sustains us and whither we are going? Many of those who believe in God, heaven and hell do not admit pre-existence of the soul but only post-existence. They believe that if a man [[loads &|leads a]] virtuous career in this life (and [[virtua|virtue]] according to them consists in obedience to the will [[P1551]] of God as [[disolosed|disclosed]] to their intellect in Revelation such as the Bible or the Koran) he would have an eternal life of bliss in heaven and that if one [[lod|led]] a life of sin and evil, he would after death remain for ever in hell. Very few accept the first [[possibllity|possibility]] (annihilation) because it is in conflict with man’s cherished desires and with deep-rooted emotional certitude that efforts made in a long life, the mental and spiritual equipment [[acquired: Cannot|acquired cannot]] have been meant to be dissolved altogether without leaving any trace behind. The 2nd possibility also leads to the prospect of eternal reward or eternal damnation for acts done during a life of a few years’ span and becomes unacceptable to many by the unparalleled disproportion between deeds (as [[Gauses|causes]]) and their endless rewards or retribution (as effects). Therefore, to many people the third possibility of transmigration appeals, since it allows continued existence of the soul after physical death in some other forms and [[environments,|environments.]]

The above [[Upanigad|Upaniṣad]] passages are enough to show how the [[dootrine|doctrine]] of transmigration was being moulded in the Upaniṣad period. The [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] knows the two paths of Devayāna and [[Pitryāpa|Pitṛyāṇa]] and also that in heaven there were joys and delights but the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] does not say how long the joys of Heaven were to last and makes no clear and definite statement on the doctrine of transmigration. In the [[Brahmana|Brāhmaṇa]] period the two paths were often referred to and the conception had dawned upon the [[minda|minds]] of thinkers that man might have to pass through death several times ([[punarmstyu|punarmṛtyu]]). But even then there is hardly any definite theory of punarjanma based on good or bad deeds. The clearest statements (and probably earliest) of the origin of the doctrine of transmigration are the two passages (Bṛ. Up. III. 3. 13 and IV 4. 5-7) in which [[Yājāavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] is concerned and is the instructor and emphasizes that it is man’s own works and conduct that lead a man to [[now|new]] births. In both those passages the Devayāna and [[Pitryāṇa|Pitṛyāṇa]] paths are not [[mention ed|mentioned]] at all. But the [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 16 and [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. V. 10 relate the two paths to transmigration and speak of a third place for those born as worms and flies. This is an addition to the [[dootrine|doctrine]] of the two paths, but they make a further [[departure,|departure.]] The [[Chān,|Chān.]] Up. [[V. 10.5|V. 10. 5]] states that those that [[performa|perform]] sacrifices, works of public utility and charity go to the moon and when the results of their actions are exhausted after staying in the moon they have to return to this world by the path they [[went(i, o.|went (i. e.]] from the Moon to ether, then to [[Vayu|Vāyu]], smoke, mist, cloud and rain [[P1552]] and they may be born from a mother’s womb.30 This shows that a double retribution awaits those who perform sacrifices [[&o. yie.|&c. viz.]] stay in the moon for a time and their rebirth on this earth. Other Upaniṣads follow the Chān. Up. in the theory of double retribution e.g. the [[Prasna|Praśna]] has the following passage in prose:31 “The year indeed is [[Prajapati|Prajāpati]], there are two parts thereof, the southern and the northern. Now those who believe in sacrifice and gifts for public utility as work that must be done, secure the moon only as their (future) world and it is they who return to this world. Therefore, the [[rșis|ṛṣis]] who desire offspring resort to the southern (path). That path of the Fathers is indeed treasure (or wealth). But those who, after having sought the Ātman by austerities, (sexual) abstinence, faith and knowledge go by the northern path to the Sun. This is the home of the vital spirits, it is immortal, free from fear, it is the highest (or final) end. Thence they do not return, that is a check to [[otber|other]] things. On this there is a verse ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 164. 12) ‘some call [[hiri|him]] the father with five feet (the five seasons) and with twelve [[fornis|forms]] (twelve months), the giver of rain in the highest of heaven; others again say that the sage is placed in the lower half, [[w|in]] the chariot with seven wheels (horses or rays of sun) and six [[spokee’”.|spokes’”.]] This verse of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] is probably quoted here in support of the conception of the two paths [[symbolisily|symbolically]] represented as two parts of the year, as the [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] [[Verse|verse]] ([[fast|first]] half) appears to [[rofer|refer]] to the Sun, placed in highest half of [[deaven|heaven]] and as the second half seems to refer to a lower ([[upan|upari]]) half of heaven with [[sir|six]] spokes (i.e. the six months of [[Daksinayana|Dakṣiṇāyana]]). Deussen (Ph. Up. p. 338) is [[positiva|positive]] that [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. [[164.1:|164. 12]] has nothing to do with [[P1553]] [[with|]] the subject [[of the two paths).|(of the two paths).]] But what is there to prevent an ancient sage of the Upaniṣadic times from holding that the verse in its two halves refers (figuratively or symbolically) to two paths, particularly when even in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] times paths called Devayāna and [[Pitryāṇa|Pitṛyāṇa]] were well-known as shown below? It should be noted that in the preceding verse (I. 164. 11) the wheel of [[įta|ṛta]] (the year or the sun) is [[Drādaśāra|Dvādaśāra]] (with twelve spokes, viz. months) and therefore when [[ṣadare|ṣaḍare]] (with six spokes) is mentioned in I. 164. 12 a period of six months could well have been intended or could very well be got by interpretation.

The [[Kauṣitaki|Kauṣītaki]] Up. (I. 2-3), however, speaks only of [[Devaya na|Devayāna]] and [[Pitryāṇa|Pitṛyāṇa]] and has no third place for worms and birds &c. and states (vide n 2520 below) that worms [[&o.|&c.]] also come to the same world to which men return. Further, the stations of the [[Dova yāna|Devayāna]] path (Agni, bright half of the [[inonth|month]] etc.) [[bad|had]] been given a counterpart in the [[Pitryāṇa|Pitṛyāṇa]] path in the [[Bș.|Bṛ.]] Up. and [[Chāu.|Chān.]] Up. which made the moon the point of divergence for the two paths. The Kauṣītaki Up. omits all the preliminary stations up to the moon and brings all transmigrating beings to the moon (in I. 2). There are some other variations also that need not be dwelt upon here.

Deussen (in Ph. Up. p. 318) argues that in the [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] verse (X. 88. 15) the two ways are really to be understood as day and night and he translates that verse as ‘I have heard from my forefathers that there are two ways alike for gods and men’, thus giving to the [[world’|word ‘]][[pitṛnām’|pitṝṇām’]] the sense of the ablative and holding that ‘my’ is to be understood before [[pitsṇām|pitṝṇām]], though not to be understood before the other two words in the genitive in the same verse. The [[mean ing|meaning]] which the [[Upanigad|Upaniṣad]] reads in the [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] verse is proper and also the original meaning of the verse. The path [[oalled|called]] [[Pitryāṇa|Pitṛyāṇa]]32 is mentioned in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] [[X. 2,7|X. 2. 7]] ([[Agoi|Agni]] knows well the path called [[pitsýāṇa|pitṛyāṇa]]) and [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 18. 1 runs ‘O Death! Follow another path which is your own and different from [[Davayāna|Devayāna]].’ These two [[Verses|verses]] [[olearly|clearly]] show that the [[Rgvedic|Ṛgvedic]] [[sagos|sages]] had arrived at the conception of paths called [[Devayāps|Devayāna]] and [[Pitryāṇa|Pitṛyāṇa]]. Therefore, the express mention of two paths in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 88. 15. in [[connnection|connection]] with [[Pits|Pitṛs]] and Devas should be deemed to refer to [[Pitryāṇa|Pitṛyāṇa]] and [[Dovayāna|Devayāna]] paths and it is far-fetched to take them as day and [[P1554]] night, which are [[bardly|hardly]] ever referred to in the [[Roveda|Ṛgveda]] as [[patha.|paths.]] This [[verae|verse]] is explained in the [[Sat.|Śat.]] Br. XII. 8. 1. 2133 (quoted below) which says that the two ways are those of the gods and [[pitys|pitṛs]] and the commentary of [[Dvivedaganga|Dvivedagaṅga]] explains the first half in the same way. Vide also [[Sat.|Śat.]] Br. I. 9. 3. 1-2. [[Devayana|Devayāna]] is sometimes used in the plural in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] (as in III. 58. 5, VII. 38. 8, VII. 76. 2, [[x.|X.]] 51. 5, [[x.|X.]] 98. 11). In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] [[x.|X.]] 15. 8 Yama is said to enjoy offerings along with the ancient [[fore fathers|forefathers]] of the sage and in [[x.|X.]] 154. 4 Yama is requested to be united with ancient pitṛs, righteous and full of [[austerities,|austerities.]] The [[Sat.|Śat.]] Br. (XIII. 8. 1. 5) states that the door to the world of [[’|]]the Fathers is in the southwest, while Northeast is the direction of Gods and men ([[1.|I.]] 2. 5. 17 and [[XII,|XII.]] 4. 2. 15). The [[Atharva veda|Atharvaveda]] (XV. 12. 5.) mentions both [[Pitryāpa|Pitṛyāṇa]] and [[Devayana|Devayāna]] paths. Soma, rain, food, retas (semen) may be described as watery, but the question is how the first [[ahuti ‘sraddha’|āhuti ‘śraddhā’]] (faith) can be [[des cribed|described]] as an [[abuti|āhuti]] offered in Agni (viz. the yonder world) by the Gods from which king Soma arises (in [[Chan,|Chān.]] Up. [[V.4,3|V. 4. 2]] and [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 9). This is answered by V. S.34 [[1II.|III.]] 1. 5 [[and (and|(and]] at some length) in the [[Saikarabhasya|Śāṅkarabhāṣya]] thereon.

The [[Kausitaki|Kauṣītaki]] Up. (I)35 sets out rather obscurely the doctrine of the two paths [[48|as]] part of the [[Pancagoividyā|Pañcāgnividyā]] taught [[P1555]] by [[Oitra|Citra]] [[Gargyāyapi|Gārgyāyaṇi]] (v. [[1.|l.]] [[Gangyayni|Gāṅgyāyni]]) to [[Svetaketu|Śvetaketu]], son of [[Aruni.|Āruṇi.]] It is passed over here for reasons of space and as not of material importance [[exoept|except]] one passage [[whioh|which]] runs ‘He ([[Oltra|Citra]]) [[Baid|said]] that all those that depart from this world go to the moon; in the bright half the moon is increased by their spirits, in the dark half the moon [[gends|sends]] them on to be born again. The moon is [[Verily|verily]] the door of the heavenly world. Now if a man disowns the moon (i.e. is dissatisfied with life there) the moon sets him free. But if a man is not dissatisfied then the moon sends him down as rain here (on the earth). And according to his deeds and according to his knowledge he is born again here as a worm, a [[looust|locust]], a bird, a tiger or a lion or a fish, or [[&|a]] snake, as [[&|a]] man or [[88|as]] something else in different places.’ Then I. 3 begins by referring to [[Dovayāna|Devayāna]] and I. 4 ends by [[saying,|saying, ‘]]being [[frood|freed]] from good deeds and from evil deeds, he, the knower of brahman, (neuter) moves towards brahman alone.’

In the [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]]36 Yama tells Naciketas the secret about Brahmavidyā and what the self becomes on the death (of the body) viz. some men go to a mother’s womb for an embodied existence while others are transferred into stumps (of trees) according to their deeds and [[knowlege.|knowledge.]]

The [[Bṭ.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 15-16 and Chān. Up. V. 3. 10 ff deal with the question of those who go by Devayāna and by the [[Pitryāna|Pitṛyāna]] paths. First37 the [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. ‘Those (even householders) who know this ([[Pancāguividyāl|Pañcāgnividyā]]) and also those ([[hormits|hermits]] and [[ascetios|ascetics]]) who, in the forest being full of faith, worship Truth (Brahman, [[Hiranyagarbha|Hiraṇyagarbha]]) go to arcis (light), from [[arois|arcis]] to day ([[uhan|ahan]]) from day to the increasing fortnight (i. [[8.|e.]] [[sukla-pakṣa|śukla-pakṣa]]), from the increasing half to the six months during which the Sun moves in the north, from those six months to the world of the Devas ([[Dayaloka|Devaloka]]), from the [[Dayaloka|Devaloka]] to the Sun, from the sun to lightning. When they have reached the place of lightning a [[P1556]] person born of the mind (of [[Brahma|Brahmā]]) comes to them and leads them to the worlds of Brahmā. In these worlds they being exalted, dwell for ages and there is no return (to [[sannsāra|saṁsāra]]) for them. But they who conquer (attain) worlds by sacrifice, charity and [[aus terities|austerities]] go to smoke, from smoke to night, from [[pight|night]] to [[decrea. sing|decreasing]] half [[of|(of]] the month), from decreasing half month to the six month during which the Sun moves in the south, from these months to the world of the fathers, from the world of the fathers to the Moon, having reached the Moon, they become food and then the [[Deras|Devas]] feed on them there as sacrificers feed on king Soma as it increases and decreases (in a sacrifice). But when this (the result of their works done on earth) is exhausted they return to [[either|ether]] (ākāśa), from that to Vāyu, from Vāyu to rain, from rain to the earth; on reaching the earth they become food. They are then again offered into the fire called man, from that (i. [[e,|e.]] man) they are born in the fire called woman. [[Theso,|These,]] making efforts to secure worlds (by sacrifices &c.), again and again repeat coming to this world. Those, however, who do not know both these paths, they become (are born as) worms, locusts (or birds) and [[flies’|flies.’]]

The Chān. Up. [[V,|V.]] [[10.1-%|10. 1-2]] are almost in the same words as [[Bp,|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 15 with a few slight changes viz. [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. reads [[“Sraddhā|‘Śraddhā]] tapa ityupāsate’ and ‘from months in the north to the year, from year to the Sun, from the Sun to the Moon, from the Moon to lightning; there is a person who is not human ([[aman avaḥ’|‘amānavaḥ’]] for ‘mānasaḥ’ of [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up.) who leads him to brahman &c.; then [[Obān.|Chān.]] Up. V. 10. 3-4 are again the same as [[Bf.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 16 except that [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. [[roads|reads ‘]]those who living in a village practise (a life of) sacrifices, works of public utility (called [[purta|pūrta]]) and almsgiving, go to smoke’, but adds [[these|‘these]] do not reach the year,’ and proceeds [[from|‘from]] the months to the world of fathers, from that world to ether, from ether to the moon, this is king Soma that is the food of Gods (i.e. the gods like or love them). Having dwelt there (in the moon) till their works are exhausted, they return by the same way by which they came i.e. to [[other|ether]], from [[other|ether]] to Vāyu, smoke, mist, cloud. (The persons that had performed sacrifices &c) having become clouds come down as rain, are then born as rice and barley, herbs and trees, sesame and [[māsa|māṣa]] beans. Escape from that state (to that of human [[be. ings|beings]]) is beset with extreme difficulties. Whoever the beings (cattle or other animals or man) may be that eat the food [[:(|(]]into which one has been transformed) and beget offspring [[be|he]] (the transmigrating entity) becomes most like them.’

[[P1557]]

One of the most important passages in the Upaniṣads on [[conduot|conduct]] fashioning the life that would follow is the [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. V. 10. 7-8 which may be rendered as follows:38 [[Those|‘Those]] whose [[oonduct|conduct]] has been good will quickly attain some good birth, the birth of a brāhmaṇa, of a [[ksatriya|kṣatriya]] or of a [[vāisya.|vaiśya.]] But those whose conduct has been evil will [[quikly|quickly]] attain an evil birth, the birth of a dog or a hog or a cāṇḍāla. Those that do not go by either of the two paths become those small creatures (worms, flies [[&o|&c.]]) that are continually returning and whose destiny may be said to be [[to|‘to]] live and die’. Theirs is a third place (apart from the two paths). Therefore the yonder world does not become full. Hence one should be disgusted (with this [[Samsāra|Saṁsāra]]).

It may be stated here that the [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] (VIII. 23-27) also refers to the two paths following one of which a yogin does not return to this world and following the other of which he returns to this world. These are called [[sukla|śukla]] (bright) and [[krśṇa|kṛṣṇa]] gati (in [[VIII,|VIII.]] 26) and [[śști|sṛti]] (in VIII. 27). The former is fire, light,39 day, the bright half of the month, the six months of the northern path of the sun; those men who have realized brahman when going from this world go to brahman (the absolute)’. The latter path is [[smoke,|‘smoke,]] night, the dark half (of the month), the six months of the southern path of the Sun; the yogin [[reaobing|reaching]] the moon-light by that path returns to this world. The [[śāntiparva|Śāntiparva]]40 of the Mahābhārata refers to the northern and southern paths, the latter of which is attained by gifts, study of Veda and sacrifices (as in [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 16 and Chān. Up. V. 10. 8). The [[Yājāavalkya-smrti|Yājñavalkya-smṛti]]41 also refers to those [[P1558]] paths. In III. 195-196 it mentions the [[Pitsyana|Pitṛyāṇa]] path, [[whtok|which]] is [[Very|very]] like the one [[dosoribed|described]] in [[Bs.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 16 (except that [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] omits ākāśa) and [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. [[V,|V.]] 10. 3-7 (which mentions more stages than in [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up.). Yāj. III. 197 also states that those who do not know any one of [[thego|these]] two paths (i. [[6.|e.]] do not perform the acts peculiar to those who go by these paths) become snakes, locusts, creeping insects or worms.

The Vedāntasūtra frequently refers to the doctrine of [[trans migration,|transmigration,]] but owing to the limits imposed by available space only a few important [[autras|sūtras]] and their explanations will be [[get|set]] out here. The three sūtras of V. [[8.|S.]] II. 1. 34-3642 are of great importance for the theory of transmigration. An objector says ’to hold that God is the cause of the world does not stand to reason, for, if it be so, God would be liable to the charges of [[un equal|unequal]] treatment (or dispensation) and cruelty. He creates some that enjoy extreme happiness (like gods and others), some lead an extremely [[migerable|miserable]] life such as beasts (of burden [[&o.|&c.]]) and some like men, who enjoy an intermediate position, [[Boours|secure]] a modicum of enjoyment. So God may be charged with acting through hatred and love (like ordinary [[mon).|men).]] God also produces [[misory|misery]] and finally destroys all persons. This aspect (of great cruelty) appears abhorrent even to evil men. To this the reply is: if god had created inequality in the world at his sweet will and without regard to any other matter, he might have been liable to the two charges of unequal treatment and cruelty. But God has regard to the righteousness (merit) or [[other wise|otherwise]] of beings when he produces inequalities among creatures. The position of God should be looked upon like that of rain, which is the common cause (or factor) in the production of crops of rice and barley, but the difference (in quality) between rice and barley is due to the varying potentialities of the seeds. God is the common cause in the creation of [[beaats|beasts]], men and gods but the inequalities in these are due to the special [[poten. tialities|potentialities]] of each. [[Saskarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] relies expressly on two [[Upa niṣad|Upaniṣad]] passages viz. [[Kaus.|Kauṣ.]] Up. [[ILI.|III.]] 8 and [[Bļ.|Bṛ.]] Up. III. 2. 13 ‘[[punyo|puṇyo]] vai [[puṇyena,|puṇyena]] karmaṇā bhavati pāpaḥ [[pāpena).|pāpena’).]] The Kauṣ. Up. passage means ‘He43 (the Absolute [[Atman|Ātman]]) is not [[P1559]] exalted by good works nor degraded by evil works, but it is He indeed who inspires (a person) to do good works, whom he wishes to lead high over those worlds and it is he who [[in. spires|inspires]] one to do evil deeds whom he wishes to lead down from these worlds’. It will be noticed that the [[Kauṣitaki|Kauṣītaki]] bases the attainment of a higher life (or birth) or a lower life on the quality of men’s deeds (just as in [[Bṭ.|Bṛ.]] Up. III. 2. 13, IV. 4. 5, [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. V. 10. 7, Praśna 3. 7). The [[Gita statos|Gītā states]] ‘I treat them the same way (with appropriate fruits) in which they come to (or approach) [[me."|me.’]]

Another objection is raised to the above proposition contained in V. S. II. 1. 34. The Upaniṣads often state ‘In the beginning there was that only which is one without a second ([[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. VI. 2. 1 [[sad-ova|sad-eva]] somyedam agra [[āsid-ekam ovā dvitiyan|āsīd-ekam evādvitīyam]]). Therefore, [[befora|before]] creation there was no difference between deeds and [[go|so]] there could have been no inequality among the first created beings dependent on difference in deeds. You may say that after difference in deeds arose God has regard to deeds of men, but all beings created in the beginning must have been alike. To this the reply is that [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] is without beginning ([[anadi|anādi]]) and that such [[&|a]] conception of the [[beginning lessness|beginninglessness]] of [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] stands to reason and is supported by (Śruti) texts.

Another discussion on the working of Karma occurs in [[V.8. II, 3,|V. S. II. 3.]] 41-42.44 [[Acoording|According]] to the highest [[metaphysioal|metaphysical]] doctrine the [[Atman|Ātman]] is one and all individual selves are really free but they are affected in the empirical state by the Upādhis of buddhi, mind [[&o,|&c.]] and in that state they are controlled by [[Isvara|Īśvara]] (as [[Anta ryāmin|Antaryāmin]], as [[Bļ.|Bṛ.]] Up. [[III,|III.]] 7. 23 or Kauṣ. Up. III. 8 says) and they [[P1560]] receive the fruits of their good and bad [[aotions|actions]] through [[Layara|Īśvara]] who does not act at his [[gwent|sweet]] will but has [[rogard|regard]] to good or bad conduct.

A few passages about karma and transmigration and stories illustrating how people’s minds were affected by them in ancient times may be cited here. The Āp. Dh.45 [[8.|S.]] provides ‘Members of all [[vargas|varṇas]] ([[olasses|classes]]) reap (in heaven) highest and measureless happiness by carrying out their prescribed duties; thereafter ([[i, e.|i. e.]] after enjoying happiness in heaven) they return (to this world) on account of the (unenjoyed) residue of the results of their actions and secure birth in an (appropriate) caste (or family), beauty of form, charming complexion, power, mental ability, wisdom, wealth, the (blessing of the) performance of duties and this results in happiness only in both worlds like a wheel. A similar rule applies to the increase of the results of evil acts. The thief of gold, one guilty of brāhmaṇa murder, according as he belongs to the brāhmaṇa, [[ksatriya|kṣatriya]] or [[vaibya|vaiśya]] class, after undergoing torments in hell for a limited time becomes [[respec tively|respectively]] [[&|a]] [[candālu,|cāṇḍāla,]] [[paulkusa|paulkasa]] or vaina.’ The Gautama [[Dharma sūtra|Dharmasūtra]] [[bas|has]] a passage in very similar words: ‘Members of the [[varpaṣ|varṇas]] (brāhmaṇa and others) and of [[aśramas|āśramas]] ([[brabmacaria|brahmacārin]] &c) who are devoted to performing the appropriate duties (of their class or stage in life) enjoy the fruit (heaven) of their actions after death and then by virtue of the residue (of their actions) they attain a (new) birth (in this world) endowed with a good country, caste, family, life, learning, character, wealth, mental ability and happiness. Those who act contrary to this are ruined by having to resort to many (evil) births.’ On [[V,|V.]] S. [[III,|III.]] 1. 8 [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] after quoting Gautama XI. 29 states [[there|‘there]] is no possibility of the destruction of the effect of an evil deed except by some of the methods prescribed by [[sāstra|śāstra]] (such as expiations and realisation of the Absolute) and that it is possible that a good deed may begin to yield its reward [[P1561]] after a long time because it is hampered by the results of an evil deed and quotes a [[smrti|smṛti]] (Mahābhārata) in support.46

The principle of the doctrine of Karma is that every act, whether good or bad, produces a certain result or return which cannot be escaped. In the physical world there is the universal law of causation. The doctrine of Karma extends this inexorable law of causality to the mental and moral sphere. The doctrine of Karma is not a mechanical law; it is rather a moral or a [[spiri tual|spiritual]] necessity. It cannot, however, be said that this doctrine is an induction from [[observod|observed]] facts nor [[oan|can]] it be asserted that it is [[ex porimentally|experimentally]] verifiable, but it is only a hypothesis or supposition; it is, however, far better than other naive and childlike theories. In the absence of the theory of karma and rebirth it would have to be assumed that the world is arbitrary, that the Creator is not bound to regard the nature of men’s actions but may distribute rewards as he pleases or by caprice. This doctrine of Karma [[em phasizes|emphasizes]] three things, firstly it regards an existence as a sort of expiation for the doings of a previous existence or existences; secondly, an evil deed cannot be expiated by works of merit but [[ita|its]] punishment must be borne; thirdly, the punishment for wrong is automatic and personal. Under the doctrine of Karma there is no such thing as [[ohance|chance]] or luck. When we use those words they correspond to no reality and are a tacit confession of our ignorance or inability to state the cause or causes of what has happened. This doctrine of Karma leads on to the doctrine of transmigration. The results of a man’s actions may not happen at once or in the present life. The [[Adiparva|Ādiparva]] and Manu say47 [[|’]]an evil deed does not yield its retribution immediately like [[& &|a]] cow (that yields plenty of milk immediately after she is well fed) but returning slowly it [[outs|cuts]] off the very roots of the [[P1562]] [[perpe trator.’|perpetrator.’]] Man’s actions in past existences determine or fashion the nature of the present existence and the actions in the present existence taken along with the residue of past actions will [[deter mine|determine]] the future existence. This, in short, is the basis of the [[dootrine|doctrine]] of [[punarjannur.|punarjanma.]] The modifications introduced by texts or popular notions will be briefly dealt with later. The theory of rebirth is as logical as any of the [[bypothoses|hypotheses]] that hold the field about what happens after physical death of the body. It is certainly as satisfactory as (or rather more satisfactory than) the theory of absolute annihilation after physical death (held by atheists) or the theory of eternal [[roward|reward]] or retribution in heaven or hell (for confirmed criminals of one life). The leaders or exponents of almost all religions hold that God is with them and they generally have never seen (up to at least the 19th century A. D.) any good outside their respective Churches. Hinduism of the Upaniṣads and [[Gita|Gītā]] is the only religion and philosophy that proclaimed thousands of years ago that the man of good deeds is nearest to God on account of his goodness and the man of evil deeds cannot secure divine grace and [[follow ghip,|fellowship,]] whomsoever he may regard as a prophet or messiah [[sont|sent]] by God.

The Vedāntasūtra in [[III, 1|III. 1]] examines the passages of the Chān. Up. and of the [[Bṭ.|Bṛ.]] Up. dealing with [[Pañoāgnividya.|Pañcāgnividyā.]] It is not possible to set out in detail the discussions in the bhāṣya of Śaṅkara on the sūtras in [[V, S.|V. S.]] III. 1. Some of the important [[final.conclusions|final conclusions]] are: The individual self, while passing from one body to another, is accompanied or surrounded by subtle elements ([[bhūtasūksma|bhūtasūkṣma]]), that the [[abutis|āhutis]] are spoken of as ‘āpaḥ[[|’]] (in Chān. Up. [[V.9.|V. 9.]] 1) because the human body is full of [[fluida|fluids]] in the form of chyle, blood &c., because sacred works like Agnihotra [[&o.|&c.]] are the causes of a new body after death and in those works the principal materials used (such as Soma juice, ghee, milk) are mainly fluid. In the statement that those who perform sacrifices &c. go to the moon by the [[Pitfyāpa|Pitṛyāṇa]] path and that śraddhā is offered as oblation from which Soma that is the food of gods arises, the words ‘food of gods’ are used in a metaphorical sense (and not in the sense of eating). The souls of sacrificers and the like, after reaching the moon and enjoying the fruits of their good deeds that can be enjoyed in the moon, return to the earth by the way they went but in reverse order of stations for enjoying the fruits of their actions that [[oan|can]] only be [[P1563]] enjoyed on the earth.48 This combines the idea of a life beyond (often referred to in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]]) with the notion of rebirth and offers two prizes for good deeds ([[Fiz,|viz.]] reward in heaven and then [[&|a]] rebirth with material well-being and cultural environment, as in Gautama Dh. [[8. XI,|S. XI.]] 29 and [[Gita|Gītā]] VI. 37-45 and there is a double penalty for evil deeds ([[viz,|viz.]] hell torture and then a despised or low life).

The V. S. ([[III,|III.]] 1. 13-17) further explains that all men do not go to the moon, but only those that perform sacrifices &c. and those that do not perform sacrifices or works of public utility but are guilty of evil acts go to the abode of Yama for [[under going|undergoing]] tortures of hell49 (which are seven, according to [[V, S. III.1.15|V. S. III. 1. 15]]) and after that they come to the earth. Those who follow the path of faith and austerity go by the Devayāna path (Chān. Up. V. 10. 1 and [[Mundaka|Muṇḍaka]] I. 2. 11) and those who perform sacrifices, charity and works of [[publio|public]] utility go by the [[pitryāṇa|pitṛyāṇa]] path ([[Chan,|Chān.]] Up. V. 10. 3 and [[Muṣdaka|Muṇḍaka]] I. 2. 10)50 and those that do not follow any one of the two have to go to a third place and be born as worms &c. ([[Chan,|Chān.]] Up. V. 10. 8), that when [[&|a]] śruti text like [[Kauṣitaki|Kauṣītaki]] Up. (I. 2) states that all those that depart from this world go to the moon, what is meant by ‘all’ is all those that have the adhikāra (capability or fitness) to go to the moon.

There is one word, namely [[sarisara’,|‘saṁsāra’,]] which occurs frequently in the comparatively later literature on Vedānta and [[Dharma Śāstra|Dharmaśāstra]] but rarely in the Upaniṣads.
