It means [[passing|‘passing]] through a succession or a round or a cycle of births and deaths.’ The [[P1564]] [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] says51 [[óthat|‘that]] person who has no understanding, who has not controlled his mind, who is always impure, does not attain that (highest) state and undergoes [[samsūra|saṁsāra]] ([[birtha|births]] and [[deaths).|deaths)’.]] The [[Sv.|Śv.]] Up. VI. 16 speaks of the Supreme Spirit as the creator and knower of the universe, as self-born, the knower, the destroyer of time, possessing (all) qualities, [[know ing|knowing]] everything, Lord of Pradhāna, individual souls and the guṇas (sattva, rajas, tamas) and as the cause of release from [[samsāra|saṁsāra]], of sustaining it and of bondage.’ The [[Maitrāyaṇi|Maitrāyaṇī]] Up. I. 4. says ‘such being the nature of [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] what is the use of the enjoyment (of pleasures)’. The Muktikā Up. (II. 37) states ‘mind is established as the root of the [[tréo|tree]] of [[sargāra’,|saṁsāra’.]] The word ‘[[samsāra|saṁsāra]]’ occurs in the Vedāntasūtra52 [[IV.2.8.|IV. 2. 8.]] The [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] mentions the word several times e.g. ‘men who have no faith in this way (of life) do not attain to me (Lord [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]]) and return to the path of death and transmigration (IX. 3); those whose minds are fixed on me I deliver in no time from the ocean of death and transmigration’ ([[XL.|XII.]] 7). The [[Manusmrti|Manusmṛti]] employs the word [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] frequently e.g. in the table of the subjects to be treated in the [[Smrti|Smṛti]] ’the passing through [[samsāra|saṁsāra]]’ is mentioned (in I. 117) and the 12th [[obap.|chap.]] uses the word often. It is said (in XII. 51) ’the entire [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] of three kinds due to three sorts of [[Kamma|Karma]] affecting all beings has been expounded’. After describing the various [[character istics|characteristics]] of the three guṇas, sattva, rajas and tamas (in XII. 26–29), and the effects of these guṇas (XII. 30-38), Manu states that those in whom sattva, rajas or tamas preponderates respectively become gods, men or lower animals and again divides each of these three classes into lowest, middling and [[bighest|highest]] ([[XI.|XII.]] 40-50). Manu [[Uses|uses]] the word ‘[[samsāra|saṁsāra]]’ in the plural (in [[XI.|XII.]] 52, 54, 70) in the sense of [[gate’|‘gati’]] or ‘yoni’. In chap. VI. 40-60 Manu first dilates at length on the daily routine of one who has entered on the stage of sannyāsa, what he should wear and carry, how he should maintain himself, how he should restrain [[P1565]] his senses and give up love and hate and practise [[ahiinsā|ahiṁsā]] and then proceeds ‘he should ponder over the destinies of men due to bad deeds, falling in hell and the tortures of hell, separation from loved ones and contact with undesirable persons, the ravages of old age and the pains of diseases, the departing from the body and again lying in the womb and the passage of the soul through thousands of crores of births of all sorts.’ It is entirely wrong to suppose (as done by Mr. Sanjana on p. 10 of his ‘Dogma of [[reinoarnation’|reincarnation’]]) that Manu prescribes that [[each|‘each]] ego has to go through ten thousand millions of existences.’ Manu [[nowhore|nowhere]] says so. All that he means is that the [[Sannyasin|Sannyāsin]] desirous of liberation should revolve in his mind the possibility of some souls having to migrate through millions of births. Yāj. (in [[III, 169|III. 169]]) employs the verb [[samsarati’|‘saṁsarati’]] in the sense of [[“undergoes|‘undergoes]] births’ and states ‘The fruition of actions done by some [[mon|men]] results after death (in other bodies) or in this very life (as in the case of [[Karli|Kārīrī]] sacrifice) and in some cases either in this world or the next (i. [[e,|e.]] there is no hard and fast [[gāstra|śāstra]] rule that fruits of actions result immediately after they are done); it also states in a fine simile53 [[just|‘just]] as an actor, when acting different parts, colours his body in different colours (fair, dark, brown), so the soul assumes various forms (as short, hunchback, [[&o.),|&c.),]] [[aggumos|assumes]] different bodies due to the various acts done by it. In Yāj. [[IIT.|III.]] 140 the word saṁsāra itself [[occurs .The|occurs ‘The]] soul affected by (or under the influence of) rajas and tamas, wandering in this world and coming in contact with undesirable objects (or mental attitudes) goes through transmigration (i.e. assumes various [[bodies).|bodies)’.]] This is in reply to the question in Yāj. III. 129 ([[1śvaraḥ|Īśvaraḥ]] sa katham [[bhāvairaniṣtaiḥ|bhāvairaniṣṭaiḥ]] samprayujyate). The [[Śantiparva|Śāntiparva]]54 remarks [[“there|‘there]] is no doubt that in life there is far more misery than happiness’. The [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] often harp on the theme that [[sarnsāra|saṁsāra]] is impermanent, abounds in [[sorrowE|sorrows]] and is fragile like a plantain leaf ([[e.&|e. g.]] vide the Brahmapurāṇa [[* samsāre…,|‘saṁsāre…,]] anitye duḥkhabahule kadalīdalasannibhe’ (178. 179). The present writer cannot help feeling that this view of the thorough miserableness of life has been so much and so persistently dinned into the ears of common people not only by [[P1566]] much of Sanskrit literature but also by medieval saints like Tukaram (who says ‘[[bappiness|happiness]] is as little as a grain of barley while misery is as big as a mountain’) that people have lost zest in life, are unable to put their heart and soul and serious efforts for improving their own lot and the lot of others. It cannot be denied that in India the doctrine of Karma, instead of being a powerful means of urging all men to put great efforts in doing good deeds and a gospel of hope, became in the minds of many confused with [[fataliam,|fatalism,]] which led men to [[beoome feeblo,|become feeble,]] submissive and disinclined to work [[hard,|hard.]]

All the above discussion in the Upaniṣads about [[transmi. gration|transmigration]] of the individual self are valid and concerned with the empirical or phenomenal world ([[samsārāvasthā|saṁsārāvasthā]] or [[vyavahārā. Vasthā|vyavahārāvasthā]]) but considered from the highest metaphysical [[stand point|standpoint]] of thorough-going advaita (the parā vidyā of [[Mundaka|Muṇḍaka]] I. 1. 5-6 or amūrta brahma of [[Bṭ.|Bṛ.]] Up. II. 3. 5-6) it falls to the ground, since the individual self is non-different from the Absolute [[Bralıman.|Brahman.]] [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] on [[V, S. II,|V. S. II.]] 3. 30 emphasizes this point. He states: As long as the individual self is in [[sainsāra|saṁsāra]] state and has not attained perfect knowledge and brought the empirical or phenomenal state to an end, so long the connection of the self with buddhi does not cease. As long as this connection with buddhi (a limiting adjunct) lasts, so long lasts the state of being an individual self merged in [[sargāra.|saṁsāra.]] But the real truth is that there is no entity called [[jiva|jīva]] by itself except in so far as it is fictitiously hypostatized by buddhi, the limiting adjunct. For, when we determine the real meaning of Vedānta texts, we find no intelligent substance other than the one omniscient Lord whose nature is eternal freedom. [[Thon Śankarācārya|Then Śaṅkarācārya]] cites certain texts (viz. Bṛ. Up. I. 4. 7, III. 7-13, [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. VI. 1. 6, VI. 8. [[7|7)]] 55 and remarks that there are [[P1567]] [[bundreds|hundreds]] of such texts. [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] states that even [[Badarāyana|Bādarāyaṇa]], the author of the Vedāntasūtras, composed some sūtras from the highest advaita standpoint and some from the [[empirioal|empirical]] standpoint. In the following sūtras of V. [[8.|S.]] [[Bādarāyana|Bādarāyaṇa]] is obliged to differentiate [[Jiva|Jīva]] and Paramātman [[1|I.]] 1. 16-17; I. 1. 21, I. 2. 20, [[1, 3.5,|I. 3. 5,]] [[11.|II.]] 1. 21-23, [[II,|II.]] 3. 21. II. 3. 41, II. 3. 43 [[&o.|&c.]] But the sūtras I. 1. 33, II. 1. 14, [[IV,|IV.]] 13 indicate that there is non-difference between the two.56

The theory of transmigration postulates that each life is the result or fruit of the actions of the preceding existence or existences. It follows from this that if we go backwards far enough, no existence or birth can be the first. Therefore, the Vedāntasūtra had to declare (in II. [[1.35|1. 35]] quoted in n. 2527 above) that [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] is anādi (beginningless). But this is in conflict with many passages of the Upaniṣads, which, in speaking of creation, employ the words ‘in the beginning’ (as in Chān. Up. VI. 2. 1, [[Bf.|Bṛ.]] Up. I. 4., 1, 10 and 17, [[V,|V.]] 5. 1, Tai. Up. II. 7. 1). In order to get over this [[oonflict|conflict]] the conception of the recurring creation of the universe periodically from all eternity was postulated by means of the machinery of kalpas,57 which provides that the universe created by brahman persists through a vast period called Kalpa, after the lapse of which it is absorbed into brahman. Vide [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]] 231. 29–32 ([[Ch,|Ch.]] ed. = [[c.|cr.]] ed. 224. 28-31). It is stated in the [[Gitā VIII,|Gītā VIII.]] 17-19 that the day of Brahmā is equal to one thousand yugas (four yugas make one unit or Mahāyuga) and the night of [[Brahma|Brahmā]] is also of the same duration. All objects spring up from [[Praksti|Prakṛti]] at the advent of the day of Brahmā and at the advent of night are absorbed (or merged) in the [[Prakyti.|Prakṛti.]] Vide [[Bhagavadgita|Bhagavadgītā]] IX. 7 also ‘at the end of a Kalpa all elements (or beings) pass into [[prakrti|prakṛti]] over which I preside; but when the next Kalpa starts I send them forth’.

The reasoning is; just as we cannot decide which comes first, the seed [[or’sprouting|or sprouting]] plant, so it is impossible to say which [[P1568]] comes first (body or Karma), since no body is possible without Karma ([[deuds|deeds]]) and no deeds are possible without a body. The [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. (V. 13. 2) states “that Being ([[doity|deity]]) that had produced fire, water, and earth thought ’let me enter those three beings (fire, water and earth) with this living self and then develop names and forms.” That shows that [[Jiva|Jīva]] (the self) was there at the time of creation and thereby indicates that [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] is [[beginning loge.|beginningless.]] The [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] in X. 190. 3, expressly says [[the|‘the]] creator arranged (or created) as before.’58 Similarly, the [[Gitā|Gītā]] (15. 3) states [[the|‘the]] real form of it (of the tree of [[samsāra|saṁsāra]]) is not thus perceived, nor its end nor beginning nor its support; having [[out|cut]] off this deeply rooted Aśvattha (pippala) tree with the powerful sword of non-attachment, that place must be sought for from which those who have reached it do not again return’. The [[Smrti|Smṛti]] verses relied upon by [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] on V. [[8. 1.3.30|S. I. 3. 30]] are noted below.59

[[P1569]]

The [[Bhagavadgita|Bhagavadgītā]] (VI. 37-45) is very emphatic that no exertions made by a man on the path of Yoga with faith are lost even if he does not quickly attain perfection. Lord [[Kroṇa|Kṛṣṇa]] replies (VI. 40 ff) that such a man failing to secure perfection does not come to an evil end, but he attains to the worlds of the righteous, dwells there for many years, is born in the houses of prosperous and pure men or is born in the family of wise [[yoging,|yogins,]] where he regains the mental impressions of his past lives. He makes fresh efforts to gain perfection and is carried forward irresistibly by his practice in former lives and being free from all [[ging|sins]] and perfecting himself through many lives attains the highest goal. In the [[Gita,|Gītā,]] [[Kroṇa|Kṛṣṇa]] says (in [[IV,|IV.]] 5) ‘many are my lives that are past and thine also. All of them I know but thou knowest them not.’ In many places the [[Gitā|Gītā]] touches upon the doctrine of transmigration (such as in II. 12-13 and 22-27, IV. 8-9, [[VII,|VII.]] 19, VIII. 6, 15-16, IX. 21).

In chapters 30-32 (of ch. ed.) of Vanaparva there is a dialogue between [[Draupadi|Draupadī]] and [[Yudhisthira,|Yudhiṣṭhira,]] who, having lost his kingdom in gambling with the Kauravas, was in [[axile|exile]] and hard pressed. [[Draupadi|Draupadī]] wonders how such a straightforward, mild, benevolent and truthful prince allowed his mind to be involved in gambling (30. 19), that God does not act towards beings like a father or mother, but He appears to be irascible like a common man, when she sees that honourable [[mon|men]] of high character [[bave|have]] difficulties in maintaining themselves and [[dis. honourable|dishonourable]] men live in pleasures (30. 38-39); she proceeds ‘human beings,60 ignorant and having no control over [[happi ness|happiness]] and misery, go to heaven or hell at the caprice of God.’ [[Yudhisthira|Yudhiṣṭhira]] warns her that she talks like an atheist, that he performed no deed with the thought of seeking the reward thereof, that [[be|he]] made gifts and performed sacrifices because he held that it was his duty to do so.’ He asked her to give up [[atheistio|atheistic]] talk and not to disrespect Highest God. Then [[Draupadi|Draupadī]] came round and replied that she did not mean to [[dis respect|disrespect]] or censure Dharma, that it is far from her thoughts to show disrespect to God, but that, being distressed, she talked like an atheist. She then enters upon a discussion61 about [[P1570]] what people mean by [[dieta|diṣṭa]] (fate) or [[hacha|haṭha]] (chance) or nature ([[svabhāra|svabhāva]]) and winds up by saying that whatever a person gains is all the result of deeds in past lives.

It is not necessary to say here anything about human effort (puruṣakāra) and [[daire.|daiva.]] That topic has been dealt with and the different views of ancient and medieval writers about the [[rospective|respective]] spheres of [[thege|these]] two have been pointed out in H. of Dh. vol. [[III,|III.]] pp. 168-170 and notes [[214-216,|214-216.]]

When the son of [[Gautami,|Gautamī,]] who was an old woman that had attained control of mind, died by [[snake-bito,|snake-bite,]] a [[bunter|hunter]] caught the [[anake,|snake,]] brought it bound to [[Gautami|Gautamī]] and said that he would kill the snake for having bitten an innocent and unoffending boy. [[Gautami|Gautamī]] [[dissuados|dissuades]] him from killing the snake by stating [[“by|‘by]] killing the snake my son would not be brought back to life, I see no good in killing the snake.’ Then Kāla came forward and explained [[Just|‘Just]] as a potter moulds from a lump of clay whatever he desires, so man secures fruits of deeds done by himself; the boy’s death was due to his deeds in a former life’ and [[Gautami|Gautamī]] agreed that her son died by his own deeds in a previous life and that her bereavement by the death of her son was due to her own former [[deeds,|deeds.]]62 This story occurs in the very first chapter of the Anuśāsanaparva.

[[Draupadi|Draupadī]] who had to work as [[Sairandhri|Sairandhrī]] (a maid servant) in the harem of [[Virāta|Virāṭa]] bewails before [[Bhima|Bhīma]] ‘in my girlhood I must have done something which displeased the Creator by virtue of which I have been reduced to this distressing [[con dition.’|condition.’]]63 The [[Anusasanaparva|Anuśāsanaparva]] states ‘just as the calf finds [[P1571]] its own mother from among thousands of cows, so karma done in a previous life pursues the doer of it.’ The [[Asyamedhika. |Āśvamedhika]]parva64 [[asaerts|asserts]] that there is no destruction of good and evil [[actions :|actions;]] they [[ripon|ripen]] and produce results when the self passes from one body to another. The [[Sāntiparva|Śāntiparva]] (cr. ed. [[316,|316.]] 25 and 35 = ch. ed. 329. 25, 35) [[avers’a|avers ‘a]] man by performing good deeds secures the state of a god, he is reborn as a human being by actions of a mixed character (good and evil both) and he is born as lower (than man) by evil deeds; when you (yourself) start after death no one will follow you; only your good or evil deeds will follow you when you leave this world.’

All strata of Hindu society were permeated by the theory of Karma and [[trangmigration.|transmigration.]] Great Sanskrit poets often allude to it. In the [[Raghuvamsa|Raghuvaṁśa]] XI. 22 [[desoribing|describing]] the visit of Rāma to the hermitage of Vāmana pointed out by Viśvāmitra, Kālidāsa remarks [[“Rāma|‘Rāma]] became restless (or disturbed) in mind, though not remembering his actions in the former existence as [[Vamana’|Vāmana’]] (both Vāmana and Rāma being avatāras of Viṣṇu). Similarly, in [[Sakuntala|Śākuntala]] (Act V) the poet remarks ‘when on seeing charming sights and hearing pleasing words, [[&|a]] person, though surrounded by pleasures, becomes restless (or sorrowful), that is indeed due to this that his mind unconsciously has the impressions of loves and friendships of past [[liyon|lives]] presented to it’. In the seventh Act of the same play when Duṣyanta and [[Śakuntala|Śakuntalā]] were re-united, she, referring to her previous rejection by the king, remarks65 ‘indeed at that time some (bad) actions of mine (in some former life) hampered my good deeds and became ripe for fruition’. In the [[Raghuvamsa|Raghuvaṁśa]] the 14th canto contains at first a message of spirited protest by [[Sita|Sītā]] whom Rāma ordered Lakṣmaṇa to abandon near [[Valmiki’s|Vālmīki’s]] hermitage on account of the scandal about her among common people, but afterwards qualifies the message by saying that the terrible misfortune that she was going to suffer was the fruition of her misdeeds in former existences and that she desired to be his wife in a future existence but without separation from him. Vide also the Meghadūta [[verge|verse]] quoted above in note 2533 p. 1563.

[[P1572]] Many questions naturally arise on the doctrine of Karma and transmigration. One of them is discussed by the bhāṣya of Vyāsa on Yogasūtra II. 13. [[in|In]] Yogasūtra II. 3 five [[kledas|kleśas]] (hindrances) such as [[aridyā|avidyā]] (nescience) are enumerated and it is stated (II. 13 ) that these kleśas lead on to fruition of actions by way of birth, life ( long or short ), kind of experience. Actions (Karma) are of four sorts according to Yogasūtra (IV. 7) viz. [[krsna|kṛṣṇa]] (dark found in wicked people), (2) [[sukluktsna (white dark)|śuklakṛṣṇa (white-dark)]] that are accomplished by external means in which there is some injury to or benefit of others; (3) śukla (white) belonging to those who engage in [[tupur, gyadhyāya|tapas, svādhyāya]] ( study of the sacred texts) and contemplation; because this kind of deed depends on the mind alone and does not depend on external means and does not involve injury to others; (4) [[Asuklūktsna|Aśuklākṛṣṇa]] (neither white [[por|nor]] dark), which is found in [[sanngāsins|sannyāsins]] (ascetic saints) whose hindrances (avidyā &c) have dwindled and whose bodies are the last (they will have). Of these four kinds, the yogin alone has non-white karma since he has renounced the fruit of all actions (even of good ones) and he has [[akrsna|akṛṣṇa]] since he will never resort to dark actions. The bhāṣya on [[Yogasutra II, 13|Yogasūtra II. 13]] states four questions, (1) whether one Karma is the cause of one birth, or [[(?)|(2)]] whether one Karma is the cause of more births than [[one;-(3)|one; (3)]] whether more than one Karma brings about more births than one; (4) whether more than one Karma brings about one birth. The bhāṣya raises objections to the first three and states that the fourth alternative alone is acceptable. Vide p. 1417 note 2325 for the text of the [[Yogabbāṣya|Yogabhāṣya]] on these four alternatives. The [[Sāntiparva|Śāntiparva]] (chap. 273. 33–34 of [[or, ed.|cr. ed.]] =chap 280. 33–34 of [[ch.ed.)|ch. ed.)]] mentions individual souls as of six colours viz. [[krsu|kṛṣṇa]] (dark), [[dhūmru/grey)|dhūmra (grey)]], [[nila|nīla]] (blue), [[rulta|rakta]] (red), [[haririta|hāridra]] (yellow) and śukla (white) in an ascending order, the dark being the lowest and [[sukla|śukla]] being the highest and verses 36-46 [[desoribe|describe]] these six in detail.

There are several features of our present life that can be more satisfactorily explained on the theory of transmigration than on any other. Why do two people who have seen but little of one another feel friendship or hatred? The surmise that in past existences they were friends or had obliged each other or harmed each other offers an explanation. The doctrine of karma and transmigration accounts for the apparently unmerited misery and suffering of many people, while some enjoy undeserved happiness or a good life. Our [[Senge|sense]] of fairness and justice would [[P1573]] be shocked by the inequalities in the world, if such a doctrine were not there. The hypothesis and belief that all human volition and conduct will suitably be rewarded and punished in subsequent existences has an important bearing on present conduct, would act as an urge to continual effort for [[good ness|goodness]] in this life and is likely to deter men from vice and cruelty. This doctrine of karma not only offers an [[explana tion|explanation]] about the varying degrees of happiness and unhappiness among human beings, but also accounts for differences in material well-being and unhealthy bodily conditions. It offers a solution of the problem of evil in the world and explains precocious abilities in mathematics, music and arts among children and grown up men as in the case of Ramanujan about whom Prof. Hardy of Cambridge (in ‘Ramanujan,’ Cambridge, 1940 ) says that he was the most romantic figure in the recent mathematics. If rightly understood, it is not pessimistic or fatalist, but rather emphasizes all out human effort in this life. It will be seen how many of the works on Dharmaśāstra or connected with it as sources emphasize effort ( puruṣakāra ) as against views of various people that it is Daiva or Svabhāva or time or the combination of all these that yield rewards or retribution in this life. It also explains sudden accession of prosperity or [[bigh|high]] position such as, for example, a poor man’s son being adopted by a prince or queen and then becoming an enlightened and famous ruler as was the case with the late Sayajirao Maharaj Gaikwad of Baroda.

Even from [[Upaniṣadio|Upaniṣadic]] times various views were held about the origin of the world and similar questions. The [[śv.|Śv.]] Up. (I. 1) asks the question ‘Is brahma the cause? Whence [[ar:|are]] we born? Whereby do we live and whither are we [[going.|going?]] Tell us, O knowers of brahman, under whose control do we abide in pain or pleasure’? The next verse states ‘Should Time or nature or necessity or chance or the elements be considered as the cause or He who is (called) Puruṣa ? It cannot be their combination either, since even the self has no power over [[happi ness|happiness]] and misery.’ Then [[verge|verse]] 3 (latter half) states ‘He being one superintends (or controls) all causes viz. Time, self and the rest.’ [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] I. 350 refers to five views on the question as to what causes desirable or undesirable results viz. some hold that it is Daiva alone, some put forward Svabhāva (nature), some rely on Time, [[sone|some]] on human effort and some on a combination of all these; but, Yājñavalkya’s own view (I. 349, 351) is that [[P1574]] good or bad results are due to [[duira|daiva]] and puruṣakāra and that the former is nothing but effort of former existences that has begun to manifest results. The [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]] (chap. 238. 4-5=[[or, ed.|cr. ed.]] 230. 4-5 ) refers to the fact that there are three views viz. either human effort or [[daira|daiva]] or [[suabhūra|svabhāva]] being the cause, but its own view appears to be that [[Purusakāra|Puruṣakāra]] and Daiva combine to produce fruits. The [[Matsyapurana (221, 8)|Matsyapurāṇa (221. 8)]] asserts that [[daira|daiva]], effort and kāla working together produce the fruits of actions. The [[Brahmaṇḍaparana (. 8. 61-62)|Brahmāṇḍapurāṇa (II. 8. 61-62)]] refers to three [[viows|views]] viz. Daiva, effort and Svabhāva are put forward as causes separately but its own view is that daiva and effort together yield fruits.

It may be stated here that Karma is put in three groups viz. [[sancta|sañcita]], [[prūrabdha|prārabdha]] and kriyamāṇa (or [[sañolyamāna|sañcīyamāna]]). The first is the total accumulated deeds of all past existences, the fruits of which have not been experienced. The prārabdha Karma is that which was the strongest among the group of [[sancita|sañcita]] deeds just before the present existence of a person begins and which is supposed to determine one’s present existence. What a person accumulates during the present existence is called [[kriyamāna|kriyamāṇa]] (or sañcīyamāna, being collected ) and the next existence is determined by the strongest (or the earliest according to some ) among the [[sancita|sañcita]] and [[kriyamāna|kriyamāṇa]] deeds put together. As the karmans66 are of different characters and yield different consequences ( heaven in case of sāttvika deeds, or the earth or mid-regions when they are mainly rājasa ), and places of tortures when deeds are mainly tāmasa the existences (or births or bodies ) differ and the self being affected by the body, the individual souls appear as different. The objection is raised that freedom of will is the basis of all ethical values and that if a man’s Karma in past lives determines the present life, then a man in the present life is [[morely|merely]] a toy in the power of Karma and has no power to do as he sees best. The question about a man’s freedom of will is a most thorny one, on [[wbich|which]] the greatest thinkers from ancient times to the present day have differed67 and no [[P1575]] satisfactory reply can be given. There are numerous works on discussions on Free will and Determinism such as Rashdall’s [[* Theory|‘Theory]] of good and evil’ vol. II. pp. 302-355 (1907) on [[Free|‘Free]] Will,’ [[Bergsons’s Time|Bergson’s ‘Time]] and Free will,’ Viscount Samuel in [[* Belief|‘Belief]] and [[action.|action’]] pp. 303-320; but the present author does not propose to recommend any of these books to the reader of this volume. But if the reader wants a small book on the Free Will controversy he may read the book by M. Davidson (London, 1942). So far as the Indian doctrine of Karma is concerned, it appears that freedom of will is postulated for a man during the present existence to lead a good moral life and perform meritorious acts subject to the limitations caused by the environment in which the present existence is cast. The important working belief is that one has free will and that one is free in the present existence to mould the future ( [[80|so]] far as he is concerned ) by means of meritorious works. This is the message of the [[Santiparva.|Śāntiparva.]]68 Lord [[Kșspa|Kṛṣṇa]], after a long discourse in the [[Bhagavadgita|Bhagavadgītā]] gives Arjuna permission to do as the latter likes ([[18.63’ yathecohasi|18.63 ‘yathecchasi]] tathā kuru’). In [[Gitā|Gītā]] 9.30 also Lord [[Krṣpa|Kṛṣṇa]] says [[if|‘if]] even a person of the vilest conduct worships me with undivided devotion, he must be held to be righteous, for he has made a right resolve.’ Similarly, in VI. 5 the [[Gitā|Gītā]] provides [[let|‘let]] a man lift himself [[bigher|higher]] by himself, let him not degrade himself; for the self alone is the friend of the self and the self alone is the enemy of the self’. Vide notes below for Upaniṣad passages, V. S. and [[Śhaṅkarabhāsya|Śaṅkarabhāṣya]]. It is possible to accept, on the ancient Indian doctrine, both predestination and free will, the first so far as one’s being born in a certain environment is concerned and the 2nd so far as one’s actions in the present life are concerned. The [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] (VI. 5-6 ) holds out the hope even to a sinner that it is never too late to mend and [[(II,|(II.]] 40 ) avers that even a little of [[righteousnegg|righteousness]] saves a man from great fear and no effort is lost.

Though the general drift of the [[Gitā|Gītā]] is in favour of Free Will, there are a few passages that seem to [[bavour|savour]] of determinism, [[e.g.’|e.g. ‘]] everyone is made to do acts helplessly by the guṇas born of Prakṛti ( III. 5); ‘all beings follow [[praksti|prakṛti]] (Nature); what can control (or repression) do’? (III. 33); [[*|”]] due to your conceit you think ‘I shall not fight,’ this thy resolve is vain; (your) Nature will compel you to do it ; you being constrained [[P1576]] by your own acts born of your nature will helplessly do that which you do not wish to [[do”(XVIII.|do” (XVIII.]] 59-60). It may have to be conceded that there is no freedom of will in the matter of environment during childhood.

The Rāmāyaṇa gives expression to the belief that sorrow in the present life is the result of similar actions done in past life or lives. When Rāma left Ayodhyā as an exile for the sake of fulfilling the promise [[giren|given]] to queen [[Kaikey!|Kaikeyī]] by king [[Dasaratha|Daśaratha]], [[Kausalya|Kausalyā]], mother of Rāma, laments ‘I believe indeed that in a past life I must have made many persons lose their sons or I must have harmed (or killed) living beings; it is therefore that this ( sorrow) has befallen me’; ‘I think beyond doubt that in a former life, I, a wretched woman, must have cut off the breasts of cows (or mothers) when their calves (or children) desired (to drink milk at the breasts) of their mothers’.69

The Purāṇas also emphasize the importance of good or evil deeds and say that one has to reap the fruits of one’s actions, whether good or evil, and Karma does not come to an end even after hundreds of lives unless the results thereof are undergone.70 The Padmapurāṇa [[states|states ‘]]there is no destruction of Karma except by reaping the fruits thereof; no one can set aside the bondage due to the Karma of past lives’ and [[further|further ‘]]man by his own actions may become a God, or a human being, cattle, a bird or a lower animal or even a tree (or rock); no man in this world is able to annul the effects of actions done in previous existences by his power or by the birth of [[progeny ‘|progeny’]]71 (son &c). The [[P1577]] idea of transmigration as [[desoribed|described]] in the [[Upaniṣada|Upaniṣads]] had become universal in India at the time of Buddha. Buddha rejected the reality of a permanent ego or self as an entity. He was not a metaphysical philosopher, but wanted to lay down a way or method by which mankind could be freed from ignorance and suffering and liberation could be achieved. Therefore, while rejecting a permanent ego he accepted the doctrine of rebirth.

A side issue has been very much debated by some eminent scholars, viz. whether the cherishers of the Vedānta thoughts were originally the Kṣatriyas and not the Brāhmaṇas. The [[prosent|present]] author dealt with that question in a brief compass in H. of Dh. vol. II. pp. 105-107 and note 222. [[Deusion|Deussen]] in [[‘Das|‘Das]] System des Vedānta’ (1883 pp. 18-19) and Ph. Up. (tr. by Geden pp. 18-19) and Dr. R. G. Bhandarkar in [[“Vaiṣṇavism|‘Vaiṣṇavism]] and [[Saiviam|Śaivism]] ’ p. 9 hold the view that the [[ksatriyas|kṣatriyas]] were the original [[Possesseors|possessors]] of Vedānta doctrines. Deussen relies mainly on six passages and Dr. Bhandarkar on two only (Chān. V. 3 and 11) and besides Deussen (in Ph. Up. p. 19) admits that his [[conclu sion|conclusion]] is not absolute certainty but has a high degree of probability. Barth in ‘Religions of India’ p. 65, Hopkins in [[* Ethics|‘Ethics]] of India’ (1924) p. 63, [[Vedic|‘Vedic]] India’ by Macdonell and Keith (vol. II, p. 206), and Tuxen on [[The|‘The]] religions of India’ (Copenhagen, 1949, p. 88 ) do not subscribe to this view. Deussen [[wont|went]] so far as to aver (p. 19) [[this|‘this]] teaching with regard to the [[at man|ātman]] was studiously withheld from them (the brāhmaṇas), that it was transmitted in a narrow circle among the [[ksatriyas|kṣatriyas]] to the [[exolusion|exclusion]] of the brāhmaṇas’. The present author had not set out the passages on which reliance was placed by [[Doussen|Deussen]] and those who followed him. They will be set out [[hore|here]] and will be examined as to the context in which they appear and the subject with which they deal. But it must first be emphasized that the most important doctrines of the main and ancient Upaniṣads are two, viz. ’non-difference of the individual self from the Supreme Self, and (2) transmigration of the self depending on his deeds and conduct. Both these doctrines are expounded by [[Yājāavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] to king Janaka in various places in the Upaniṣads e. g. in Bṛ. Up. [[IV,|IV.]] 4. 4-7 and following [[paggages|passages]]. Deussen regards this part of the instruction as deepest, truest and noblest (Ph. Up. [[p. 348:|p. 348;]] quoted above on p. 1547) and this passage is (acc. to him) the leading passage on the [[dootrine|doctrine]] of transmigration and on what accompanies the self in its [[trans migration|transmigration]] (Ph. Up. p. 281). Besides, the words of [[Yajñavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] [[P1578]] in Bṛ. Up. III. [[2. 13.He|2. 13 ‘He]] who does good is born good’ and in IV. 4. [[5’He|5 ‘He]] who does good is born good, he who does evil is born evil, he becomes righteous by righteous works &c.’ are declared by [[Doussen|Deussen]] himself (Ph. Up. p. 410) as ’the oldest in which a doctrine of transmigration is found’. Therefore, according to [[Deusgen|Deussen]] himself, the original possessor of these two fundamental doctrines of the Upaniṣads is the [[brābmana|brāhmaṇa]] [[Yājāavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] who in the same Upaniṣad ([[Bp. Up. II,|Bṛ. Up. II.]] 4. 1-14) propounds to his wife [[Maitreyi|Maitreyī]] the identity of the self and elements and everything else with brahma (idam [[saryam|sarvam]] yadayamātmā). Not only [[30|so]], there are other brāhmaṇa teachers of these. For example, [[Uddalaka Aruṇi|Uddālaka Āruṇi]] expounds at great length to his son [[Svetaketu|Śvetaketu]] the doctrine of identity in the famous words ’tat-tvamasi’ (Chān. Up. VI. 8-16).

Now the examples on which Deussen (Ph. Up. pp. 17-19) bases his [[couclusions|conclusions]] must be cited and examined. Chān. Up. (V. 11. 1) tells the story of five (named) wealthy householders and great students of Veda who came together and discussed the question [[what|‘what]] is our ātman, what is brahma’. They proposed to go to Uddālaka Āruṇi who [[know|knew]] the self called [[Vaisvānara|Vaiśvānara]]. [[Uddalaka|Uddālaka]] thought that he would not be able to explain all and therefore told them that Aśvapati Kaikeya (king of Kekaya country) at that time [[know|knew]] the self called Vaiśvānara and all five together with Uddālaka went to that king, who said he would give an answer the next day. The next day those six went to him with fuel in hand ([[i. 0.|i. e.]] as pupils) and the king without performing further preparatory rites asked each of them what they [[medidated|meditated]] upon. When each replied as to what he meditated upon, such as heaven, [[Aditya|Āditya]], air, ether, water and earth (this last by Uddālaka [[Āruni|Āruṇi]]), he told them that all [[those|these]] are parts of [[Vaisvānara|Vaiśvānara]] and he explained to them how the proper Agnihotra is to be performed.

Two things should be noticed viz. [[Uddalaka|Uddālaka]] Āruṇi [[ia hero|is here]] shown as ignorant of true [[Vaisvānaravidyā|Vaiśvānaravidyā]], though in the very next section (Chān. Up. VI. [[8, 7.|8. 7.]] ff.) [[Uddalaka Aruṇi|Uddālaka Āruṇi]] teaches the sublime doctrine of [[. Tat-tvamasl’.|‘Tat-tvamasi’.]] Probably these two [[Uddalakas|Uddālakas]] are different or the present story is more or less a myth. In the [[Becond|second]] place, all that Aśvapati Kaikeya teaches is about Vaiśvānara and not [[brahmavidya|brahmavidyā]] (about the identity of [[in dividual|individual]] soul with the Supreme Self. From before the times of [[Yaska|Yāska]], there have been various opinions about Vaiśvānara, frequently mentioned in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] (e. g. in I. 52. 6. I. 98. 1.). The Nirukta (VII, 21-23) has a long discussion and quotes [[P1579]] three different views viz. [[tbat Vaisvānara|that Vaiśvānara]] is lightning or Āditya or terrestrial fire. The [[Chan, Up.(V. 18.2)|Chān. Up. (V. 18. 2)]] winds up the description of Vaiśvānara and brings it (V. 19-24) in line with the oblations to five breaths (as ‘Prāṇāya’ [[gvāhā|svāhā]] &c.) and sets forth the results of the observance of Agnihotra with full [[know ledge|knowledge]] of its true purport. The Vedāntasūtra (I. 2. 24–32) deals with what is meant by Vaiśvānara in Chān. V. 11 [[f,|ff.]] and the conclusion is that it means paramātman ( the highest Self) and not individual self or fire as an element or the digestive (stomach) heat.

Deussen then refers to the story of Gārgya Bālāki ( in Bṛ. Up. II. 1) who offered to expound brahma to Ajātaśatru, king of [[Kāsi|Kāśī]], who offered to give a thousand cows if he did [[go|so]] and remarked that people run exclaiming [[Janaka,|‘Janaka,]] Janaka’ ([[mean ing|meaning]] Janaka is donor and also listener to brahma exposition).72 [[Bāiāki|Bālāki]] offered twelve objects of meditation on brahma such as the person in the sun, the person in the moon [[&c,|&c.]] Ajātaśatru replied as to the twelve objects that he knew them already and brahma is different from them and cannot be understood by what he said. Then Bālāki remained silent. Bālāki offered to be his pupil. Then [[Ajātasatru|Ajātaśatru]] said73 " this is topsy-turvy that a brāhmaṇa should approach a [[ksatriya|kṣatriya]] as a pupil with the idea [[the|‘the]] kṣatriya would expound [[Urahnan|brahman]] to [[him,|him.’]] I shall make known to you (brahma).” So saying he took hold of Bālāki’s hand and got up (from his seat ). Certain matters in this story must be carefully noted here. This does not at all say that the class of brāhmaṇas did not know [[brahmavidya|brahmavidyā]] and that [[ksatriyas|kṣatriyas]] alone were then the only possessors of it. On the contrary, Janaka is specially mentioned as a donor of cows and is eager to listen to brahmavidyā and people ran to him desiring to accept large gifts of cows for imparting brahmavidyā. We [[P1580]] know about Janaka from [[Bf.|Bṛ.]] Up. III. 1 ff. wherein Janaka king of Videha, offered a thousand cows and, when Yājñavalkya took away those cows, numerous persons present in Janaka’s court such as his hotṛ priest Aśvala, [[Artabhāga|Ārtabhāga]], [[Gargi|Gārgī]], [[Uddalake|Uddālaka]] Āruṇi, Vidagdha [[Sākalya|Śākalya]] ply him with questions. In [[Bṭ, Up,|Bṛ. Up.]] IV. [[4,7|4. 7]] (Janaka offers a thousand cows to Yājñavalkya, in IV. 4.23 (Janaka offers to give his Videha kingdom to Yājñavalkya along with himself as a slave ). The story of Bālāki only comes to this at the most that when Janaka had learnt brahmavidyā, a [[brābmapa|brāhmaṇa]] Bālāki did not know it though he professed to know it, and had to be instructed in it by king Ajātaśatru of Kāśī, who [[know|knew]] it and who only stated that a [[brāhmana|brāhmaṇa]] did not become a pupil of a [[ksatriya|kṣatriya]]. All [[brāhmṇas|brāhmaṇas]] could never have been proficient in brahmavidyā much less kṣatriyas. One regrets to say that [[Deugsen|Deussen]] is guilty of making a sweeping generalisation without adequate data. It may be noted that in this story Ajātaśatru of Kāśī does not say that the Vidyā was not known to any brāhmaṇas before (as Pravāhaṇa Jaivali claimed ), but, on the contrary, expressed surprise that a brāhmaṇa should come to him for learning the Vidyā.

The [[game|same]] story occurs in Kauṣītaki [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]]74 IV. 1-19 often in the same [[wordsBālāki|words. Bālāki]] offers 16 explanations about the objects of his meditation. The V. S. devotes three sūtras ([[1.|I.]] 4. 16-18) to the passage in which [[Ajātasatru|Ajātaśatru]] stated ‘He who is the maker of those persons ( you mentioned ), he of whom all this is the work, he alone is to be known’. There is in Bṛ. Up. [[II,|II.]] 1 and [[Kausitaki|Kauṣītaki]] Up. IV hardly anything about [[trans migration|transmigration]]. Both passages simply affirm that from the [[Atman|Ātman]] all [[prāpas|prāṇas]], all worlds, all gods, all elements spring forth ([[Br.|Bṛ.]] II. 2. 20 ). This is nothing more than what is stated in the leading text ([[Bṭ.|Bṛ.]] Up. IV. 4. 7) of Yājñavalkya or in Chān. Up. VI. 1-16 in the oft-repeated words of [[Arupi|Āruṇi]] to his son [[Svetaketu|Śvetaketu]] ([[aitadatmyam|aitadātmyam]] idam sarvam…tattvamasi).

It is most surprising that Deussen should press into service the story of Sanatkumāra and Nārada in order to fortify his proposition that [[ksatriyas|kṣatriyas]] were the original possessors of the great doctrines of Vedānta. He relies on Chān. Up. VII, where [[P1581]] it is said that [[Narada|Nārada]] approached Sanatkumāra and prayed [[*Teach|‘Teach]] me, Sir.’ Sanatkumāra said to him ’ tell me what you know; then I shall tell you what is beyond that’. [[Narada|Nārada]] stated (in Chān. Up. VII. 1-2) that he had studied the four Vedas,75 Itihāsa-purāṇa and gave a long list of lores [[includ ing|including]] [[Dovavidyā|Devavidyā]], [[Brabmavidyā|Brahmavidyā]] (whatever that may mean), [[ksatravidyā|kṣatravidyā]], Nakṣatravidyā, admitted he knew mantras only and did not know Ātman (the Self) and added ‘I have heard from men like you that he who knows the [[Atman|Ātman]] overcomes sorrow. I am in sorrow; bhagavan [[I|!]] do help me to cross (to get over) my [[borrow|sorrow]].’ Sanatkumāra replied [[whatever|‘whatever]] you have studied is mere [[Dame|name]], there is something better than a name’. Then Sanatkumāra teaches him to meditate on speech as better than name, then on manas as better than speech and [[Beveral|several]] others as better than the preceding (in VII. 4–24) till [[ho|he]] reaches bhūman (the Infinite, the Paramātman) and describes bhūman in VII. 25–26 ending with the words ‘all this springs from the Self’ and it is stated at the end (in VII. 26. 2) [[*|’]] Bhagavān ([[veporable|venerable]]) Sanatkumāra showed to Nārada, all whose blemishes had been crushed (uprooted), what is beyond darkness ( avidyā); they call him ( Sanatkumāra) [[Skanda.|Skanda.’]]76

In all this long passage there is not a word whether [[Sanat kumāra|Sanatkumāra]] and Nārada belonged to a [[brahmana|brāhmaṇa]] or [[ksatriya|kṣatriya]] class. Skanda is known in classical Sanskrit as the God of War (as in [[Gitā|Gītā]] X. 24 [[‘Senāninām-abam|‘Senānīnām-aham]] Skandaḥ’) and in the [[Vana parva|Vanaparva]] 229. 22–23 he is mentioned as the commander of the armies of gods and the [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]] mentions that Nārada approached Devala for knowledge about the creation and dissolution of the world (chap. 275 = 267 of [[Or, ed.|Cr. ed.]]). From this [[Deusson|Deussen]] at once draws the conclusion that Sanatkumāra must have been a kṣatriya and Nārada a brāhmaṇa. In the Mahābhārata, [[Manu smrti|Manusmṛti]] and the [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] both of them are semi-divine sages and beyond varṇa or caste. [[Gita|Gītā]] X. 13 speaks of [[Narada|Nārada]] as [[Dovarsi|Devarṣi]], the [[Vayu-purāṇa|Vāyu-purāṇa]] speaks of Parvata and Nārada as sons of [[Kasyapa|Kaśyapa]] and as included among [[Devarsis|Devarṣis]] (Vāyu 61. 85). The [[Manusmrti|Manusmṛti]] includes Nārada among the first [[ton|ten]] Prajāpatis [[P1582]] [[(1 25)|(I. 25)]]. The Brahmapurāṇa (I. 46-47) describes both Skanda and Sanatkumāra as sons of [[Brahmā,|Brahmā.]] The [[Nāradiyapurāṇa|Nāradīyapurāṇa]] (Pūrvabhāga, 2. 3) speaks of Sanaka, Sanandana, [[Sabatkumāra|Sanatkumāra]] and Sanātana as the mind-born [[song|sons]] of [[Brahma|Brahmā]] and [[Sanat kumāra|Sanatkumāra]] as brahmavādin and as expounding all Dharmas to Nārada. The Vāmanapurāṇa (60. 68–69) describes the same four as sons of Dharma and [[Ahimsā|Ahiṁsā]], and as [[exponders|expounders]] of [[Yoga. śāstra|Yogaśāstra]]. To crown all, Kūrmapurāṇa77 I. 7. 20-21 speaks of these four along with Kratu as Vipras (brāhmaṇas), yogins, and as mind-born sons of [[Brabmā|Brahmā]]. Sanatkumāra might have been literally or metaphorically called ‘Skanda’, because he attacked and routed [[Audya|Avidyā]] just as God Skanda routed armies of asuras, if at all the myth about Skanda was known in times before the Upaniṣads.

Chān. Up. I. 8 narrates that there were (in some part of India not mentioned ) three persons well versed in (the [[osoterio|esoteric]] meaning of [[Udgitha|Udgītha]] i. e. Om ), viz. [[Silaka|Śilaka]] Śālāvatya, Caikitāyana [[Dalbhya|Dālbhya]] and [[Pravābana|Pravāhaṇa]] Jaivali. They sat down for a discussion on [[Udgitha|Udgītha]]. The first two (who were brāhmaṇas) first put questions to each other and answered. Then [[Pravābana|Pravāhaṇa]] Jaivali told them that they were giving replies about matters that were not permanent. Then [[Pravāhana|Pravāhaṇa]] Jaivali told them that Ākāśa was the origin of this world, that beings owe their origin to Ākāśa and will return into it, that Ākāśa is [[Udgitha|Udgītha]], greater than the great and without [[ond|end]] &c. [[Doussen|Deussen]] relies on this passage also for supporting his theory. [[Udgithavidyā|Udgīthavidyā]] is only one of [[soveral|several]] Upāsanās in the [[Upanisads|Upaniṣads]]. Therefore, what would follow is that [[Pravahana|Pravāhaṇa]] Jaivali [[know|knew]] it and two [[brābmanas|brāhmaṇas]] of some place not mentioned did not know it. It is not possible to see how this story lends any support to Deussen’s sweeping thesis of all brāhmaṇas being originally excluded from the central doctrine of identity. In the same context ([[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. [[I, 9,3|I. 9. 3]]) [[Pravāhapa|Pravāhaṇa]] mentions that one Atidhanvan [[Saunaka|Śaunaka]]78 taught [[Udgitha-vidyā|Udgītha-vidyā]] to [[Udaraśāndilya|Udaraśāṇḍilya]].

[[P1583]] [[Deusgon|Deussen]] without giving any reason [[asserts’|asserts]] ’the names allow of the conjecture that in this case also a brāhmaṇa received instruction from a [[ksatriya ‘(p.|kṣatriya’ (p.]] 18). He probably connected the first name with [[dhanus’|‘dhanus’]] (bow) which was the forte of [[ksatriyas|kṣatriyas]]. But he forgets that [[saunaka|Śaunaka]] and [[Sandilya|Śāṇḍilya]] are both brāhmaṇa names. This will illustrate how even the most profound scholar goes astray when he becomes over-enthusiastic about a theory of his. In this story Pravāhaṇa does not claim that the [[Udgitha vidyā|Udgīthavidyā]] was unknown among brāhmaṇas. On the contrary he himself states that [[Saunaka|Śaunaka]], a brāhmaṇa (called Atidhanvan) expounded that Vidyā to [[Udaraśāṇdilya|Udaraśāṇḍilya]], another [[brābmaṇa,|brāhmaṇa.]] Besides, [[Udgithavidya|Udgīthavidyā]] is only one among numerous upāsanās and what Pravāhaṇa teaches is that all bhūtas spring from [[Akāśa|Ākāśa]] and are absorbed in [[Akaśa|Ākāśa]], the purport being that [[Akāśa|Ākāśa]] here denotes brahman as the V.S. [[(I, 1.22)|(I. 1. 22)]] establishes. This doctrine is the same as the one taught in Tai. Up. III. 6 (basis of V.S. I. 1. 2) and other texts. Moreover, there is nothing about transmigration in this passage of Chān. Up.

The sheet anchor of the views of Deussen and Dr. Bhandarkar are the dialogue between [[Pravāhapa|Pravāhaṇa]] Jaivali and [[Svatakatu|Śvetaketu]] ( [[Bṛ, Up. VI. 2, Chṛn. Up, P.3-10|Bṛ. Up. VI. 2, Chān. Up. V. 3-10]] ) about [[Pañoāgni vidyā|Pañcāgnividyā]] and that between [[Asvapati|Aśvapati]] Kaikeya and [[Uddalaka Āruni|Uddālaka Āruṇi]] about Vaiśvānara (Chān. [[V, 11.24|V. 11. 24]] ). The latter has been already dealt with. In the former [[oocurs|occurs]] an important [[paggage|passage]] which has been made much of and misunderstood. Before proceeding to expound the [[Pancāgnividyā|Pañcāgnividyā]] to [[Svetaketu|Śvetaketu]] and his father [[Aruṇi|Āruṇi]] Gautama, [[Pravāhana|Pravāhaṇa]] Jaivali remarks ( Chān. Up. [[V.3.7)|V. 3. 7)]] [[this|‘this]] vidyā did not go to brāhmaṇas before you; therefore in all worlds domination (or government) has remained with the kṣatriya class alone’. In the corresponding Bṛ. Up. passage the words [[are’|are ‘]]this vidyā did not reside in any [[brāhmana|brāhmaṇa]] before this day, but I shall expound it to you, for, who would like to refuse when you address me thus’79 ( [[vim.|viz.]] I approach you as pupil). In the [[P1584]] Kauṣītaki Up. I. the [[dootrine|doctrine]] of the two paths of [[Devayana|Devayāna]] and Pitṛyāṇa is expounded to [[Aruni|Āruṇi]] (and his son [[Svetaketu|Śvetaketu]]) by Citra [[Gārgyāyapi|Gārgyāyaṇi]] ( [[v. 1.|v. l.]] [[Gangyāyani|Gāṅgyāyani]]), but the remarks about kṣatriyas alone being the first possessors of the doctrine do not occur there at all and [[Gargyayari|Gārgyāyaṇi]] appears to be a [[brābmaṇa|brāhmaṇa]] teacher. The question is: what is meant by [[this|‘this]] vidyā’ in the above passages of [[Chān,|Chān.]] and Bṛ. Upaniṣads? In the Upaniṣads (particularly in Chāndogya and Bṛ.) numerous vidyās for the upāsanā of brahman by men that are not yet far advanced on the path of [[brahmaridya|brahmavidyā]] are dilated upon, such as [[Udgithavidya|Udgīthavidyā]] ([[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. I. 8-9, [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. I. 3), Daharavidyā (Chān. VIII. 1. 1-2, Bṛ. Up. [[1.3|I. 3]], Vedāntasūtra [[1, 3,|I. 3.]] 14-21), Madhuvidyā (Chān. III. 1. 1 ff., Bṛ. Up. II. 5. 1-15), [[Samvargavidyā|Saṁvargavidyā]] ([[Chan.|Chān.]] [[IV.3|IV. 3]]). In the same way [[Pancāgnividya|Pañcāgnividyā]] is an Upāsanā. [[Deusson|Deussen]] and others admit ( as shown above ) that the great and original texts about the identity of the individual self with the Supreme Self and about transmigration of the soul based on works and conduct are those of [[Yājāavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] in the [[Bṛ, Up.|Bṛ. Up.]] The [[Pancāgaividyā|Pañcāgnividyā]] expatiates upon and is concerned mainly with only one aspect of transmigration [[via,|viz.]] the path of those who living in a village practise a life of sacrifices, works of public utility and alms. The five fires and five offerings relate only to the [[pitļyāṇa|pitṛyāṇa]] path. It contains an [[esoterio|esoteric]] and semi-physical explanation of the process by which persons [[oome|come]] to be born again on the earth. At the most it may be argued that some [[ksatriya|kṣatriya]] rulers or [[noble men|noblemen]] claimed to have given an esoteric or metaphorical explanation of the mode of the return of pious [[mon|men]] from the moon to the earth again. Nothing positive is said as to whether [[Pravāhana Jaiyali|Pravāhaṇa Jaivali]] was the ruler of a country or only a kṣatriya (rājanya in Bṛ. Up. VI. [[2.3|2. 3]], [[Chin.|Chān.]] Up. [[V. 3,5|V. 3. 5]] ) but we are told that [[Asyapati|Aśvapati]] was king of the Kekaya country in the extreme northwest of India, while the original proclaimer of the [[immorta. lity|immortality]] of the self and its identity with the supreme self was [[Yajna valkya|Yājñavalkya]] who was in Videha (Mithilā, modern Bihar), which was at least a thousand miles away from Kekaya. [[Yājāavelkya’s philo sophy|Yājñavalkya’s philosophy]] must have taken a long time before it became well known in the distant Kekaya. Conceding for argument that a few rulers like Aśvapati were the first to propound an explanation of the [[P1585]] way of the transmigration of pious sacrificers and the like, it is [[&|a]] far cry from that to assume as Deussen does (Ph. Up. p. 20 ) that the doctrine of the Ātman as the first [[Prinoiple|Principle]] of [[tbe|the]] universe was fostered and progressively developed by the kṣatriyas in opposition to the principles of the brahmanical ritual and this assumption is contrary to what he himself states elsewhere in his own work ( pp. 410, 281, 348 ).

Before proceeding further a passage from the [[Bṭ.|Bṛ.]] Up. I. 4. 10 and another from the Ait. Up. (II. 4-5) would have to be considered. In [[Bs.|Bṛ.]] Up. occurs the following passage :80 “In the beginning brahman was there, it cognised itself as ‘I am brahma’; therefore it became all; whoever from among the Gods perceived this (viz. ‘I am brahman’), he became that (brahman); the [[game|same]] occurred to sages and [[mon|men]]. The sage [[Vāmadova|Vāmadeva]] realizing this reached the conclusion ‘I was (or became) Manu and the Sun also.[’|’] Therefore, this ( will [[ocour|occur]]) even now. Whoever ([[oven|even]] a man) who realizes ‘I am brahman he becomes (identical [[with|with)]] all this (universe); even the gods are not able to prevent that man’s being one with brahman and with all, for he is the soul of all these.” Deussen translates [[aham|‘aham]] [[Manurabhayam Būryaśca’|Manurabhavam Sūryaśca’]] as ‘I was once Manu, I was once the Sun’. There is no word for ‘once’ and it is supplied by Deussen who remarks [[‘88|‘as]] a proof of his knowledge of brahman, (Vāmadeva) alleged his acquaintance with his former births as Manu and Sūrya’ (p. 317). The words can very well be construed as meaning that [[Vanadeva|Vāmadeva]], having realized [[P1586]] brahman when he sang the hymn [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] IV. 26, expressed his identity with Manu and [[Surya|Sūrya]] (and the whole universe ). There is no express reference to former births and [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] in81 his [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on Bṛ. Up. understands the passage in this sense and does not refer to former births. It cannot be said that the Bṛ. Up. quotes [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] IV. 26. 1 for supporting the [[concep tion|conception]] of transmigration but it relies on it merely for the [[concep tion|conception]] of identity of the individual soul with the Supreme Self.

Then there is a passage in the [[Ait, Up.|Ait. Up.]] which Deussen refers to (pp. 317–318 of Ph. Up.). In the 2nd adhyāya that [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] refers to the conception of a person as the first birth; when the child is actually born that is described as the 2nd birth. Then it proceeds “He (the son) being the self of the father is placed in his (the father’s ) stead for the performance of all sacred works; then his other self (the father) having performed all he has to do and having reached the span of his life (having become very old ) departs (from this world) and departing from this world is born again, that is his third birth. And this has been declared by a sage ‘while dwelling in (mother’s ) womb I came to know all the births of the gods; a hundred strongholds of ayas (copper or iron) held me, but I [[98caped|escaped]] down with speed like a [[hawk,|hawk.’]] [[Vāmadova|Vāmadeva]] lying in his mother’s womb declared this”. In this passage the father and son are treated as identical, as elsewhere it is so said (vide note below). This [[Rgveda Verse|Ṛgveda verse]] (the 2nd half) is liable to several interpretations. In the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] (I. [[80, %,|80. 2,]] VIII. 95. 3, IX. 68. 6, X. 11. 4 &c.) it is frequently stated that [[(syena)|(śyena)]] hawk brings Soma for Indra. [[Syena|Śyena]] may also be interpreted as standing for the soul and the iron fortresses as the bodies through which the transmigrating soul wanders. The [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] sage might have only meant the mythical hawk of Indra. But as the word ‘janimāni’ occurs in the first half the [[Upaniṣadio|Upaniṣadic]] sage (in Ait. Up.) might have used the [[verge|verse]] to support his ideas about the three births. But even in the Ait. Up. passage the three births are not clearly of one person, unless we resort to a myth, viz. the conception of a son and the son’s birth are [[P1587]] taken as the two births of the father himself on the ground that the son is the father himself born again.82

The Upaniṣads laid down an inexorable law that fruits of all actions, good or evil, must be experienced and that a person’s deeds and conduct determined the character of his succeeding existences. But from [[gome|some]] passages in the Upaniṣads themselves it appears that they recognised some exceptions. One exception was that when a [[pesron|person]] realized that he was one with brahman the actions both good and evil, if any, done by him after that Realization and before the physical death of the body produced [[20|no]] results. In Chān. Up. VI. 14. 3 Satyakāma83 Jābāla tells his pupil Upakosala [[that’Just|that ‘Just]] as water does not cling to a lotus leaf, so no evil deed clings to one who knows (realizes) brahman’; [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. V. 24. 3 ‘just as the (soft) cottonlike fibres of the [[iṣikā|iṣīkā]] reed when cast into fire are burnt out, so all evil deeds of him who knowing (the purport of) Vaiśvānara (brahma) [[offors|offers]] Agnihotra are burnt’. The Bṛ. [[Up,|Up.]] states ‘Him who knows these two do not overwhelm, whether he says that he did evil for some reason or that he did a good deed for some reason; he overcomes both these; neither what he has done nor what he has omitted to do afflict ([[lit,|lit.]] heat or burn) him’. The [[Mupdaka|Muṇḍaka]] Up. provides ‘when a person has seen (realized) the Highest (cause ) and lowest (effect) his actions perish.’ But this is only true as regards all acts before he attained Realization and all acts that his body may do after his attainment of Realization but he cannot rescind the prārabdha karma which brought about that existence wherein he reached Realization of brahman. The idea is that the actions which led to a person’s present embodiment must all be consumed by the body persisting till the appointed period for its death, and then only he becomes free from the [[P1588]] physical body. The [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. states84 [[89 toaman|as to a man]] who has attained true knowledge of the Supreme Self from a teacher that ‘for him there is only delay so long as he is not delivered (from the body ), then he will become [[perfeot’|perfect’]].’ All these [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] passages are relied upon in V. S. IV. 1. 13-15 and [[Śhaṅkaracārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] explains their purpose concisely but very clearly. The [[Gitā|Gītā]] also says (IV. 37) that the fire of knowledge reduces to ashes all deeds. Here all means only [[Sancita|Sañcita]] and Sañcīyamāna and excludes prārabdha-karma. About the acts during the period between the attainment of [[Vidya|Vidyā]] and the fall of the body [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] gives the instance of an arrow shot from a bow, which stops only after the initial impetus is exhausted in the flight of the arrow. Some works say that when the meritorious deed or the sinful deed done in the present life is of the extremest kind it may yield results in this very life.85

The Upaniṣad theory is that one must bear the consequences of all actions, good or evil. But sometimes an evil action is done without any previous thought, as, for example, when a man’s gun goes off by accident and somebody is killed or seriously injured. This led to a discussion in Dharmasūtras and Smṛtis and the doctrine of [[prāyasoittas|prāyaścittas]] (expiations) for sins was developed. From Vedic times some rites had been performed to counteract mishaps and irregularities in the course of religious [[Ceremonies|ceremonies]] and for portentous phenomena or personal misfortunes such as a dog-bite. In these the idea was [[P1589]] of ceremonial purity and the averting of evil results from such phenomena in which no question of sin as ordinarily understood entered. In the Gautama Dharmasūtra there is a discussion on this, which is probably the earliest clear exposition on [[expia tions|expiations]] for sins. Gautama states that there were two views on the efficacy of expiations against sins. One view was that expiations should not be performed as regards sins, because a sin is not destroyed unless its consequences are experienced ; the other view was that ‘one should perform expiations since there are Vedic passages indicative of this, such as ‘after performing the sacrifice called [[Pupaḥstoma|Punaḥstoma]] one can come back ( i. e. become fit for) Soma sacrifice ([[i e.|i. e.]] for all Vedic rites in general)’; ‘after performing the Vrātyastoma’ (one becomes fit for vedic [[saorifices|sacrifices]] ), ‘he who offers the [[Asvamedha|Aśvamedha]] sacrifice crosses beyond all sin, beyond (even)86 brāhmaṇa murder.’ Some held the view that only sins committed inadvertently were removed by expiations;87 while others held the view that expiations were efficacious even against sins committed [[wil fully|wilfully]] as there are Vedic indications to that effect (Manu XI. 45). Manu, however, appears to have held that a man is relieved of the lapses inadvertently committed by means of the study of the Veda, but sins wilfully committed can be removed only by various prescribed expiations (XI. 46) and that a man must always resort to expiations for sins, since those who have not done so have to undergo a double retribution [[viz,|viz.]] the tortures of horrible hells for long terms of years and also being born in other human existences with bodily deformities and diseases (Manu [[XI|XI.]] 48 and [[XII,|XII.]] 54). The subject of expiations and of Karmavipāka (fruition of evil actions by being born as a worm or lower animal, or having a short life and premature death and undergoing torments of Hell) has been dealt with at length in H. of Dh. Vol. IV. pp. 1–178).

[[P1590]] So far as rebirth for sins is concerned attention may be drawn here to the following authorities : [[Manusmști|Manusmṛti]] XII. 54-69, [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] III. 131, 135–136, 207-215, [[Viṣṇndharmasūtra|Viṣṇudharmasūtra]], chap. 44, [[Atrismrti 4,|Atrismṛti 4.]] 5-14, 17-44, [[Mārkandeye-purāṇa|Mārkaṇḍeya-purāṇa]] 15. 1-41 (B. I. [[od.|ed.]] ), Brahmapurāṇa 217. 37–110, Garuḍapurāṇa, [[Pretakānda|Pretakāṇḍa]], chap 2. 60-88 (which incorporate almost word for word [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] III. 206-215) and also quotations from [[Saṅkha|Śaṅkha]] Dharmasūtra collected by the present author in ABORI. vol. VIII, pp. 116-117 [[itens|items]] 375-378 [[oulled|culled]] from the [[Mitakṣarā|Mitākṣarā]] on [[Yāj. UI,|Yāj. III.]] 216, the Madanapārijāta pp. 701-702, the Parāśara-[[Mādhaviya|Mādhavīya]], vol. II, part 2 pp. 246, 259, 263, 269. For reasons of space it is not possible to set out this whole mass but a few illustrative [[pass. ages|passages]] will be cited.

Manu states ( XII, 54-69, with which Yāj. [[111.|III.]] 206-208 and 212-215 agree in many places) ’those guilty of mortal sins (Mahāpātakas), having gone through terrible hells during a large numbers of years, come after the expiration (of hell punishment) to the following births. The slayer of a Brāhmaṇa passes through the births of dog, pig, ass, camel, cow (or ox ), goat, sheep, deer, a bird, a [[cīṇdāla|cāṇḍāla]] and a pukkasa; a [[brābınapa|brāhmaṇa]] who drinks the liquor called Surā enters the bodies of worms and (large ) insects, of moths (or locusts ), of birds feeding on ordure and of carnivorous animals; a brāhmaṇa ( who steals gold of a brāhmaṇa) shall pass a thousand times through the births of spiders, snakes, lizards, aquatic animals and of destructive [[gobling|goblins]]; the violator of his guru’s bed passes [[&|a]] hundred times through (the form of) grasses, shrubs, creepers, carnivorous animals and of beasts with fangs and of those (animals) like tigers doing cruel acts. Those men who are given to injuring others become animals that eat raw flesh, those who eat forbidden food become worms, those that thieve become creatures [[consum jag|consuming]] their own kind (such as fishes) and those who have [[inter course|intercourse]] with women of the lowest classes become pretas (ghostly spirits); one who has associated with outcasts (for certain specified periods), he who approaches the wives of other men and he who has deprived a brāhmaṇa of his property (other than gold) becomes [[Brahmarakṣasa|Brahmarākṣasa]] (an evil demon); a man who has on account of greed stolen gems, pearls or coral or any of the other kinds of precious stones is born among goldsmiths; for stealing grain a brāhmaṇa becomes a rat, for stealing bell-metal a man becomes a [[hamsa|haṁsa]] bird, for depriving another of water he becomes the bird called Plava, for stealing honey a stinging [[P1591]] insect, for stealing milk a cow, for stealing sweet juice ([[sugar-Oane|sugar-cane]] &c) a dog, for stealing [[olarified|clarified]] butter an ichneumon; for stealing meat [[&|a]] vulture, for stealing fat a cormorant, for stealing oil a bird called Tailapaka, for stealing salt a cricket, for stealing curds a bird called Balākā (crane); for stealing silk a partridge, for stealing linen a frog; for stealing cloth of cotton threads [[Kraunca|Krauñca]] bird, for stealing [[&|a]] cow an iguana, for stealing molasses a bird called vāgguda (a bat?), for stealing fine perfume a musk-rat, for stealing vegetables consisting of leaves a peacock, for [[‘stealing|stealing]] cooked food of various kinds a porcupine and for stealing uncooked food a hedgehog; for stealing fire a person becomes a heron, for stealing household utensils a mason-wasp, for stealing dyed garments [[&|a]] cakora bird; for stealing a deer or an elephant a wolf, for stealing a horse a tiger, for stealing fruits and roots a monkey, for stealing a woman a bear, for stealing drinking water a [[cūtaka|cātaka]], for stealing vehicles a camel, for stealing cattle a [[ho-goat|he-goat]]; that man who has forcibly taken away any kind of property belonging to another or who eats sacrificial food of which no part has been offered in sacrifice certainly becomes [[&|a]] lower animal; women also who in like manner committed theft shall incur guilt; they become the females of those same creatures ( enumerated above).

It may be noted that some of the births [[designed|assigned]] to those guilty of thefts of various articles have some logic or reason behind those regulations e. g. when Manu XII, 62 and [[Yāj. 11. 214|Yāj. III. 214]] prescribe that a thief of grains becomes a rat in the next birth or when Manu XII. 61 prescribes that a thief of jewels, pearls and coral is born among the class of goldsmiths, one can easily appreciate this retribution as appropriate or reasonable, but the same cannot be said of all others. It is noteworthy that, in spite of what the bhāṣya on Y. S. quoted in note 2325 and pp. 1417-18 above says, a single mortal sin [[lika|like]] [[brahmana-murder|brāhmaṇa-murder]] might make the perpetrator [[pags|pass]] through several vile births, [[ag|as]] stated by Manu in XII. 55-58, [[Yaj. III,|Yāj. III.]] 207–208 and by [[Saṅkarācārya.|Śaṅkarācārya.]]88

Once the strict rule of the original doctrine of Karma in the Upaniṣads was loosened by the theory of [[prayaścittas|prāyaścittas]], a [[P1592]] quick pace was set even in early times for the removal or reduction of the effects of sins in other ways than by means of expiations. Gautama89 [[proscribes|prescribes]] five means for redeeming (or expiating) blamable acts viz. inaudible recitation of the Veda, austerities, sacrifice in fire, fasting, giving gifts. The history of Dharmaśāstra vol. IV deals at some length with [[japu|japa]] (pp. 44-51 ), tapas (pp. 42-43), homa (pp. 43-44), [[dana|dāna]] [[(pp. 51-52) •|(pp. 51-52)]] and fasting (pp. [[52-51).|52-54).]] Therefore, it is not necessary to say here anything about them. But attention must be drawn to some special and far-reaching modifications of these and other means. [[Sūdras|Śūdras]] and members of pratiloma castes were not authorized to study the Veda. Hence the authors of medieval works, particularly of [[Purāṇas,’|Purāṇas,]] went so far as to say that remembrance90 of the name of [[Kșspa|Kṛṣṇa]] is superior to all expiations and all forms of austerities and that if a man only remembers Nārāyaṇa in the morning, noon, evening, at night and other [[timnes,|times,]] he at once secures the destruction of (the consequences of) sins. Other means were also provided for the removal of sins, one being pilgrimage to holy places. Vide H. of Dh. vol. IV. pp. 55-56 and 552-580. Another was the practice of [[prāpāyāma|prāṇāyāma]] which has been dealt with on p. 42 of H. [[Dh,|Dh.]] vol. IV.

Even in the earliest times open confession of sin was in certain cases regarded as freeing the person from sin. In the Cāturmāsya called [[Varupa-pragbāsa,|Varuṇa-praghāsa,]] the wife who took part with her husband had to confess expressly or by some indirect ways whether she had at some time a paramour and if she did [[80|so]] she was purified and could take part in the [[sacrad|sacred]] rites [[there. after.|thereafter.]] Vide H. of Dh. Vol. [[II,|II.]] pp. 575-576 and 1098 for this. Similarly, according to [[Ap,|Āp.]] Dh. [[8.|S.]] I. 9. [[24,|24.]] 15, [[1.|I.]] 10. 28. 19 and [[1, 10.29.|I. 10. 29.]] 1 it was necessary [[wben|when]] undergoing expiations for forsaking one’s wife or for [[brahmana|brāhmaṇa]] murder to confess one’s sin. So also the brahmacārin, if guilty of sexual intercourse, [[P1593]] had to proclaim his lapse while begging at seven houses (vide Gautama and Manu quoted below).91

Repentance ([[anutāpu|anutāpa]]). Manu (XII. 227 and 230 ) provides that a sinner is relieved of the consequences of sin by [[proolaim ing|proclaiming]] his sin to people, by repentance, by austerities, by recitation of Vedic texts, by gifts in case of distress ([[i. o.|i. e.]] if he is unable to undergo tapas); a man after [[commiting|committing]] a sin becomes free from it by feeling remorse and if he makes a resolve ‘I shall never again do so’ he is purified. The Viṣṇupurāṇa provides that, when [[afer|after]] committing a sin the man feels remorse, the one high expiation that he has then to undergo is the remembrance of [[Hari|Hari.]]92 MacNicol, a Scottish Missionary, wrote [[&|a]] work (in 1915) on ‘Indian theism’ in which he boldly asserts, after referring to the [[Upanisadic|Upaniṣadic]] doctrine of Karma that [[’there|there]] is no place for repentance in the Hindu doctrine of Karma’ (p. 223). It has been shown above that confession was known in Vedic times and that repentance was valued in Dharmaśāstra works either as removing [[sing|sins]] or as making the repentant man fit for receiving expiatory rites (vide H. of Dh. [[VoL|vol.]] IV. pp. 41-42). The worthy missionary author probably thinks his Christian brand of remission of sins by confession and repentance better than the Upaniṣadic teaching. Ancient Indian authors were not prepared to accept mere confession and repentance as enough to absolve a man of all [[sing.|sins.]] The Biblical position that if one believes in Christ and his gospel, confesses his sins and says he repents (as required in Matthew 4. 17, Mark I. 15 and Luke 24. 47 ) is very likely to engender among common men a complacent attitude about sins. This complacent frame of mind, the ancient Dharmaśāstra writers wanted to prevent and therefore, they insisted upon a good deal more than repentance and confession of sins. They blended the old doctrine of [[con fession|confession]] and repentance with retribution viz. hell tortures and vile births. Later Paurāṇik writers approached very close to the probable attitude of common Christians that one has simply to [[ - believe|believe]] in Christ as a redeemer, to confess and to take the name of God for being absolved from all sins. Macnicol either did not [[P1594]] read the relevant texts from [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] or [[be|he]] forgot them when he made the assertion noted above.

Long before MacNicol wrote and since his days numerous Western scholars brought up in Christian [[Burroundings|surroundings]] have expressed their view that the ancient Indian doctrine as regards man’s destiny after death is far more preferable to Biblical ideas on the same subject. It is enough to refer to [[oply|only]] two or three [[authors,|authors.]] Arberry in his [[“Asiatic Jones’|‘Asiatic Jones’]] quotes (p. 37) from Sir William Jones’ letter to Earl Spencer the following passage ‘I am no Hindu, but I hold the doctrine of the Hindus concerning a future state to be incomparably more rational, more pious and more likely to deter [[mon|men]] from vice, than the horrid opinions inculcated by Christians on punishments without end’. Lowes Dickinson in ‘Religion and Immortality’ (Dent and Sons, 1911) observes (on p. 74) ‘it is really a consoling idea that our present capacities are determined by our previous actions and that our present actions again will determine our future character’. Owen Rutter, author of [[’the|‘The]] scales of Karma’ (London, 1925) says that Christianity has failed to solve the intellectual and moral [[problemas|problems]] which beset those who live in the complexities of the modern world, that he began to study the doctrine of Karma and reincarnation seven years before he wrote the book, which is a personal statement rather than an essay on Karma (pp. 12–13). Many who have written against this doctrine, while conceding that the [[Upanisadic|Upaniṣadic]] doctrine is an ancient and serious attempt to solve the [[problema|problem]] of injustice and evil in the world, call it a weak one and beset with difficulties. One should like to ask, what systems of religion and philosophy are not beset with difficulties? One may take the Christian doctrines for example. To all non-Christians (and to [[soveral|several]] Christians also in modern times) [[ita|its]] doctrine of original [[gin,|sin,]] its damnation of unbaptized infants, of predestination deduced from the belief that God is an Omniscient and Omnipotent Creator of heaven and earth appear strange and unjustified. L. T. Hobhouse in ‘Morals in Evolution’ part II (1906) pp. 130 ff points out how all systems with a personal God, particularly Christianity, are beset with difficulties, such as making the problem of evil urgent, eternal punishment for [[&|a]] great sinner in one life suggestive of a moral impossibility, the inescapable conclusion that all men, however good, that did not or could not or do not believe in Christ and are not members of the Christian Church by baptism are irrevocably doomed. That [[P1595]] Christianity is unique and that Christians are God’s chosen people are beliefs which would make God appear quite unjust and therefore some Christian writers like Prof. Toynbee in [[Christianity|‘Christianity]] among the religions of the world’ (Oxford [[Un,|Un.]] Press, 1958) have begun to urge that Christianity must be purged of such beliefs (pp. 13 and 95 ).

The strict doctrine of Karma would require that there can be no transfer of good or bad karma from one man to another and a man cannot suffer for the sins of others. But in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] there are allusions to the belief that God might inflict [[punish ment|punishment]] on the sons for the sins of their fathers. For example, in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VII. 86. 5 [[Vasistha|Vasiṣṭha]]93 prays to [[Varupa|Varuṇa]] “cast [[awuy|away]] from us the transgressions of our fathers, and those that we committed in our own person’; [[*May|‘May]] we not have to suffer for the sin committed by another, may we not do that for which you punish (this is addressed to [[Vißve-devāḥ).|Viśve-devāḥ).]] The [[Santiparva,|Śāntiparva,]] however, asserts ‘whatever deed a man does in four ways viz. with eye, with thought, speech or action, he receives (in return) that same kind of action; a man does not enjoy (i. e. experience the results of) the good deeds or evil deeds of another; man attains (a result) in consonance with the actions done by himself ‘.94 Similarly, in the [[Gṭdhra-gomāyu-sarvāda,|Gṛdhra-gomāyu-saṁvāda,]] it is asserted ’the son does not have to go by the path due to the actions of the father, nor vice versa, they being bound by their own respective good or evil deeds proceed each by a different path’. Whatever deed a man does, whether holy or very unholy (terrible), the consequences of that are experienced by the [[door|doer]] alone, what have the relatives to do with that’ ([[Santiparva 153,|Śāntiparva 153.]] 38 and 41=cr. ed. 149. 34 and 37).

Modifications of this doctrine were early introduced. The Gaut.95 [[Dk. 8.|Dh. S.]] provides that the king should guard all the [[P1596]] varṇas and [[asramas|āśramas]] according to Śāstra and if they [[gwerve|swerve]] from their duties he should make them follow their duties, since he gets a share of the dharma (merit) observed by them. Manu provides (VIII. 304-305, 308) that the king who protects his subjects secures the sixth part of the (spiritual) merit of all (his subjects), but if he does not guard them he also shares in the sixth part of their adharma (sin); the king who protects well is entitled to the sixth part of the merit of the Veda study, the sacrifices, the gifts, and of the worship of gods done by all subjects ; that king who takes as his one-sixth part of the crops and other products, but fails to guard the subjects is declared (by sages) as receiving all the sins of all his subjects’. Manu says elsewhere (in IX. 301) that the king is the Yuga, that his actions may bring in [[Kr̥ta|Kṛta]] or any of the other Yugas. Kālidāsa in [[Sakuntala|Śākuntala]] echoes this [[idea|idea.]]96 Manu (in VIII. 316) provides that when a thief comes to a king, confesses his guilt and asks the king to punish him with a heavy staff or a sharp weapon and the king either punishes him or lets him off, the thief is freed from (the effects of) the sin, but the king incurs the guilt of the thief if he does not punish him. To the same effect is [[Vasistha|Vasiṣṭha]] 19. 46 and 20. 41. It was stated by Manu (III. 100 ) that all the merit of even a man who lives a plain life by subsisting on grains left in the field after the crops are garnered and who performs homa in the five fires (to be kept by an householder) is taken away by a brāhmaṇa guest who stays unhonoured by him. [[Santiparya,|Śāntiparva,]] Viṣṇu Dh. S. and several [[Parānas|Purāṇas]] provide that97 when a guest, being disappointed (in getting food), turns away from the house of a person, the guest goes away taking all the merit of the person and transfers to him all his own misdeeds. This was probably not meant to be taken literally. It is only an arthavāda ( in the language of the [[Mimāmsā)|Mīmāṁsā)]] and [[morely|merely]] exhorts a householder to [[bonour|honour]] a guest properly. The exhortation addressed by the judge to a witness before the latter began to depose contains in Yāj. the following [[P1597]] verse98 : ‘whatever good deeds you performed in hundreds of [[existencos,|existences,]] all that will go to that party whose defeat you will bring about by your [[falso|false]] evidence.’ The Mit. and [[Aparārke|Aparārka]] both say that this is meant only to frighten and quote a [[verge|verse]] of Nārada ([[rpādāna|ṛṇādāna]] 200) in support ‘he (the judge) should frighten them (from telling lies) by citing [[anoient|ancient]] [[dharma Sāstra|dharmaśāstra]] texts, by [[rocounting|recounting]] the greatness of truth and by [[censur ing falsehood.’)|censuring falsehood.’]] Manu (VIII. 90), however, makes the [[exhorta tion|exhortation]] run as follows ‘Whatever good deeds you performed from [[pour|your]] birth, the merit of all of them would go to dogs if you will [[depoze|depose]] falsely’. Manu XII. 81 says99 ‘with whatever [[dis position|disposition]] of mind (either sāttvika or [[rūjasa|rājasa]] or tāmasa ) a man [[dnes|does]] an act he reaps the fruits thereof (in a future life ) in a body endowed with the same character.’

The [[Bhagavadgitā,|Bhagavadgītā,]] apart from the fact that knowledge of reality destroyed the effects of all deeds, emphasized at the end the path of singleminded devotion (bhakti) to God and [[surrender. ing|surrendering]] all one’s acts and their fruits to Him ‘giving up all the various paths, come to me alone as your refuge; don’t grieve, I shall release you from the consequences of all evil deeds, if any, of yours’.100

As regards husband and wife, the Dharmaśāstra works say a good deal but all that is said should not be taken literally.
