For example, Manu (V. 164-166 ) states101 ‘by playing false to her husband ([[i. 8.|i. e.]] by adultery ) the wife is [[consured|censured]] in this world, she becomes ( after death) a female jackal and is tormented by evil diseases (such as leprosy). That woman controlling herself in thought, word and deed, who does not play false to her [[P1598]] husband, resides in the same world (heaven) as her husband, and is called a virtuous wife. A woman restrained in thought, word and deed, secures by such conduct (as is laid down for her) in this life highest renown and [[rosidence|residence]] with her husband in the next world’. For [[byperbolical|hyperbolical]] descriptions of the power of a chaste wife ([[patirratā|pativratā]]) in the great epic and Purāṇas, vide H. of Dh. vol. II. pp. 567-568. A verse cited there may be repeated [[here|here ‘]]just as a snake-charmer forcibly draws out from a hole [[& snako,|a snake,]] so a chaste wife snatches away her husband’s life from the messengers of death and reaches heaven with her husband’. This is also an arthavāda but it probably reflected the popular ideas of those times.

The Mahābhārata has certain interesting verses on the transference of the effects of karma to one’s descendants. The Ādiparva states ‘if the consequences of sin are not [[soon|seen]] affecting the perpetrator, they will surely be seen in the sons or grandsons’. This again is an arthavāda.102

It was believed that punishment by the king for a sinful deed (like a theft &c.) liquidated the consequences of the sin, made the offender pure and enabled him to reach heaven as men of good deeds do.103

It is difficult to reconcile the doctrine of Karma and [[punar janma|punarjanma]] with the system of Śrāddhas in which balls of rice are offered to the three paternal ancestors of the performer of śrāddha. This subject has been dealt with in H. of Dh. vol. IV. pp. 335-339. The offering of balls of rice to the spirits of the departed male ancestors was in vogue in the times of the Veda probably and even before the Vedas and the theory of Karma and Punar-janma arose later and as people were not prepared to give up the theory of śrāddhas, they kept both.

The popular idea of the last thought at one’s death leading to a future birth appropriate to that thought in spite of a life of sin and iniquity is an interference with the working of the law of Karma and transmigration. This notion has been dealt with [[P1599]] above (pp. 972-973) where it has been pointed out that that notion is not supported by the [[Bhagavadgita|Bhagavadgītā]] ([[VII.|VIII.]] 5–7).

Apart from the Upaniṣads (and commentaries thereon), the Vedāntasūtras (and the [[bhāgyas|bhāṣyas]] thereon) and the [[Bhagavad gitā,|Bhagavadgītā,]] there are very few regular treatises on the doctrine of Karma and [[re-incarnation,|re-incarnation.]] One work comparatively early in date is the [[Vijñānadipikā|Vijñānadīpikā]] of Padmapāda (said to be the same as the favourite pupil of the first [[Saṅkarācārya)|Śaṅkarācārya)]] in 71 [[vorges|verses]] (edited with a commentary and Introduction by M. M. Dr. Umesha Mishra, Allahabad, 1940) which deal with the means of achieving mokṣa by realizing the identity of the individual self and the Supreme Self (they being like the reflection and its original) and by annihilating karma. Some of the important [[Verges|verses]] in it have been adduced above in the notes. That work (verse 9)104 compares [[sanolyamāna-karma|sañcīyamāna-karma]] to grain standing in the field, [[sancita|sañcita]] to grain stored in one’s house and [[prārabdha. karma|prārabdha-karma]] to food put in one’s stomach. The food put in one’s stomach is exhausted by its being digested which takes some time. Therefore, Karma of the [[sancita|sañcita]] and [[sañolyamāna|sañcīyamāna]] types is annihilated by correct knowledge, while prārabdha karma by undergoing its results for some time. It emphasizes that it is Vairāgya (extreme non-attachment to objects of sense) that leads to the rise of correct knowledge of Reality, to the [[elimina tion|elimination]] of vāsanās (unconsciously working impressions), to the annihilation of Karma and to [[cossation|cessation]] of rebirth.

Another work is the [[Janma-maranavicāra|Janma-maraṇavicāra]] of [[Bhatta|Bhaṭṭa]] Vāmadeva (published in the Kashmir Series of Texts and [[Studios|Studies]] vol. 19, 1918 A. D.), It is a small work in 25 pages.105 It appears to belong to the Kashmir Śaiva cult. It states that Śiva has three [[Saktis,|Śaktis,]] [[Cit-sakti|Cit-śakti]] (which is of the nature of light or consciousness ), Svātantrya (free will, independence) and [[P1600]] Ānandaśakti (bliss). There are six [[kiñenkus|kañcukas]] ([[slioaths,|sheaths,]] covers) viz. Māyā, Kalā, [[Śuddha-vidya,|Śuddha-vidyā,]] [[Rāga.|Rāga,]] Kāla and Niyantraṇā. On p. 15 it says that when the bodily machine is broken, consciousness, taking hold of [[prūnana|prāṇana]] (breathing), is carried to another body by the [[Ativāhika|Ātivāhika]] (subtle body ).106 This latter body becomes like a vehicle between the body that lies dead and the next [[pbysical|physical]] body. The work [[thon|then]] quotes from a [[Kośabbāsya|Kośabhāṣya]] three verses which support the theory of the [[antara bhavadeha|antarābhavadeha]] (the intermediate body between the dead physical body and the future physical body) and also relies on [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 85. 16.107 It also quotes [[Bhagavadgitā.|Bhagavadgītā]] 14. 14-15 (yadā sattve pravṛddhe tu &c) and says that those bodily states are referred to in such Vedic passages as [[‘Pancare|‘Pañcāre]] cakre &c’([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 164. 13), in [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] [[1.6|I. 6]] (sasyam-iva [[martyah|martyaḥ]] pacyate), in [[‘Vāsiinsi jirpāni’|‘Vāsāṁsi jīrṇāni’]] ([[Gita|Gītā]] II. 22). He further says that by God’s grace a man becomes pure and by [[dikṣit|dīkṣā]] and other means he understands his real character and reaches Śiva. It states that [[all,nen|all men]] do not attain liberation, while those who hate ( discard) [[likṣā,|dīkṣā,]] temples and correct knowledge go to hell. There is very little discussion about the kinds of Karma and the removal of the effects of these.

There is another rather modern work called [[Prārabdha duvānta-samhști|Prārabdha-dhvānta-saṁhṛti]] (destruction of the darkness [[i, e.|i. e.]] ignorance about [[prorabdha)|prārabdha)]] composed in [[śaku|śaka]] 1741 (1819 A. D.) at [[Pañcavaṭi|Pañcavaṭī]] near Nasik by [[Acgutaraya Modaka,|Acyutarāya Moḍaka,]] a learned and prolific writer. The only known ms. is in the Mysore Government [[P1601]] Oriental Library and is in [[Kannada|Kannaḍa]] script. The present author got a [[Devanagari|Devanāgarī]] transcript of the Ms. through the kindness of the authorities of the Library.

Dr. H. G. Narahari (in [[N. I A. VOL.|N. I. A. vol.]] V. pp. 115–118) furnishes an account of the ms., its date and a brief description of its contents. In another paper the same scholar gives a tolerably full analysis of its contents ([[¡in|in]] J. G. J. R. [[I,|I.]] vol. III. for [[1945.46,|1945-46,]] pp. 349-367 ).

The author Acyutarāya states that the work really [[meang *Prārabdha-pada-dhyāntasamhrti’|means ‘Prārabdha-vāda-dhvāntasaṁhṛti’]] ( destruction of the [[darknees|darkness]] caused by the doctrine of prārabdha) and that all that he wants is to combat the false idea that all human acts from the moment of conception to death are governed by past deeds alone to the exclusion of everything [[else,|else.]] He postulates that all human activity is due to prārabdha, to [[sainskāras|saṁskāras]] (sub-conscious or latent impressions or tendencies) and prayatna (human effort). He explains : the moment after the body dies, all the accumulated good and bad deeds set in motion by God become ready to yield their retribution (results ) and that a good or evil deed (or both ) that is strongest begins a suitable body. The birth is that of a [[brahmapa|brāhmaṇa]] or the rest when there is mixed good and evil as the strongest karma, as a lower animal when sin is strongest, [[&s &|as a]] godly birth when a good [[dood|deed]] is strongest. The duration of life may be 100 years, less or more, and bhoga means experiencing pleasure or pain that is regarded as favourable or [[unfavoura ble.|unfavourable.]]108 Then he launches on the discussion of pleasure (sukha) as threefold, viz. illusory ([[pratibhasika),|prātibhāsika),]] actual ([[vyāyahārika)|vyāvahārika)]] [[of|or]] actual due to illusory pleasure. Pleasure is again ramya (beautiful) and priya ( dear or liked). These two are not [[syno nymous,|synonymous,]] since gold would appear beautiful to an ascetic but it is not dear to him. Then he divides what is priya into three kinds (or degrees). He divides each of the three kinds of pleasure into three varieties, which are passed over here. The preceding are classifications of pleasure that is ‘Laukikakārya’ (ordinary or common); but there are also other sukhas, viz. vaidika [[(fourfold|[fourfold]] such as [[pratikopāsanā,|pratīkopāsanā,]] [[ahārya|āhārya]] (assumed) and vāsanātmaka [[P1602]] (as in sleep)]. This last he subdivides into three kinds ([[sattvika,|sāttvika,]] [[rajasa|rājasa]] and [[tamasa)|tāmasa)]] that are passed over. Pain (duḥkha) is divisible in the same way as sukha and the author illustrates the different kinds of pain, that are left out here. Then the author dilates upon the cause or causes of pleasure and pain (sukha and duḥkha) that constitute man’s experience on earth, which causes are prārabdha (past karma that has begun to operate and determines the body the soul secures), [[sanskara|saṁskāra]] and prayatna.109 He states that the bliss that a man feels in meditation ([[samadhi)|samādhi)]] is due to prārabdha, the pleasure he feels in deep sleep is due to [[sarskāras|saṁskāras]]110 and the pleasure on the fall of rain after the performance of the [[Kārlrt|Kārīrī]] (sacrifice) is due to human [[effort|effort.]] From the [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] [[passager|passages]] it follows (says Acyutarāya) that in deep sleep there is no desire, [[nokarma|no karma]] and no fear and so the pleasure (expressed on waking in the [[.words ‘sukham ahamasvapsam’|words ‘sukham-ahamasvāpsam’]] (I had a happy sleep) is not due to prārabdha but to [[samakāras.|saṁskāras.]] He relies on the [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up.111 (IV. 4. 2) for holding this view (i.e. he [[taken *pūryaprajñā’|takes ‘pūrvaprajñā’]] in that [[paggage|passage]] as equal to vāsanā (latent [[impres Bions|impressions]] or reminiscences of past experiences). The activities of a person in past lives may be madhya112 (middling or moderate), [[P1603]] manda (inferior), [[tiyra|tīvra]] (formidable). A good deed which is three parts [[sāttvike|sāttvika]] and one part [[rājaga|rājasa]] is called madhya; a deed which is half sāttvika and half rājasa is [[tivta.|tīvra.]] Then Acyutarāya refers to a conflict of views. Following the words ‘puṇyo vai puṇyena karmaṇā bhavati pāpaḥ pāpena’ (Bṛ. Up. III. 3. 13 quoted in note 2510 above), some writers held that it is only Karma that is the cause of a new birth and that [[vidya|vidyā]] (knowledge of all kinds) and pūrvaprajñā have nothing to do with it, while others relying on [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. IV.4.2 (to which [[refør ence|reference]] is made in the [[Saṅksepaśāriraka’III,|Saṅkṣepaśārīraka III.]] 18) held that [[Vidya,|Vidyā,]] Karma and [[Puryaprajñā|Pūrvaprajñā]] have all to do with the assumption of a new body by the soul. Achyutarāya agrees with this latter view. He divides and subdivides Karma, vidyā and [[pūrva. prajñā,|pūrvaprajñā,]] so that ultimately the threefold apparatus for giving a new body comes to 96 varieties and remarks that these divisions are not of much use.113 To the objection that all this refutation of [[prārabdhavada|prārabdhavāda]] is idle and serves no purpose he replies: men are of three sorts, mukta (liberated), [[mumuksu|mumukṣu]] (desirous of liberation) and viṣayin (immersed in enjoying the objects of sense). As regards the first the [[Chan.|Chān.]] Up. [[VI, 14. %|VI. 14. 2]] says ‘for him there is delay so long as he is not delivered (from the body), then he will be [[perfect,|perfect’.]]114 The [[game|same]] is relied upon in V. S. IV. 1. 19. The idea being that until prārabdhakarma that brought about the body in which Realisation was attained is done away with by the death of the body, the self is [[jivanmukta,|jīvanmukta,]] but his other karma that has not begun to yield fruit is done away with by [[Realisation,|Realisation.]] To the same effect is [[Bhagavadgita.|Bhagavadgītā.]]

Acyutarāya quotes thirteen verses from Vārtikasāra about [[Vāgana|Vāsanā]] (pūrvaprajñā), Karma and Vidyā (pp. 31-33). [[H.|He]] [[P1604]] quotes the [[JIvanmuktiviveka|Jīvanmuktiviveka]] on the three means of [[jivanmukti|jīvanmukti]] viz. [[tattvajītāna|tattvajñāna]] (knowledge of the Reality as enunciated in [[“Tattvamasi’|‘Tat-tvamasi’]] Chān. Up. VI. 8. 7, [[‘Alam brahmasmi’|‘Aham brahmāsmi’]] [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. [[I,|I.]] 4. 10), [[manonāśu|manonāśa]] (disappearance of the activities of the mind) and Vāsanākṣaya (destruction of the instincts of anger [[&o).|&c).]] Then he gives a lengthy disquisition on the condemnation of prārabdhavāda and the superiority of effort from the [[Vāsiṣtha Rāmāyapa.|Vāsiṣṭha-Rāmāyaṇa.]] About [[mumuksu|mumukṣu]] [[Acyutaraya|Acyutarāya]] advises going to a guru as laid down in [[Muṇdakopaniṣad|Muṇḍakopaniṣad]] I. 2. 12 (tadvijñānārtham sa [[gurumovābhigacchet|gurumevābhigacchet]] &c).

The present author has read either wholly or partially the following modern works, mostly written by Western scholars on Karma and rebirth, that either offer remarks for or against that [[dootrine.|doctrine.]] The space at the author’s disposal precludes any detailed statement about the objections raised against this doctrine in some of them. But a few important objections will be stated and will be briefly dealt with. The works read or consulted are: “Philosophy of the Upanishads” by Paul Deussen, translated by A. S. Geden (1906) pp. 313-338; J. R. [[A.S.|A. S.]] for 1906 pp. 586-593 and for 1907 pp. 665–72 on modifications of the doctrine of Karma by E. W. Hopkins; ‘Transmigration of souls’ by Alfred Berthelot, translated by H. J. Chaytor (Harper, London, [[1909);’|1909); ‘]]Karma and redemption’ by A. G. Hogg ([[od.|ed.]] of 1910); ‘Religion and immortality’ by G. Lowes Dickinson (Dent, 1911); ‘Re-incarnation’ by E.D. Walker (Rider and Co., London, 1913); this book contains extracts from Western authors (22 in number) on re-incarnation (pp. 63–124); there is also a long chapter (pp. 127-191) of extracts from Western Poetry on the same subject; he also deals with evidences of the doctrine in the Bible and early Christendom and a Bibliography on pp. 329-343; [[* Fate|‘Fate]] and free will’ by Ardesar Sorabji N. Wadia (Dent and Sons, London 1915). He states the problem to be [[whether|‘whether]] fate by itself influences and directs the course of human [[evolu tion|evolution]] [[of|or]] freewill by itself or both fate and freewill working conjointly lay down the lines along which human progress must lie’. He states that it must be the one or the other and that it can never be both (p. 180). He himself believes in a fixed and determinist future; [[‘Immortality’eggays|‘Immortality’ essays]] on, by [[B.H. Stroeter|B. H. Streeter]] and others (Macmillan & Co. 1917); pp. 293-317 deal with [[re-incarna tion|re-incarnation]] and Karma by Miss Lily Dougall and pp. 317-341 deal with modern Theosophy which adopts the doctrines of re-incarnation and Karma ; ‘Religion and philosophy of the Veda and [[Upew nishads’|Upanishads’]] by A. B. Keith, vol. II. pp. 570-584; [[“Karma|‘Karma]] and [[P1605]] [[re incarnation’|re-incarnation’]] by Paul Yevtic (Luzac & Co., London, 1927 ); L’Ame et le Dogme de la Transmigration dans les Livres Sacres de L’Inde ancienne’ by [[Erio|Eric]] de Henseler, Paris, 1928; ‘Some dogmas of Religion’ by [[MoTaggart|McTaggart]] with Introduction by [[O.|C.]] D. Broad (London, 1930 pp. 77–111 on ‘Human Immortality’), pp. 112–139 on human pre-existence, pp. 140-185 on Free Will ; [[“Vicissitudes|‘Vicissitudes]] of karma doctrine’ by Prof. H. D. Bhattacharya in Malaviya Commemoration Volume (1932) pp. 491–526; ‘Reincarnation for every man’ by Shaw Desmond (Rider & Co. London). This work mentions in chap. VIII European great men that believed in this doctrine; ‘The [[goales|scales]] of karma’ by Owen Rutter (London). This book contains numerous references to Greek and Roman writers and to modern English and German poets and [[philosophors|philosophers]] from whose works extracts are [[oited|cited]] for showing that they believed in Karma and reincarnation such as Shakespeare, Hume, Goethe, Wordsworth ; ‘Indian [[Philosophy|Philosophy’]] by [[Dr.8.|Dr. S.]] Radhakrishnan (1941) pp. 244-249; ‘The circle of life’ by Kenneth Walker (Jonathan Cape, London, 1942) chap. VIII and IX pp. 81-104; ‘Karma and [[re-birth”|re-birth’]] by Christmas Humphreys (London, 1944); in chap. VII. pp. 62-66 he tries to show that Schopenhauer, Tennyson, Browning, Rossetti and John Masefield, the English Poet Laureate, believe in this doctrine; ‘The problem of re-birth’ by Sri Aurobindo (Pondicherry, 1952); [[•The|‘The]] Brahmasūtras’ by Dr. [[8.|S.]] Radhakrishnan (1959), pp. 194–207 of the Introduction; [[“The|‘The]] dogma of re-incarnation’ by J. [[E,|E.]] Sanjana (New Book Co., Bombay, 1954) pp. 1-134 with an Appendix on the rationale of the dogma of rebirth, pp. 137– 157. This is one of the largest and latest works on [[’re-incarna tion’|’re-incarnation’]] that the present author read. Mr. Sanjana writes bitterly and sometimes in offensive language about those from whom he differs. He asserts that he is a true Zoroastrian and that ‘it can be said without any exaggeration and with the most [[porfect|perfect]] reason and justice that a man who believes in re-incarnation is not a true Zoroastrian’ (p. 125). None [[Deed|need]] quarrel with his first belief, but the latter part of the sentence invites serious consideration. He appears to aver that he has [[&|a]] monopoly of the true doctrines of Zoroastrianism and like the Roman Church and the Popes, that held that the true religion was only in their keeping and that all others (even those who believed in the Bible and Christ) were damned, holds that Zoroastrians believing in [[ro-incarnation|re-incarnation]] are apostates. I shall cite only one or two Parsi scholars who hold just the opposite view. Mr. R. F. Gorvala contributed [[&|a]] paper on the “Immortal Soul, its pre-existence, [[P1606]] persistence after death and transmigration” to the Spiegel [[Oom memoration|Commemoration]] Volume edited by no less [[&|a]] Parsi scholar than Dr. J. J. Modi (Bombay, 1908) pp. 99-124, in which he states (p. 124) ’that the Hindu doctrine of the transmigration of the soul influenced the faith of the ancient Zoroastrians in all times. Indeed the struggle between Good and Evil could not end in the triumph of the former if but one brief life was the only time given for the fight.’ Vide [[algo|also]] ‘Philosophy of [[Zoroastrianism|Zoroastrianism’]] by [[Farodun|Faredun]] [[K,|K.]] Dadachanji (Bombay, Times of India Press, 1941) pp. 108–133, particularly pp. 116 ff. Mr. Sanjana seems to have been much upset by the [[faot|fact]] that some Parsis, [[wbo|who]] were scholars of Avesta and Sanskrit, became Theosophists and admirers of Mrs. Besant and other famous personages in the Theosophist movement. Nearly half the main book of 129 pages is [[con cerned|concerned]] with the criticisms (sometimes extremely offensive ) of the doings and writings of Madame Blavatsky (pp. 28-42), Mrs. Besant (pp. 43–51), C. W. Leadbeater (pp. 57-59), A. P. Sinnett and others (like Col. Olcott and Mr. K. A. [[K,|K.]] Iyer, author of [[Vedanta),|‘Vedanta),]] W.Q. Judge and a Gujarati book of an unnamed scholar (pp. 59–73). On pp. 82-88 he deals with [[MoTaggart,|McTaggart,]] [[G, L,|G. L.]] Dickinson and W. [[Lutogowski|Lutosowski]] (Polish writer who claims that he remembers his many past lives). Mr. Sanjana returns (pp. 89–103) to the charge against the Theosophists and the storms [[aud|and]] [[sruptions|eruptions]] in the Theosophical society and [[considera|considers]] some works on Sufism [[alao|also]] (pp. 106-115). He emphasises the good points of Zoroastrianism as certified by W. B. Henning, J. W. Waterhouse and Hopkins. He does not devote much thought to the objections, more or less insuperable, against all monotheistic religions with a personal God in such works as Hobhouse’s ‘Morals in Evolution’ part 2, though in the appendix (on p. 140) he has to admit that no [[satisfaotory|satisfactory]] answer [[oan|can]] be given to the question why an omnipotent and omniscient God should allow Evil to exist. He contrasts Zoroaster’s religion and philosophy with [[Hinduiem|Hinduism]] and Buddhism and winds up with an article by Winternitz (pp. 119-121) and then ends on an illuminating personal note in which he confesses (p. 126) that he in his youth was particularly attracted by the doctrine of re-incarnation eloquently propounded by Mrs. [[Begant,|Besant,]] but later he became sceptical about what was said by Mrs. [[Besant|Besant.]] Neophytes generally indulge in opprobrious language for the cult once ardently professed by them and later abjured and Mr. [[Banjana|Sanjana]] seems to be no exception to this. I may mention here only one or two instances of his language against [[Theosophista;|Theosophists;]] [[P1607]] on p. 64 he speaks of Blavatsky, [[Mrg.|Mrs.]] Besant, Sinnett and Leadbeater as “proved charlatans” and on p. 95 he writes about Mrs. Besant’s entry into [[Politios|Politics]] as “[[those|these]] later metamorphoses of the megalomaniac Mrs. Besant”. It appears to the present writer that his study of original Sanskrit works is not deep and that he commits serious mistakes in the small portion of his work that he devotes to the ancient Indian view of the doctrine of re-incarnation. His wrong interpretation of Manu VI. 63 (p. 10 of his book) has been pointed out (on p. 1565). Some more cases of mistakes may be cited here. On p. 11 of his book he relies on Thibaut for the statement that there is nothing in the sūtras (the [[Vedānta-būtras)|Vedānta-sūtras)]] to warrant the main doctrine of [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] about two Vidyās, the higher (parā) and the lower (aparā), which respectively lead to the higher (para ) brahma and the lower (apara ) [[brahme.|brahma.]] If he had carefully read only a few of the eleven principal Upaniṣads (the [[Iśāvāsya,|Īśāvāsya,]] Kena, Kaṭha, [[Mundaka,|Muṇḍaka,]] Praśna) he would have found for himself that Thibaut is not a trustworthy guide in all cases. The [[Mundaka|Muṇḍaka]] Upaniṣad (I. 1. 4-5) speaks of two vidyās, [[para|parā]] and aparā ‘dve vidye [[veditavyo|veditavye]] [[para|parā]] [[oa,|ca,]] aparā ca, atha parā yayā tadakṣaram adhigamyate’ and this [[Mundaka|Muṇḍaka]] text is treated as the basis of Vedāntasūtra [[1.|I.]] 2. 21-23 by [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] and Rāmānuja. The Praśna Upaniṣad [[%|2]] states both para and apara brahma ’etadvai Satyakāma param [[cāparama 0a|cāparam ca]] brahma yad-omkāraḥ”. On pp. 21, 121, 146 he alleges that the words “[[yadaharava|yadahareva]] virajet [[tadaharova|tadahareva]] [[pravzajet”|pravrajet”]] (a man should become a [[sapnyasin|sannyāsin]] the very day on which he feels disgust for this worldly life) are a dictum or saying of Śaṅkara. But this text is taken from the [[Jābālopanigad|Jābālopaniṣad]] 4. The whole passage of the Jābālopaniṣad 4 on the [[asramas|āśramas]] is quoted by [[Saökara|Śaṅkara]] on Vedāntasūtra III. 4. 20 and Śaṅkara calls it [[Jabalasruti,|Jābālaśruti.]] On p. 22 Mr. Sanjana quotes [[Bhaga vadgita|Bhagavadgītā]] (IX. 32, ‘mām hi pārtha [[vyapāṣritya|vyapāśritya]] yepi [[syu)|syuḥ]] [[pāps yodayaḥ,|pāpayonayaḥ,]] striyo [[vaiśyās-tatha|vaiśyās-tathā]] [[sudrās-tepi|śudrās-tepi]] yānti parām gatim) and holds that women, [[Taisyas|vaiśyas]] and śūdras are declared to be included under [[“pāpayonaya'|‘pāpayonayaḥ’]] (of evil or [[ginful|sinful]] birth). This is quite wrong. A [[Vaibya|Vaiśya]] from very ancient times belonged to the three higher classes and in the [[Chandogya|Chāndogya]] Upaniṣad V. [[10.7,|10. 7,]] it is expressly stated that Vaiśyayoni is of good birth (those whose conduct has been good will [[quiokly|quickly]] attain some good birth, the birth of a brāhmaṇa or of a kṣatriya or of a [[vaisya,|vaiśya,]] but those whose conduct has been evil will attain an evil birth, the birth of a dog or a hog or a [[cindala’.|cāṇḍāla’.]] So [[vaisya’s|vaiśya’s]] was good [[P1608]] birth in Chān. [[Upanisad.|Upaniṣad.]] Women of the higher classes could not study the Veda in medieval times but women of the three [[Nigher|higher]] classes co-operated with their husbands in all religious rites (including Vedic sacrifices). Therefore, the [[Gitā|Gītā]] first mentions the worst case viz. that of [[Cāṇdālas|Cāṇḍālas]] and affirms that even [[Cāṇdālas|Cāṇḍālas]] (who belonged to the lowest class ) would attain the highest goal if they surrendered themselves as devotees to God and then adds three more classes of people viz. women, [[vaisyas|vaiśyas]] and [[sūdras.|śūdras.]] A [[Sūdra|Śūdra]] was never among untouchables ; on the contrary, early [[Smrlis|Smṛtis]] like Manu VI. 253, Yājñavalkya I. 166, Parāśara XI. 19 provide that a brāhmaṇa could take food at the house of a śūdra provided the latter was born of his slave woman or cultivated the brāhmaṇa’s fields on the [[ront|rent]] of half share of crops or was the friend of his family or tended his cattle or was his barber. In medieval times (after the 5th or 6th century [[AD.),|A. D.),]] however, the caste system became more rigid but even up to the 12th century A.D. a brāhmaṇa could take food at a śūdra’s house in extreme calamity (vide History of [[Dharma gastra,|Dharmaśāstra,]] vol. [[111.|III.]] pp. 952-53). Thereafter this was forbidden and was included among Kalivarjya acts. The [[vaisyas|vaiśyas]] are probably equated with women and [[sūdras|śūdras]] because in their pursuit of wealth they never cared to study the Veda and Upaniṣads. Other passages of Mr. Sanjana’s work are not cited for criticism for reasons of space.

Some of the objections raised by Western thinkers and writers against the doctrine of Karma and re-incarnation will now be mentioned and briefly dealt with. One or two [[prelimin ary|preliminary]] matters have to be emphasized. Many in the West regard our life after the death of the bodies as certain, but [[bardly|hardly]] [[any. one|anyone]] of them regards our life before our present bodies as a [[pos sibility.|possibility.]] The explanation of this attitude of many Western [[thinkorg|thinkers]] lies in the fact that in modern Western thought the great support of the belief in immortality of the soul has been the Christian religion, which is supposed not to recognize the pre-existence of the soul. Besides, Christian writers from Augustine onwards firmly believed that religious truths outside the revelation in the Bible as interpreted by them were a work of the devil. [[MoTaggart|McTaggart]] in ‘Some Dogmas of religion’(London, 1930) attacks this attitude. He holds (p. 113 ) that any evidence which will prove immortality will also prove pre-existence and that there is nothing in pre-existence that is [[inoompatible|incompatible]] with any of the dogmas that are generally accepted as fundamental [[P1609]] to [[Christianity.|Christianity.’]] L. P. Jacks in [[Near|‘Near]] the brink’ (Allen and Unwin, London, 1952, pp. 10-11) holds the same opinion ( at the age of 92 ) as [[MoTaggart’s.|McTaggart’s.]] Wordsworth in his famous Ode appears to agree that the present life is preceded by another existence when he says ‘Our birth is but a sleep and a forgetting, the soul that rises with us, our life’s star, had elsewhere its setting and cometh from afar.’ L. P. Jacks in the work [[men tioned|mentioned]] above remarks (on p. 9) that an endless punishment is not, strictly speaking, [[&|a]] punishment at all but an exercise of cruelty or vengeance and that the same is true of an endless reward.

The first objection raised against the doctrine of Karma by Pringle-Pattison in ‘Idea of immortality’ (Oxford, 1922) is that absence of memory of the pre-existence is fatal to it (p. [[11%)|112)]] and that immortality without recollection is quite useless. Miss Lily Dougall in ‘Immortality’ by Canon Streeter and others [[raizes|raises]] a similar objection (p. 29). This objection can be answered in various ways. Does any one remember anything of what one did during the first two years of his life? It is also well-known that [[mon|men]] do not correctly remember in old age even the names of their grand-children nor does a man remember what he did exactly ten years ago during his present life. It is rather merciful that there is no [[momory|memory]] of past lives. If all the multifarious memories of many lives were to crowd on us in the present life, our minds would be confused and matters in the [[progent|present]] life would be very much complicated. Karma is a [[cosmio|cosmic]] law like the law of gravitation. The latter was not recognized by humanity for thousands of years but all the same it existed and worked. Several people claim to remember their past lives. The story of [[Santidevi|Śāntidevī]] was published as a case of re-incarnation by Lala Deshbhandhu Gupta, Pandit Nekiram Sharma, and Tarachand Mathur. The ‘Theosophist Monthly’ for January 1925 mentions concrete instances of the memory of past lives. [[The|‘The]] lives of [[Aloyone’|Alcyone’]] ([[Adgar,|Adyar,]] 1924) is a work in two volumes by Mrs. [[Begant|Besant]] and Mr. Leadbeater in which 48 lives are traced from 70000 B. C. to 624 A. D. with photographs of some of them as they must have appeared in the respective incarnations. The present author does not go into that question here. In the Essays on Immortality edited by B. H. Streeter it is said (on p. 297 ) that lack of conscious continuity between two incarnations of a soul negatives the doctrine. One may ask: what grounds are there to hold or to prove that the reprobate [[oriminal|criminal]] who will be awarded a judgment of eternal [[punish ment|punishment]] [[P1610]] will remember while undergoing hell fires his past [[lifo|life]] on earth? If one can believe in future eternal punishment for a confirmed sinner without any tangible evidence of memory of the sinful life he lived, why refuse to believe in pre-existence merely on the ground of absence of memory? Vide ‘Some Dogmas [[do.’|&c.’]] p. 130. Further, science holds that matter and energy are indestructible, they are only transformed into other forms. Why should one regard it as absurd if one were to hold that the soul (to which post-existence is granted by Christianity and some other religions) should be held to come into existence in the present life all at once and not be held to have had previous existence but only in a different form? The continuity of the soul in past and future existences [[ia|is]] merely a [[metapbysical|metaphysical]] question and cannot be affected by the loss of memory. The latter occurs in one life of many years as to numerous happenings and at least in that one life the soul is admittedly one throughout.

Another objection is on the ground of heredity. We often find a strong likeness between parents and children in body and sometimes in mental faculties also. How are we to explain this? One answer may be that by some law not yet discovered the soul that has to undergo rebirth is born to parents suited to his condition. Besides, children are not exactly like their parents, some resemble in several physical features the mother or the father, but not in all and children of the same parents differ among themselves even as regards physical features. As regards mental qualities, children do not very often resemble their parents. What sort of mental equipment was possessed by the parents of such men of genius as Shakespeare, Newton, [[Ramapujan,|Ramanujan,]] Einstein ? Karma does not express what a man inherits from his ancestors, but it expresses that which he inherits from himself in some previous state or states of existence.

Another objection is often raised that belief in the doctrine of Karma would make men callous to [[buman|human]] misery and suffering and unwilling to [[belp.|help.]] This also is a perverse idea. From the ancient Vedic times charity and compassion have been inculcated as the duties of all men without distinction. The Ṛgveda (X. 117.6) sage declares [[that|‘that]] man who cooks food for himself and eats it himself alone simply incurs sin’ ([[kevalagho|kevalāgho]] bhavati [[kovaladı).|kevalādī).]] The [[Bf.|Bṛ.]] Up. V. 2. 3 prescribes for all [[mon|men]] the three duties [[viz,|viz.]] self-restraint, charity and compassion. [[I|If]] [[P1611]] [[4|a]] man having the means to help another does not offer help he would be failing in his duty. It is possible that the result of the sufferer’s Karma might have been meant to be mitigated by the kindness of helpers.

Another objection is sometimes raised as follows. The population of the earth is growing rapidly. The question is: where do the additional egoes come from (vide Berthelot’s work, English tr. p. 127 and J. E. Sanjana’s book on the ‘Dogma of [[Te-incarnation’|re-incarnation’]] p. 81). Several answers are possible. Several species of lower animals have become [[extinot|extinct]] and the number of wild animals such as the lion is rapidly being reduced. It is possible for one who believes in Karma doctrine to argue that the selves that were masquerading as beasts now appear as human beings, because [[thetr|their]] Karma that reduced them to the state of beasts has been exhausted.

Some of the [[Purāñas|Purāṇas]] declare that a man who is very sinful may reach lower and lower states e. g. the [[Vayupurāṇa|Vāyupurāṇa]]115 ([[obap.|chap.]] 14 verses 34-37) provides that a very [[sipful|sinful]] man may become paśu ([[Cattle|cattle]] or a beast), then deer, then a bird, then [[&|a]] creeping insect and then a tree or other immobile object. Theosophists and some modern scholars hold that once the human state is reached there is no regression at all. It may be noted that the [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] expressly states (in V. 6-7) that after the death of the body the souls take up birth in other bodies, while some become tree-trunks according to their actions and [[knowledge,|knowledge.]] The [[Oh.|Ch.]] Up. V. [[10, 7 days|10. 7 says]] ’those whose conduct has been evil will quickly attain an evil birth, the birth of a dog or hog or [[candāla.’|cāṇḍāla.’]] Manu (XII. 9) avers that a man is reduced by bodily sins to the stage of a [[treo-trunk,|tree-trunk,]] by sins due to speech to being a bird or a [[boast|beast]] and by mental sins to lowest births (of a [[cāṇdāla|cāṇḍāla]] [[eto.).|etc.).]] In [[verges|verses]] 62-68 of chapter XII, Manu describes the different births of various animals by the [[con mission|commission]] of thefts of various articles and in verses 52-59 the births due to the grave sins of [[brahmana-murder,|brāhmaṇa-murder,]] drinking liquor, [[in oost.|incest.]] The [[Yājñava)kya|Yājñavalkya]] [[Smarti|Smṛti]] (III. 213-215) also contains [[provi sious|provisions]] similar to Manu XII. [[53-59,|53-59.]] The Yogasūtra II. 13 also suggests that the retribution for various sins results in being [[P1612]] born in low births or dying prematurely or experiencing misery and suffering.

It is rather difficult to regard all this body of authority as mere [[artbavādas|arthavādas]] (not meant to be taken seriously but intended only to frighten intending sinners). Dr. Radhakrishnan (in [[“An|‘An]] idealist view of life’ ed. of 1932) suggests that it is possible that rebirth in animal form is a figure of speech for rebirth [[an|as]] human beings with animal or beastly qualities (page 292).

All verses on which [[Prot.|Prof.]] Ranade relies are set out here in one place: को ददर्श प्रथमं जायमानमस्थन्वन्तं यदनस्था [[विभति।|बिभर्ति।]] भूम्या [[असुरसगात्मा क खित्|असुरसृगात्मा क्व स्वित्]] को विद्वांसमुप [[गात्मण्दुमेतत्|गात्प्रष्टुमेतत्]] [[॥ . I. 164.|॥ ऋ. I. 164.]] 4; [[श्रियः|स्त्रियः]] सतीस्ताँ उ [[मे घुस|मे पुंस]] आहुः [[पश्यदक्षण्वान|पश्यदक्षण्वान्न]] वि चेतदन्धः । कविर्यः पुत्रः [[सईमा|स ईमा]] चिकेत यस्ता [[विजानास पितुपितासत्|विजानात्स पितुष्पितासत्]] ॥ [[16:|16;]] अवः परेण पर एनावरेण पदा वत्सं [[विनती गौरवस्थात्|बिभ्रती गौरुदस्थात्]] । स [[कदीची के स्विदध|कद्रीची कं स्विदर्धं]] परागात् [[क खिसूते|क्व स्वित्सूते]] नहि [[यूपे अन्त: 17:|यूथे अन्तः॥ 17;]] [[अपर्णा|द्वा सुपर्णा]] सयुजा सखाया [[समानं वृक्ष परि|समानं वृक्षं परि]] षस्वजाते । तयोरन्यः पिप्पलं [[साबस्यनश्नसन्यो|स्वाद्वत्त्यनश्नन्नन्यो]] अभि चाकशीति। 20; अनच्छये [[तुरगात|तुरगातु]] जीवमेजद् ध्रुवं मध्य आ [[परस्यानाम्|पस्त्यानाम्]] । जीवो [[मुवस्य|मृतस्य]] चरति [[स्वधाभिरमत्यों मत्येना|स्वधाभिरमर्त्यो मर्त्येना]] [[सयोनिः॥ 30,|सयोनिः॥ 30;]] अपश्यं [[गोपामनिपचमानमा चपराच पथिभि श्वरन्तम्|गोपामनिपद्यमानमा च परा च पथिभिश्चरन्तम्]] । [[स सधीचीः|स सध्रीचीः]] स [[विधुचीर्वसान|विषूचीर्वसान]] आ [[घरीवति|वरीवर्ति]] [[भुवनेष्वन्तः ॥ 31,|भुवनेष्वन्तः ॥ 31;]] [[अपामाति स्वषया गृभीतोऽमयों मत्यना सोनिः|अपाङ् प्राङेति स्वधया गृभीतोऽमर्त्यो मर्त्येना सयोनिः]] । [[ता शश्वता|ता शश्वन्ता]] [[विपूचीना|विषूचीना]] वियन्ता न्यन्यं [[चिक्युने निचिक्युरल्यम्। 383;|चिक्युर्न नि चिक्युरन्यम्॥ 38;]] the word जीव occurs only in I. 164. 30. In I. 164. 38 the [[word जीब|word जीव]] does not occur. At the most there is a contrast between the immortal [[part i.e. soul)|part (i. e. soul)]] and the mortal part (the body) in the verses 30 and 38. These two ( soul and body) are always bound together; one of them (the body) is well known to [[peopte,|people,]] the other (the soul) is not so known. The verse अपश्यं [[गोपा (क.|गोपां (ऋ.]] I, 164. 31) is repeated [[in x. 177.|in X. 177.]] 3. The [[आम्बमेषिकपर्व|आश्वमेधिकपर्व]] 47. 16 [[हाविमो पक्षिणो निल्यो|द्वाविमौ पक्षिणौ नित्यौ]] is an echo [[ofer Tो|of द्वा सुपर्णा]] सयुजा etc. ↩︎

  15. [[सूर्य चक्षुर्गच्छतु|सूर्यं चक्षुर्गच्छतु]] [[पातमात्मा धां|वातमात्मा द्यां]] च गच्छ पृथिवीं च धर्मणा। अपो वा गच्छ यदि [[aga FC Arrig afa fast TR: II . X. 16.3,|तत्र ते हितमोषधीषु प्रति तिष्ठा शरीरैः॥ ऋ. X. 16. 3.]] [[gaurt|धर्मणा]] [[means o a|means सुकृतेन]] (good deeds or sacrifices ). [[In KB. X, 85.24|In Ṛg. X. 85. 24]] we have [[frien haluat otsitet at WETCAT QUIA.|ऋतस्य योनौ सुकृतस्य लोकेऽरिष्टां त्वा सह पत्या दधामि.]] Compare [[with #.|with ऋ.]] X. [[16.3,|16. 3,]] [[ṣaqu|शतपथ]] X. [[3.8,|3. 8,]] [[radiaatregtareita पाचैवानिमप्येति चक्षुषावित्र्य मनसा चन्द्र श्रोत्रेण विशः प्राणेन वायुस पतन्मय एव सूखेतासा देवताना यां यां कामयते सा भूत्वेलयतिः|स यदैवंविदस्माल्लोकात्प्रैति वाचैवाग्निमप्येति चक्षुषादित्यं मनसा चन्द्रं श्रोत्रेण दिशः प्राणेन वायुं स एतन्मय एव भूत्वैतासां देवतानां यां यां कामयते सा भूत्वेत्यति;]] and [[यत्ते यम वैवस्वतं|यत्ते यमं वैवस्वतं]] मनो जगाम दूरकम् । तत्त [[San STAHE para ofrer ®|आवर्तयामसीह क्षयाय जीवसे॥ ऋ]] X. 58. 1. The words [[anderlined|underlined]] occur in all twelve verses of the [[hyma,|hymn,]] which must be supposed to have been addressed to the man who is moribund. The first verse means ’thy mind that has reached far away to Yama, the son of Vivasvat, we bring back for the purpose that it may reside and live here.’ The remaining eleven verses refer to the dying man’s mind having gone to (i.e. as having the dying thought of heaven and earth, to the earth with four points, four intermediate quarters, [[the dea,|the sea,]] rays and high hilly tracts, waters and plants, [[to the San and|to the Sun and]] [[Dawa.|Dawn,]] the big mountains, this whole world, most distant regions, past and future, ↩︎

  20. [[यधाभवदनुदेयी तती|यथाभवदनुदेयी ततो]] अग्रमजायत । [[पुरस्ताद शुध्न|पुरस्ताद् बुध्न]] आततः [[पश्चाविरयर्ण कृतम् । #.|पश्चान्निरयणं कृतम्॥ ऋ.]] X. 135. 6. The [[word staat occurs|word अनुदेयी occurs]] only thrice [[in the Rg.|in the Ṛg.]] viz. in this hymn twice (in verses 5 and 6) and once [[lo æg.|in Ṛg.]] X. 85. 6 where it is in the feminine [[geoder,|gender,]] means a friend of the newly married bride who goes along with her to the bridegroom’s house to keep her company’ [[(arriagesi)|(रैभ्यासीदनुदेयी)]]. [[well literally|अनुदेयी literally]] means ‘what is given after the principal gift’. Even in these days [[wben|when]] a substantial gift is made to a brāhmaṇa, another small gift is added to it. The [[word Farrou occurs|word निरयणं occurs]] only here in the whole of the [[Rgveda, xu|Ṛgveda. अग्र]] [[and quia are|and बुध्न are]] contrasted as top and bottom [[la Rg.|in Ṛg.]] X. 111. 8 [[‘कविवर्ग क विद्वान भासाम् ।|‘क स्विदग्रं क स्विद् बुध्न आसाम्’.]] ↩︎

[[1974AAT HET POSHTAT … fai qua quart am|स वा अयमात्मा ब्रह्म विज्ञानमयो मनोमय … इति। यथाकारी यथाचारी तथा]] भवति साधुकारी साधुर्भवति पापकारी पापो भवति पुण्यः पुण्येन कर्मणा भवति पापः पापेन । अथो [[खल्याहुः|खल्वाहुः]] । काममय एवायं पुरुष इति । स [[यधाकामो|यथाकामो]] भवति तत्क्रतुर्भवति यत्क्रतुर्भवति तत्कर्म कुरुते यत्कर्म कुरुते तदभिसम्पद्यते। तदेष श्लोको भवति। तदेव सक्तः सह कर्मणैति [[लिई|लिङ्गं]] मनो यत्र निषक्तमस्य। प्राप्यान्तं कर्मणस्तस्य यत्किंचेह [[करोत्ययश्|करोत्ययम्]] । [[तस्माल्लोकात्पुनर त्यस्मे|तस्माल्लोकात्पुनरैत्यस्मै]] लोकाय कर्मणे। इति नु कामयमानः। अथाकामयमानो “योऽकामो निष्काम आप्तकाम आत्मकामो न तस्य [[माणा|प्राणा]] उत्क्रामन्ति ब्रह्मैव सन्ब्रह्माप्येति। तदेष श्लोको भवति। [[यदा सर्व प्रमुच्यन्ते|यदा सर्वे प्रमुच्यन्ते]] कामा येऽस्य हृदि स्थिताः । अथ [[मोऽमृतो|मर्त्योऽमृतो]] भवत्यत्र ब्रह्म समश्नुते ॥” [[वृह.|बृह.]] उप. IV. 4. 5-7; the verse यदा सर्वे occurs in [[कठोपनि;|कठोपनि०;]] the भाष्य of शङ्कराचार्य on this begins: स वा अयं य एवं संसरत्यात्मा ब्रह्मैव [[पर एव ।.|पर एव ।]] He distinguishes यथाकारी [[यथा चारी as’|यथाचारी as ‘]]करणं नाम नियता क्रिया विधिप्रतिषेधादिगम्या चरणं [[नामानियतामिति विशेषः।।.|नामानियतमिति विशेषः।’.]] [[On ऋतु he says ऋतु माध्यवसायो|On क्रतु he says ‘क्रतुर्नामाध्यवसायो]] निश्चयो यदनन्तरा क्रिया प्रवर्तते।’. आत्मकाम is explained as यस्य आत्मैव नान्यः कामयितव्यो वस्त्वन्तरीभूतः [[पदार्थों|पदार्थो]] [[भवति. with कामयमानः|भवति. With कामयमानः]] and अकामयमानः, compare [[मुण्डकोप-|मुण्डकोप०]] III. 2.3 (कामान्यः कामयते). The [[afix|affix]] मय as in काममय is employed in two [[seases|senses]] viz. प्राचुर्य and विकार [[acc.to|acc. to]] पाणिनि ‘[[तत्पकृतवचने मयत् ।|तत्प्रकृतवचने मयट्’]] (V. 4.21 and IV. 3. 134 and 143). [[The word क्रत by|The word क्रतु by]] itself and in compounds like शतक्रतु and सुक्रतु occurs hundreds of times [[in the Rgveda.|in the Ṛgveda.]] It is an [[Iodo-European|Indo-European]] word, as in Greek [[we have Kratos’|we have ‘Kratos’]] meaning power. Several meanings have been [[assigned to ms.|assigned to क्रतु.]] Indra called [[‘Satakrata’|‘Śatakratu’]] is invoked to bestow ojas, courage, an heroic son and stated to be the father and mother of the devotee [[(io Rg.|(in Ṛg.]] VIII. 98. 10-12). [[With the Br.|With the Bṛ.]] Up. passage, we may compare छा.उप. [[III. 14.1’सर्व|III. 14. 1 ‘सर्वं]] खल्विदं ब्रह्म तज्जलानिति शान्त उपासीत । अथ खलु [[ऋतुमयः|क्रतुमयः]] पुरुषो यथा [[ऋतुरस्मिल्लोके|क्रतुरस्मिँल्लोके]] पुरुषो भवति तथेतः प्रेत्य भवति । स क्रतुं [[कुर्वीत।"|कुर्वीत।’]] ↩︎

  24. Compare Epistle to Galatians by Apostle Paul 6.7 ‘[[what soever|whatsoever]] a man [[sowetb,|soweth,]] that shall he also reap’. Even [[in the old|in the Old]] Testament we find in the Book of Job, [[chap. 4. 8’|chap. 4. 8 ‘]] they that plough iniquity [[and cow|and sow]] wickedness reap the same’. From the question of the disciples of Jesus as regards a man blind from birth ‘Master! who did sin, [[this war|this man]] or his parents that he was [[boro blind’|born blind’]] (St. [[Jobo|John]] IX. 2), it appears that the idea of Karma was prevalent among the [[Jewisb|Jewish]] people in the time of Jesus. From Matthew XI. 14 and XVII. 12-13 it appears [[that Jobn|that John]] the Baptist was declared to be an incarnation of [[:prophet|prophet]] Elias. Shaw Desmond in ‘[[Re-in. carnation|Re-incarnation]] for every [[maa’|man’]] points out (on p. 63) that the doctrine of [[re-incar. Dation|re-incarnation]] was taught in the Christian Church in the early centuries and that the 2nd Council of Constantinople in 551 [[A.D,|A. D.]] made the doctrine of [[re locarration|re-incarnation]] an [[anatbema.|anathema.]] ↩︎

  29. In ‘In search of faith’, a ‘symposium’ edited by E. W. Martin (London, 1943) G. B. Shaw states (pp. 9-10) that he may be described as a creative evolutionist, that he does not [[believo ia|believe in]] personal immortality and abhors it, that he [[does sot|does not]] believe [[in tbe|in the]] Resurrection of Jesus. It is difficult for a non-Christian to state definitely the [[doctrina|doctrine]] as to [[wbat bappons|what happens]] after death, there being many varying interpretations [[accopted|accepted]] by the Christian [[Churh|Church]] at various times and in different countries. But the general traditional scheme is a [[particolar|particular]] judgment immediately after death, the general judgment at the general [[Resurrection,|Resurrection–]] the [[ultimata|ultimate]] issue of judgment being consignment to Hell or admission to Heaven [[otornally.|eternally.]] ↩︎

[[अभं भूला|अभ्रं भूत्वा]] मेघो भवति मेघो भूत्वा प्रवर्षति । त इह [[ब्रीहियवा|व्रीहियवा]] [[ओषधिवनस्पतय. स्तिलमाषा|ओषधिवनस्पतयस्तिलमाषा]] इति जायन्तेऽतो वै खलु [[दुनिष्पपतरम्|दुर्निष्प्रपतरम्]] । यो यो [[त्यहमत्ति|ह्यन्नमत्ति]] यो रेतः सिञ्चति [[तय|तद्भूय]] एव भवति । छा. उप. V. [[10.6;|10. 6;]] and 8 is [[अर्थतयोः पधयोर्न कसं च न तानीमानि शुदाण्य सकवावनि|अथैतयोः पथोर्न कतरेण च न तानीमानि क्षुद्राण्यसकृदावर्त्तीनि]] भूतानि भवन्ति जायस्व [[नियस्वेति|म्रियस्वेति]] । एतत्तृतीयं स्थानम् [[।नेनासौ|। तेनासौ]] लोको न सम्पूर्यते । Compare मुण्डक II. 1. 5. शङ्कराचार्य has a very long and [[intresting|interesting]] note on this passage. He explains [[दुनिष्प्रपतरम्|दुर्निष्प्रपतरम्]] in two ways [[via.|viz.]] [[‘दुनिष्क्रमणं दनासरणम् ।। ‘|‘दुर्निष्क्रमणं दुर्निःसरणम् ।’]] and again says the word is [[निज्मपततरम्|निष्प्रपततरम्]] (one [[a|त]] being lost) ‘तकार एको [[लसो|लुप्तो]] द्रष्टव्यः । [[ब्रीहियवादिभावो दुनिष्प्रपतः।|व्रीहियवादिभावो दुर्निष्प्रपतः ।]] [[तस्मादपिदुनिष्प्रपतादेतःसिग्देहसम्बन्धो दुनिष्प सतर हत्यर्थः।|तस्मादपि दुर्निष्प्रपताद्रेतःसिग्देहसम्बन्धो दुर्निष्प्रपततर इत्यर्थः ।]] [[यस्मादृरतोभिबोलेः पुंस्त्वरहितः स्थावरेवा|यस्मादूर्ध्वरेतोभिर्बालैः पुंस्त्वरहितैः स्थविरैर्वा]] भक्षिता अन्तराले शीर्यन्ते [[अनेक वादनादानाम्|अनेकत्वादन्नादानाम्]] । [[कदाचिरकाकतालीयवृत्त्या रेतसिभिर्भक्ष्यन्ते|कदाचित्काकतालीयवृत्त्या रेतःसिग्भिर्भक्ष्यन्ते]] ।’. ↩︎

III. 1. 5;]] भाष्य ‘यदि नाम [[पर्जन्याविष्युत्तरेषु चतुर्वनिख्वपो हौम्यग्यता|पर्जन्यादिषूत्तरेषु चतुर्ष्वग्निष्वपां हौम्यद्रव्यता]] परिकल्प्येत परिकल्प्यतां नाम । तेषु [[होतग्यतयोपासानां सोमादीनामबहुलवोपपत्तेः|होतव्यतयोपात्तानां सोमादीनामब्बहुलत्वोपपत्तेः]] । प्रथमे [[स्वनो भुतां भद्धा परित्यज्यामुता|त्वग्नौ श्रुतां श्रद्धां परित्यज्याश्रुता]] आपः [[परिकल्प्पन्त|परिकल्प्यन्त]] इति साहसमेतत् । … [[नेष|नैष]] दोषः । … [[अवाकार्य सोमयादि स्थूलीभवद महलं|श्रद्धाकार्यं सोमवृष्ट्यादि स्थूलीभवद्बहुलम्]] लक्ष्यते । सा च श्रद्धाया [[अपत्ये|अप्त्वे]] युक्तिः । कारणानुरूपं हि [[कार्य|कार्यं]] भवति । [[श्रद्धाशया सुपपद्यते वैदिकमयोगदर्शनात्-भद्धा या आपः-इति। तत्वं|श्रद्धाशब्दश्चाप्युपपद्यते वैदिकप्रयोगदर्शनात् - श्रद्धा वा आपः - इति । तनुत्वं]] श्रद्धासारूप्यं [[मारप आपो देहीजभूता इत्यतः अद्धाशब्दाः|गच्छन्त्य आपो देहबीजभूता इत्यतः श्रद्धाशब्दाः]] स्युः । It may be noted that [[v.|V.]] S. III. [[I.|1.]] 1-7 are explanatory of Chān. [[Up.v.3.9|Up. V. 3. 9]] and [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. VI. 2. 9-13 and the whole of V. S. III. 1 [[explaids|explains]] on the basis of [[Paācāgaividyā|Pañcāgnividyā]] how the self passes through various stages of [[samsāra.|saṁsāra.]] ↩︎

  58. It is possible that the words ‘dhātā yathāpūrvamakalpayat’ (in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 190. 3) simply mean that the Creator arranged the [[Suo|Sun]], the Moon, Heaven, Earth and regions according to the priorities (or their proper [[ordor).|order).]] But it cannot be gainsaid that these words are at least the germs of the later doctrine of Kalpa, [[Maavantara|Manvantara]] & [[C.|c.]] [[Sarkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] on V. S. I. [[3.30|3. 30]] [[explaios|explains]] the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] verse as ’the creator created in the present Kalpa the universe [[inclading|including]] the Sun and the Moon in the same way as in the preceding Kalpa.’ यथा पूर्वस्मिन्कल्पे [[सूर्याचन्द्रमाप्रभृति जगत् कृत तथास्मिनपि|सूर्याचन्द्रमःप्रभृति जगत् कॢप्तं तथास्मिन्नपि]] कल्पे [[परमेश्वरोऽकल्पयदित्य.|परमेश्वरोऽकल्पयदित्यर्थः ।]] He quotes as supporting his views several [[smrti|smṛti]] verses which occur in the [[Mabābhārata|Mahābhārata]] and in some of the Purāṇas. The present writer feels that it is difficult to [[bold|hold]] that a mere illustration or analogy (of seed and tree) [[cab|can]] amount to a logical argument. At the most an illustration may lend some [[plansibility|plausibility]] to a theory, belief or [[conclusion,|conclusion.]] ↩︎

  63. नूनं [[हिमालया|हि बालया]] धातुर्मया [[वैविप्रियं|वै विप्रियं]] कृतम् । यस्य [[प्रसादादनात|प्रसादाद्दुर्नीतिं]] प्राप्तास्मि [[भरतर्षभ। facica|भरतर्षभ ॥ विराटपर्व]] 20. 14.   84. तदधिगम [[उत्तरपूर्वाधयोरलेषविनाशौ तस्यपदेशात्|उत्तरपूर्वाघयोरश्लेषविनाशौ तद्व्यपदेशात्]] । इतरस्याप्येवमसंश्लेषः पाते तु। [[अनारब्धकायें|अनारब्धकार्ये]] एव तु [[पूर्व तववधेः|पूर्वे तदवधेः]] । वेदान्तसूत्र IV. 1. [[13-15,शाङ्करभाष्य|13-15; शाङ्करभाष्य]] ‘ब्रह्माधिगमे सत्युत्तरपूर्वयोरघयोरश्लेषविनाशौ भवतः उत्तरस्याश्लेषः, पूर्वस्य विनाशः इतरस्यापि पुण्यस्य कर्मणः एवमघवदसंश्लेषो विनाशश्च ज्ञानवतो भवतः। अवश्यंभाविनी विदुषः शरीरपाते [[मुक्ति रित्यवधार्यते।.|मुक्तिरित्यवधार्यते ।’.]] On [[वृह.|बृह.]] उप. [[1.|I.]] 4. 10 the [[शाकमान्य|शाङ्करभाष्य]] states [[’ यापरीरयातस्तापत्फलो पभोगाङ्गतया विपरीतमत्ययं रागाविदोषं|यावच्छरीरपातस्तावत्फलोपभोगाङ्गतया विपरीतप्रत्ययं रागादिदोषं]] च [[तावन्माचमाक्षिपत्येव|तावन्मात्रमाक्षिपत्येव]] । [[मुक्तेपुवत्प्रवृत्तफलवात्तवेत कस्य|मुक्तेषुवत्प्रवृत्तफलत्वात्तद्धेतुकस्य]] कर्मणः। तेन न तस्य निवर्तिकी विद्या । अविरोधात् । … ज्ञानोत्पत्तेः [[प्रागर्व सल्काल|प्रागूर्ध्वं तत्काल]]जन्मान्तरसञ्चितानां च कर्मणामप्रवृत्तफलानां विनाशः सिद्धो भवति ।.’ The विज्ञानदीपिका of [[पप्रपाद|पद्मपाद]] says [[‘उभयोनितो|उभयोर्ज्ञानतो]] नाशो [[भोगात्मारब्धकर्मणः।।|भोगात्प्रारब्धकर्मणः ।’]] [[(verse9).|(verse 9).]] The com. says: ज्ञान is of two kinds, परोक्ष and अपरोक्ष. The first is in the form [[’ there|‘there]] is [[brabman|brahman]] and I must engage in [[upasanā|upāsanā]] thereof.’ The other is of this sort: [[‘मा|ब्रह्म]] सत्यं जगन्मिथ्या। अतोहमपि [[बहोवेश्याकारकं|ब्रह्मैवेत्याकारकं]] यज्ज्ञानं तदपरोक्षम् । [[अपरोक्षज्ञान|अपरोक्षज्ञानं]] [[सावत्मारब्धेतरकर्मनाशकम्|तावत्प्रारब्धेतरकर्मनाशकम्]] । एवं चात्र ज्ञानमपरोक्षमेव । तस्मादुभयोः सञ्चितसञ्चीयमानयोः [[कर्मणो शो बीजलोपः।|कर्मणोर्नाशो बीजलोपः ।’]] ↩︎

  107. [[द्वे से चक्रे सूर्य ब्रह्माण तथा विदुः । अथैक चक्रं यनुहा तदबातच इशितः॥ #.X.|द्वे ते चक्रे सूर्ये ब्रह्माण ऋतुथा विदुः । अथैकं चक्रं यद्गुहा तदद्धातय इद्विदुः ॥ ऋ. X.]] 85.16. This literally means [[o|‘O]] Sūryā (daughter of the [[Suo)|Sun)]] ! two are thy wheels [[wbich|which]] the brāhmaṇas ( or priests ) know as coming at proper seasons; one of [[tbe|the]] wheels is inside a cave and is [[koown|known]] only to [[addhālis|addhātis]] ( wise men ?).’ Acc. to [[Sayana|Sāyaṇa]] here Sūryā addresses herself. The two wheels are the Sun and the Moon and the wheel in the 2nd [[ball|half]] is a third one viz. the year. The [[Nighanto|Nighaṇṭu]] [[includes’ addhātayah’|includes ‘addhātayaḥ’]] among the twenty-four words meaning [[‘medbāvin’|‘medhāvin’]] (III. 15). That word occurs only here [[ia|in]] the whole of the [[Rgveda,|Ṛgveda.]] The work [[explaios|explains]] [[‘Surya’|‘Sūryā’]] as ‘ātmaśakti’ and takes [[*cakra’|‘cakra’]] as meaning [[.body’;|‘body’;]] two bodies would be ’the present and the future body’ and it holds that [[addbātayah|addhātayaḥ]] means ‘yogins’ and [[‘gubā’|‘guhā’]] (in the cave) suggests that one body cannot be seen but is only known to yogins. [[Tbar|That]] one body is the [[ativāhika|ātivāhika]] body according to this writer. ↩︎

[[उहालको हावाणिः श्वेतकेतु पुत्रमुवाच । स्वमान्तं मे सोम्य विजानहिीति। यत्रतत्पुरुषः स्वपिति माम|उद्दालको हारुणिः श्वेतकेतुं पुत्रमुवाच । स्वप्नान्तं मे सोम्य विजानीहीति । यत्रैतत्पुरुषः स्वपिति नाम]] सता सोम्य तदा संपन्नो भवति । तस्मादेनं स्वपितीत्याचक्षते । [[व छपीतो|स्वं ह्यपीतो]] भवति । [[म. उप.|छा. उप.]] VI. 8.1. In स्वपिति, स्व means ‘his own i.e. [[Sell’, पित्ति|Self’, पिति]] represents अपीत (absorbed). अपीति or अप्यय means लय. The [[.सु.|वे. सू.]] I. 1.9 ([[स्वाप्ययाद|स्वाप्ययात्]]) and IV.4.16 are based on this Chāndogya [[text:|text;]] compare [[ह.|बृह.]] उप. IV. 3.19 [[पत्र ‘सतो|यत्र ‘सुप्तो]] न कंचन कामं कामयते न कंचन [[स्वमं पश्यति.’|स्वप्नं पश्यति.’]] ↩︎

  112. एवं [[मध्यमदतीवाभिधाग्यपि जन्मान्तरीयकर्माण्येष तारतम्यात्तथा तथोमानि।|मध्यममन्दतीव्राभिधान्यपि जन्मान्तरीयकर्माण्येव तारतम्यात्तथा तथोक्तानि ।]] तत्र [[त्रिपादसाचिकमेकपावराजसं|त्रिपादसात्त्विकमेकपादराजसं]] पुण्यं मध्यम् । एवं [[द्विपाद-सात्त्विक दिपाइराजसमपि fettatrice diarira I|द्विपाद-सात्त्विकं द्विपादराजसमपि किंचित्पापोपसृष्टं तीव्रमिति ।]] p. 18 transcript. What is half Sāttvika and half rājasa and slightly affected by [[visciousness|viciousness]] should be called manda and [[tiora|tīvra]] should be described as one part sāttvika and three parts [[rājana|rājasa]] and predominantly sinful. ↩︎

When]] he reaches the stage of describing the step of [[Moka|Mokṣa]] due to [[roalisation|realisation]] he passes on to the [[irst|first]] person from the third and [[state|states]] about [[bimself|himself]] ‘I shall become [[saukta|mukta]] and perfect at that stage.’ ↩︎

[[HOTT HAFT Qrat teratai Aaya He ah a a पाहरजातानि पापानि पूर्व कर्माणि देहिनः । संसार तामस ताटक पदविध प्रतिपद्यते ॥ भाज्य पानुभावं च पशुभाषामगो भवेत् । सूगत्वात्पक्षिभावं तु तस्मानचैव सरीसपः । सरीसपत्वालण्छति For Hirst 9.|कर्मणा मनसा वाचा यदभीक्ष्णं निषेव्यते । तत्प्रसह्य हरेत्पापं तस्मात्सुकृतमाचरेत् ॥ यादृग्जातानि पापानि पूर्वं कर्माणि देहिनः । संसारं तामसं तादृक् षड्विधं प्रतिपद्यते ॥ मानुष्य पशुभावं च पशुभावान्मृगो भवेत् । मृगत्वात्पक्षिभावं तु तस्माच्चैव सरीसृपः ॥ सरीसृपत्वाद्गच्छेद्धि स्थावरत्वं न संशयः । वायुपु.]] [[14,|14.]] 34–37. ↩︎
